Subject-wise percentiles (Maths, Physics, Chemistry) are also published and are used only for tie-breaking. Raw scores can be negative (bulletin worked example shows lowest raw scores of -39, -38, -49, -44 and a candidate at 1.1034216 percentile with raw score -15). Merit uses the BEST of the two sessions' Total NTA Scores (not per-subject best). If cut-off percentiles differ across shifts, the LOWEST is applied to all shifts. => IMPLICATION: Our leaderboard/analytics must compute percentile within the cohort that took the SAME mock (our analogue of a shift), on the total, to 7 dp, and must gracefully display negative raw scores. Never average subject percentiles. For a multi-'shift' mock released in waves, replicate the merge step. Redis sorted sets are a natural fit but need 7-dp precision — store score as an integer of (percentile x 10^7) to avoid float ties.
- (high) Three different tie-break chains — JEE Main, JEE Advanced, NEET — all must be implemented separately: JEE Main 2026 (IB section 2.4, Paper 1): 1) NTA score in Mathematics, 2) NTA score in Physics, 3) NTA score in Chemistry, 4) candidate with LESS proportion of attempted-incorrect to correct answers across all subjects, 5) same ratio in Mathematics, 6) in Physics, 7) in Chemistry, 8) otherwise SAME RANK is assigned. NEET UG 2026 (IB Chapter 8 s.2, per UGMEB/NMC Public Notice 13.06.2023 + NMC email 28.01.2026): a) higher marks/percentile in Biology (Botany & Zoology), b) Chemistry, c) Physics, d) less proportion of incorrect:correct across all subjects, e) same in Biology, f) Chemistry, g) Physics, h) if still tied, resolved by a RANDOM process guided by an independent expert committee. Age is NO LONGER a NEET tie-breaker (removed 2023). JEE Advanced 2026 (jeeadv.ac.in FAQ Q57): 1) higher POSITIVE marks (marks from correct answers before negatives are subtracted), 2) higher Mathematics score, 3) higher Physics score, 4) otherwise same rank. => IMPLICATION: Tie-break is a per-exam strategy object, not shared code. Note JEE Main leads with Maths, NEET leads with Biology, and JEE Advanced leads with total positive marks (which requires us to persist positive-marks-earned separately from net score on every attempt row — a column most implementations forget and cannot backfill).
- (high) Post-submit flow: response sheet, provisional key, paid challenge window, final key, result: JEE Main 2026 (IB s.5.6): NTA displays recorded responses + attempted question paper on jeemain.nta.nic.in BEFORE the result, typically for 2-3 days; provisional answer key displayed alongside recorded responses, also 2-3 days; challenges cost a NON-REFUNDABLE Rs 200 per question, paid online, only via the official key-challenge link, and must carry justification/evidence; subject experts review; a FINAL answer key is declared and the result is compiled on it; no grievance entertained after result. NEET UG 2026 (IB Ch.15): scanned OMR answer sheets + recorded responses displayed for challenge at Rs 200 per question (OMR grading challenge) and Rs 200 per answer (answer key challenge), both non-refundable; if a challenge is upheld the key is revised for ALL candidates. JEE Advanced 2026 actual timeline: response sheet 21 May 2026, provisional answer key 25 May 2026 10:00, challenge window closed 26 May 2026 17:00, final answer key + result 01 June 2026 (challenge fee reported as Rs 500/question by some outlets and Rs 200 by others — unverified). => IMPLICATION: Ship a post-submit 'Response Sheet' screen mirroring NTA: question-by-question recorded response vs key, marks awarded, and a time-boxed 'Challenge this answer' flow routed to ADMIN. Because a sustained challenge revises the key for everyone, the rescore job must be idempotent, versioned, and must re-emit leaderboard/rewards deltas.
- (medium) 2026 calendar and scale reality: NEET 2026 was cancelled and re-run; NEET goes CBT in 2027: JEE Main 2026 Session 1: 21-29 January 2026, 13,55,293 registered, 13,04,653 appeared, results 16 Feb 2026, 12 candidates at 100 percentile (Paper 1). Session 2: 02-09 April 2026, results 20 April 2026, 26 candidates at 100 NTA score. JEE Advanced eligibility cut at top 2,50,000 across categories; General cutoff reported as 93.4123549 percentile (2026). JEE Advanced 2026 (IIT Roorkee) held 17 May 2026; 1,79,694 appeared in both papers; 56,880 qualified; qualifying CRL 92/360 (25.56%) aggregate with a per-subject minimum, OBC-NCL/EWS 82 (22.78%), SC/ST/PwD 46 (12.78%). NEET UG 2026 held 3 May 2026 was CANCELLED amid an alleged leak (Rajasthan SOG reportedly recovered a guess paper matching 140 questions); re-exam conducted 21 June 2026, key-challenge window closed 28 June 2026 23:50. Union Education Minister announced on 15 May 2026 that NEET-UG moves to CBT mode from 2027, affirmed by NTA to the Supreme Court; the 2027 Information Bulletin is still awaited. => IMPLICATION: Two product consequences: (1) our NEET mock UI should be built CBT-first now, because from 2027 the NTA console applies to NEET too — the same console component then serves all three exams and NEET students become the largest CBT-fidelity audience; (2) build an admin-facing 'exam calendar / pattern change' content model, because cancellations and re-exams are recurring events that invalidate scheduled test series mid-flight.
- (medium) Competitor partial-marking practice is inconsistent and frequently wrong: Widely republished secondary sources (infinitylearn, collegedunia, matrixedu, pw.live, aakash.ac.in as of July 2026) still state the JEE Advanced multi-correct penalty as -2, which is the 2025 rule and is WRONG for 2026 (-1). Several also publish the formula 'Partial marks = (4 x number of correct options selected) / (total number of correct options)', which contradicts the official ladder (it yields 2.67 for 2-of-3 where NTA awards +2, and 3.0 for 3-of-4 where NTA awards +3 only if all four options are correct). Allen and PhysicsWallah market 'NTA pattern' / 'replica' test series with performance analytics but publish no scoring spec; PW ran a free 'NEET Replica Test 2026'. Official practice consoles that DO reproduce the real interface: JEE Advanced 2026 practice tests on the TCS iON/digialm platform at https://cdn3.digialm.com//OnlineAssessment/index.html?32044@@M217 (Paper 1) and ...?32044@@M219 (Paper 2), linked from jeeadv.ac.in Resources; NTA hosts mock tests at nta.ac.in (Quiz/Abhyas) and physical Test Practice Centres. => IMPLICATION: Scoring correctness on multi-correct is a credible, demonstrable differentiator. Publish our marking rules in-app per test (as NTA does on-screen) and show a per-question marks-awarded breakdown in the solution view so students can verify. Use the two official digialm practice consoles as the visual/behavioural reference for our test player.
- (medium) All-India Rank predictor: the input set and the honest accuracy ceiling: To predict a JEE Main AIR you need, at minimum: (1) raw marks out of 300 (or per-subject raw marks), (2) the exact session/date/shift the student attempted (shift difficulty differs and normalisation is per shift), (3) the candidate's category (GEN/OBC-NCL/EWS/SC/ST/PwD) and gender and home state for category rank and state-quota outcomes, (4) total appeared count for that year (13,04,653 appeared in Session 1 2026; ~15.5 lakh commonly used as the denominator across both sessions), (5) historical marks-vs-percentile and percentile-vs-AIR anchor tables from prior years, (6) our own cohort's marks distribution for the same mock as a proxy shift. The naive conversion published everywhere is Rank ~= ((100 - percentile) / 100) x TotalCandidates + 1, e.g. 95 percentile x 12,00,000 -> ~60,000. It is only valid if our cohort distribution matches the national one, which it will not (self-selected, coaching-skewed). For a college predictor you additionally need JoSAA/CSAB opening-closing ranks by institute-branch-category-quota-gender-round (6 rounds + CSAB special rounds) and for NEET the MCC 15% AIQ plus state-quota counselling data. NEET rank prediction is simpler: no normalisation (single shift, OMR), so marks-to-rank is a near-deterministic mapping from the published rank list, and the main input is just marks + category + the year's appeared count. => IMPLICATION: Ship an anchored predictor, not a naive percentile x N: calibrate our cohort's marks distribution against published national marks-vs-percentile anchor points (e.g. the year's 99.5/99/95 percentile marks) via monotone interpolation, and always surface a confidence interval plus 'based on N students in this mock'. Collect category/gender/home-state/target-exam at onboarding — retrofitting these later on 10k+ users is painful and they are also valuable for RBAC-scoped analytics.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- Model marking as data, never code. Schema: exam_pattern(id, exam, year, paper) -> pattern_section(id, pattern_id, ordinal, name, max_marks, question_count, question_type) -> marking_rule(section_id, jsonb). Rule JSON carries {full, zero_on_unanswered, negative, partial_ladder[], numerical_precision, negative_on_numerical}. Seed four rows for JEE Advanced 2026 P1, four for P2, one per JEE Main subject-section, one for NEET. A 2027 pattern change becomes an INSERT, not a release. Store the pattern_id on every mock test so PYQ replays score by their own year's rules.
- Implement scoreMultiCorrect() exactly per the official ladder — |C|=4 and |S|=3 -> +3; |C|>=3 and |S|=2 -> +2; |C|>=2 and |S|=1 -> +1; S==C -> +4; any element of S outside C -> negative; S empty -> 0 — and lock it with a golden test suite generated from the verbatim worked example in the JEE Advanced 2026 paper (correct = {A,B,D}: AB=+2, AD=+2, BD=+2, A=+1, B=+1, D=+1, ABD=+4, {}=0, anything else=-1). Explicitly reject the proportional formula. Run the same function server-side in a Postgres function or Edge Function so the client can never be the source of truth for marks.
- Build one NTA-faithful test player component reused across JEE Main / JEE Advanced / NEET-2027-CBT, implementing exactly: server-anchored countdown (top-right, minutes) with auto-submit at zero and no manual end required; five-state question palette on the right with collapse/expand; Save & Next; Mark for Review & Next; Clear Response; palette-number navigation that explicitly does NOT persist the current in-progress answer; section tabs on the top bar with free switching and auto-advance to the next section's first question after Save & Next on a section's last question; per-section summary above the palette; a 'Question Paper' full-view; a pre-test Instructions screen; and a final submit confirmation summarising answered / not answered / marked counts per section.
- Ship NO calculator. Instead ship (a) an on-screen virtual numeric keypad for numerical-value entry that is mouse/tap-only, matching NTA, and (b) an optional digital rough-work scratchpad. Enforce input rules per exam: JEE Main NVQ = integer, evaluated after round-to-nearest-integer; JEE Advanced numerical = truncate/round to TWO decimal places with exact match at 2 dp. Keep font-size control, a dark/light toggle and a magnifier, mirroring NTA's documented accessibility set — these double as genuine mobile accessibility wins.
- Persist an attempt state tuple per question — (visited, answer, marked_for_review, time_spent_ms, answer_change_count, first_seen_at, last_saved_at) — and derive the five palette states rather than storing an enum. Also persist positive_marks_earned separately from net_marks on the attempt row: JEE Advanced tie-breaking needs positive marks first, and it cannot be backfilled once responses are archived. Store the raw response set for multi-correct (array of option ids), not a boolean correct flag, so a key revision can rescore losslessly.
- Implement three separate tie-break strategy objects keyed by exam: JEE Main (Maths -> Physics -> Chemistry -> incorrect:correct ratio overall -> Maths -> Physics -> Chemistry -> equal rank), JEE Advanced (positive marks -> Maths -> Physics -> equal rank), NEET (Biology -> Chemistry -> Physics -> incorrect:correct ratio overall -> Biology -> Chemistry -> Physics -> deterministic pseudo-random seeded by attempt id). Explicitly do NOT use age for NEET — that criterion was removed in 2023.
- Compute mock-test percentile the NTA way: percentile of TOTAL raw score within the cohort that took the same mock, to 7 decimal places, never an average of subject percentiles; publish subject-wise percentiles alongside for tie-breaks only; support and correctly render NEGATIVE raw totals. For Redis leaderboards store score as an integer (percentile x 10^7) to avoid float collisions at 10k+ concurrent students.
- Build an idempotent, versioned answer-key-revision pipeline before launch, not after. It must support per-question flags MULTI_KEY (+4 to anyone who marked any correct option), ALL_CORRECT (+4 to all who attempted) and DROPPED (+4 to all who appeared — for JEE Main scoped to the shift), rescore all affected attempts transactionally, write an audit row, and re-emit leaderboard and rewards-coin deltas. Pair it with a student-facing post-submit Response Sheet (recorded response vs key, marks awarded per question) and a time-boxed ADMIN-routed 'challenge this answer' flow mirroring NTA's Rs 200/question window.
- Author the JEE Main blueprint as exactly 3 subjects x (Section A 20 MCQ + Section B 5 NVQ), all 25 compulsory, 300 marks, 180 min; JEE Advanced 2026 P1 as 3 x (4 single-correct / 4 multi-correct / 4 numerical-2dp / 4 matching-list) = 48 Q / 180 marks and P2 as 3 x (4 / 5 / 5 / 2 stems x 2) = 54 Q / 180 marks, 180 min each; NEET as Physics 45 + Chemistry 45 + Botany 45 + Zoology 45 = 180 Q / 720 marks / 180 min with no sections. Validate blueprints at save time (sum of section max_marks must equal declared total) so ADMIN cannot ship a malformed paper.
- Add first-class content types for Matching List (List-I with 4 entries P/Q/R/S, List-II with 5 entries 1-5, plus 4 permutation options) and for Question Stem / shared paragraph with N independently-scored child questions. Both are required for JEE Advanced fidelity and neither is expressible as a generic MCQ. Given heavy LaTeX, render both lists and stems through the same math pipeline and cache rendered output.
- Collect exam target (JEE Main / JEE Advanced / NEET), category (GEN/OBC-NCL/EWS/SC/ST/PwD), gender, home state and target year at onboarding. These are the required inputs for any credible rank/college predictor and for category-scoped percentile analytics, and retrofitting them across 10,000+ existing users is materially harder than asking once.
- Build the NEET experience CBT-first now. NEET-UG is announced to move to computer-based testing from 2027, which means our single NTA console component will serve the largest cohort in the market from next cycle, while 2026-pattern OMR practice remains a thin presentational variant of the same engine.
PITFALLS:
- Hardcoding JEE Advanced marking constants. The multi-correct penalty was -2 in 2025 and -1 in 2026; Paper 1 Section 2 had 3 questions in 2025 and 4 in 2026; Paper 1 carried no paragraph section in 2026 while Paper 2 used question stems. jeeadv.ac.in commits only to being 'consistent with previous examinations' and discloses exact counts and marking on-screen on exam day. Any constant in code will be wrong within twelve months.
- Using the proportional partial-marking formula (4 x selected_correct / total_correct). It is the most-republished formula on the web and it is wrong: with 3 correct options and 2 selected, NTA awards +2, not 2.67; and +3 is only ever awarded when ALL FOUR options are correct and exactly three are chosen. Selecting even one wrong option voids all partial credit and applies the full negative.
- Implementing the retired NEET Section A / Section B (35 + 15-choose-10, 200 minutes) pattern. That structure was scrapped after 2024; NEET 2025 and 2026 are 180 compulsory questions in 180 minutes with no optional choice. Equally, implementing JEE Main Section B as 10 questions with any-5 optional — the choice was removed in 2025 and Section B now carries -1 negative marking, which it never did in the optional era.
- Shipping an in-test calculator. NTA Public Notice dated 02 November 2025 states the on-screen calculator described in Appendix-VIII of the JEE Main 2026 Information Bulletin 'forms part of the generic test conducting platform and does NOT apply to JEE (Main)' and that 'the use of calculators in any form is not permitted'. The stale bulletin PDF still on the CDN contains the erroneous text — trusting the bulletin alone reproduces NTA's own bug.
- Making palette-click save the current answer. In the real console, clicking a question number in the palette navigates WITHOUT saving; only Save & Next and Mark for Review & Next persist. Students lose answers to this in the real exam and expect the same behaviour in a faithful mock. Getting it 'helpfully' wrong is the single most common fidelity failure.
- Dropping 'answered and marked for review' responses at scoring time. NTA explicitly evaluates them; only marked-for-review-WITHOUT-an-answer is excluded. Collapsing the five palette states into a single enum is what causes this bug.
- Averaging subject percentiles to produce a total percentile. The JEE Main bulletin states outright that the Percentile of the Total 'shall NOT be an aggregate or average of the Percentile of the individual subject'. Percentile must be computed on the total raw score within the shift/cohort, to 7 decimal places, and raw totals can legitimately be negative.
- Client-authoritative timing or scoring. NTA's clock is server-set and the exam auto-ends at zero with no user action; time lost to a malfunction is compensated at the server. On mobile with backgrounding, network loss and clock skew, any client-side timer will be gamed and any client-side scoring will be tampered with. Deadlines and marks must live in Postgres/Edge Functions with RLS, with an explicit admin-visible mechanism to extend a single attempt.
- Treating tie-breaking as shared logic. Three exams, three different chains, with different lead subjects (Maths / positive-marks / Biology) and different terminal behaviour (JEE Main and JEE Advanced assign the SAME rank; NEET resolves by a random process under an expert committee). Also do not use age as a NEET tie-breaker — that was removed by the UGMEB/NMC public notice of 13 June 2023.
- Assuming the answer key is immutable. Both NTA exams run paid public challenge windows (Rs 200 per question) after which keys are revised for all candidates, and questions can be declared multi-key, all-correct or dropped — each with a different award rule and a different eligible population (attempted vs appeared, and for JEE Main scoped to a single shift). Without an idempotent rescore pipeline and audit trail, a key revision corrupts leaderboards and already-issued reward coins.
- Predicting All India Rank with the naive Rank = (100 - percentile)/100 x TotalCandidates formula on our own cohort. Our user base is self-selected and coaching-skewed, so its raw distribution is not the national distribution; without anchoring to published national marks-vs-percentile points the predictions will be confidently wrong and will destroy trust.
- Assuming the 2026 exam calendar is stable. NEET UG 2026 held on 3 May 2026 was cancelled amid an alleged paper leak and re-run on 21 June 2026, and NEET moves to CBT from 2027. Test-series scheduling, streaks and contests keyed to fixed official dates need an admin-editable exam-calendar entity that can absorb cancellations and re-exams mid-cycle.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- Exact hex values of the five NTA question-palette states are not published in any official document — the legend appears only as a raster image in the bulletin. We should sample them from the official digialm practice consoles (jeeadv Paper 1 M217 / Paper 2 M219) or from NTA's mock test at nta.ac.in, then choose an accessible equivalent that keeps the same semantics and legend wording. Colour alone must not encode state (WCAG).
- The exact wording and layout of NTA's final submit confirmation dialog (per-section answered / not answered / marked-for-review counts, and whether a second confirmation is required) is not documented in the bulletin text and needs to be captured from a live official mock run.
- JEE Advanced 2026 answer-key challenge fee is reported inconsistently in secondary media (Rs 500 vs Rs 200 per question). The authoritative figure is in the JEE (Advanced) 2026 Information Brochure, which we did not parse.
- JEE Advanced 2026 per-subject minimum qualifying marks: the aggregate figures (CRL 92/360 = 25.56%, OBC-NCL/EWS 82 = 22.78%, SC/ST/PwD 46 = 12.78%) and appeared/qualified counts (1,79,694 / 56,880) come from secondary sources; one source quotes the per-subject minimum as 8 marks but calls it 7.30% when 8/120 is 6.67%. Needs confirmation from the official jeeadv result notification.
- The NEET UG 2026 re-exam on 21 June 2026 was reported as running 14:00-17:15 (195 minutes) rather than the bulletin's 14:00-17:00 (180 minutes). Unverified — needs the NTA re-exam public notice to confirm whether extra time was granted and why.
- The NEET UG 2027 CBT Information Bulletin is not yet published. Unknown: whether it will be single-shift or multi-shift (multi-shift would introduce NEET normalisation for the first time, changing our percentile engine), whether sections are introduced, and whether the console will match the JEE Main console exactly.
- Whether NTA's CBT console enforces any section-entry locking or per-section time limits for JEE Main/Advanced. The bulletin says candidates 'can shuffle between sections and questions anytime', which implies none — but this should be confirmed against a live mock before we build section navigation.
- How competitors actually implement multi-correct scoring internally (Allen, PhysicsWallah, Aakash, Marks, Embibe) is not publicly documented; only their marketing pages and their published exam-pattern articles are visible, and several of those articles state the stale -2 rule. A hands-on comparison of a paid test series would be needed to confirm whether the product-level scoring is also wrong.
- Whether NTA publishes per-shift appeared counts and marks distributions in a machine-readable form. If not, our rank predictor must depend on scraped or manually curated marks-vs-percentile anchor tables, which is a maintenance and reliability liability worth costing before committing to the feature.

## open-source
SUMMARY: Licence risk is the dominant constraint: nearly every mature open-source assessment platform is copyleft — Moodle GPLv3+, Canvas LMS AGPLv3, Open edX AGPL, TAO CE AGPLv3, TCExam AGPL-3.0-or-later, Frappe LMS AGPL-3.0, ILIAS GPL, Rogō/ExamSys GPLv3, DOMjudge GPLv2+, Anki AGPL-3.0. For a closed-source Supabase-backed SaaS, AGPL is the hard blocker (network use triggers source disclosure), so these should be treated as *study* references (schema, state machines, behaviours) rather than *adopt*, and no code copied. The permissively-licensed pieces that are genuinely adoptable are the algorithm libraries and players: ts-fsrs (MIT, FSRS-6, Node>=20), fsrs-rs (BSD-3, has the parameter optimiser), py-fsrs (MIT, optimiser via extra), catsim (BSD-3, CAT engine), py-irt (MIT, Pyro/PyTorch 1PL/2PL/4PL), girth (MIT, 1PL/2PL/3PL + GRM/PCM), amp-up-io qti3-item-player (MIT), h5p-standalone (MIT), PyLTI1p3 (MIT), Elo-MMR (MIT, includes a Codeforces-rating implementation), openskill.py (MIT), CTFd (Apache-2.0), OpenOLAT (Apache-2.0), Moodle App (Apache-2.0 — the best legally-safe reference for offline mobile quiz attempt sync), Safe Exam Browser (MPL-2.0, file-level copyleft, linkable). On interchange formats, QTI 3.0 is the only forward-looking standard (QTI 2.1 legacy, 1.x deprecated) but is heavy; Moodle XML is the pragmatic import/export target for Indian coaching-content pipelines because it round-trips images, feedback and LaTeX, with GIFT/Aiken as cheap bulk-authoring text formats. xAPI 1.0.3 ratified as IEEE 9274.1.1-2023 is the analytics event model to emit; Caliper is a parallel 1EdTech framework, SCORM is irrelevant for a native app. For adaptivity, full 3PL IRT calibration is impractical at cold start; Elo/Glicko-style online difficulty rating (well-documented in the adaptive-learning literature, used by Duolingo-class systems) converges fast, is O(1) per response, and can be back-filled with offline 2PL/3PL calibration via girth/py-irt once each item has ~200-500 responses. Spaced repetition maps onto a question bank by treating (student, question) or (student, sub-topic) as the "card" and using FSRS-6; benchmarks over ~727M Anki reviews show FSRS-5 log-loss 0.291 / RMSE 5.3% vs SM-2 0.354 / 16.2%. Mobile anti-cheat is asymmetric: iOS gives a real primitive (AutomaticAssessmentConfiguration / AEAssessmentSession, app self-lock), Android needs Lock Task Mode which requires Device Owner — unattainable for a consumer BYOD app, so plan on soft signals (app-state, screenshot blocking, Play Integrity/App Attest) rather than hard kiosk.
FINDINGS:
- (high) Moodle question engine — the reference state machine for attempts (GPLv3+, study only): Moodle is GPLv3+. Its question engine models every attempt as a question_attempt containing an ordered list of question_attempt_steps (each step = one interaction, with its own state, fraction and submitted data), separating question *type* from question *behaviour*. Shipped behaviours: Deferred feedback, Deferred feedback with CBM (certainty-based marking: 3 certainty bands <67% / >67% / >80%, a correct answer at lowest certainty scores 0.33 instead of 1), Immediate feedback, Immediate feedback with CBM, Interactive with multiple tries (tries = number of hints + 1), Adaptive mode, Adaptive mode (no penalties), Manually graded. Contributed behaviours exist (qbehaviour_adaptiveallnothing, qbehaviour_adaptivemultipart). Moodle 5.0 (Apr 2025) shipped a major Question Bank redesign — multiple question banks per course, questions authored inside a quiz stay scoped to that quiz; Moodle 5.1 released 2025-10-06, Moodle 5.2 released 2026-04-20. => IMPLICATION: Copy the *design*, never the code: model our attempt table as append-only steps (attempt -> question_attempt -> step) so a server-authoritative timer, resume-after-crash, partial answers and audit trail all fall out for free. Adopt 3-4 behaviours only: deferred feedback (mock tests), immediate feedback (practice), interactive-with-tries (learning mode). CBM is a genuinely differentiating feature for JEE/NEET negative marking psychology and is cheap to implement.
- (high) TAO (Open Assessment Technologies) — the only QTI-native OSS assessment stack; AGPLv3: TAO Community Edition core is AGPLv3. Repos: github.com/oat-sa (package-tao, lib-tao-qti and ~100 extensions), plus github.com/tao-ce/tao-ce for the CE distribution. PHP-based, QTI + LTI standards-based, used by large national/international assessment programmes (PISA-class). => IMPLICATION: AGPL = do not vendor, do not fork into our backend. Study its QTI item model and outcome-processing/response-processing design if we ever need QTI import. For actually rendering QTI in the app, use the MIT-licensed amp-up-io player instead of TAO.
- (high) TCExam — closest single-purpose OSS CBT engine to our use case, but AGPL-3.0-or-later: github.com/tecnickcom/tcexam. Licence AGPL-3.0-or-later, copyright 2004-2026 Nicola Asuni / Tecnick.com. Actively maintained (594 commits, CI on PHP 8.2/8.3/8.4; MySQL/MariaDB, PostgreSQL, Oracle). Directly relevant features: LaTeX and MathML formula support in questions, random question and answer shuffling per candidate, timed exams, TSV/XML import-export, OMR paper scanning, 26 languages, LDAP/RADIUS/CAS auth. => IMPLICATION: Best single reference for 'CBT engine with heavy maths' semantics (question shuffling, per-candidate ordering, LaTeX in stems). AGPL blocks reuse of code. Its TSV/XML import schema is a useful shape to imitate for our admin bulk-upload format.
- (high) Canvas LMS AGPLv3, Open edX AGPL, Frappe LMS AGPL-3.0, ILIAS GPL, Rogō/ExamSys GPLv3, OpenOLAT Apache-2.0, Sakai ECL-2.0: Canvas (github.com/instructure/canvas-lms, 6.7k stars, ~78.8k commits, Ruby/Rails + Postgres + Redis) is AGPLv3. Open edX is AGPL with some Apache-2.0 components; latest named release Verawood, 22nd community release, 2026-06-09, covering changes 2025-10-30 to 2026-04-09; the Open edX community itself documents 'Dependency Licensing Problems' from AGPL incompatibility. Frappe LMS (github.com/frappe/lms, 3.1k stars, Frappe Framework + Vue) is AGPL-3.0 and has quizzes with multiple question formats. ILIAS is GPL (open-sourced 2000). Rogō (now ExamSys, University of Nottingham, rogo-oss.nottingham.ac.uk / examsys-oss.nottingham.ac.uk) is GPLv3 — purpose-built e-assessment incl. summative exams and item analysis. OpenOLAT (github.com/OpenOLAT/OpenOLAT) is Apache-2.0. Sakai is Educational Community License 2.0 (Apache-2.0 derivative). => IMPLICATION: Only OpenOLAT (Apache-2.0) and Sakai (ECL-2.0) are legally safe to lift code from — but both are large Java monoliths and a poor fit for a Supabase/RN stack, so realistically all of these are 'study'. ExamSys is the highest-signal study target for summative exam workflow + post-hoc item analysis despite GPLv3.
- (high) QTI 3.0 is the only forward-looking interchange standard; QTI 2.1 is legacy, 1.x deprecated: 1EdTech QTI 3.0 is current and stable; QTI 1.0/1.1 are deprecated and 2.x superseded (QTI Result Reporting 3.0 supersedes 2.x). QTI 3 mandates modern W3C tech — HTML5, MathML 3, WCAG 2.0, WAI-ARIA 1.0, SSML 1.1, CSS3 — and targets 'user experience interoperability' so items render identically across delivery systems. LMS support is still uneven in 2025-2026. => IMPLICATION: Do NOT make QTI 3 the internal storage format — it is XML-heavy and would fight our Postgres/JSONB + LaTeX model. Treat it as an export/import adapter built later, only if we need to ingest publisher content or sell content to institutions. MathML 3 is the QTI maths carrier, so keep a LaTeX->MathML conversion path in mind.
- (high) amp-up-io qti3-item-player — MIT-licensed QTI 3 renderer, the practical QTI on-ramp: github.com/amp-up-io/qti3-item-player (Vue 2.6) and github.com/amp-up-io/qti3-item-player-vue3 (Vue 3), both MIT. npm qti3-item-player v1.1.20. Roadmap targets QTI 3 Delivery System 'Basic' + 'Advanced' conformance plus PCI (Portable Custom Interactions) support; sibling qti3-stimulus-player for shared stimulus. Alternative: github.com/agencyenterprise/qti-3-player, framework-agnostic JS/TS, npm-installable. => IMPLICATION: If we ever need to render third-party QTI 3 items in the RN app, embed one of these MIT players inside a WebView rather than writing a QTI renderer. MIT means we can fork and strip it. Verdict: study now, adopt only if a QTI content deal appears.
- (high) Moodle XML is the pragmatic import/export target; GIFT and Aiken are the cheap bulk-authoring formats: Moodle's question bank imports Aiken, Blackboard, Blackboard V6+, Embedded Answers (Cloze), Examview, GIFT, Learnwise, Missing Word, Moodle XML, WebCT; exports GIFT, Moodle XML, XHTML, Word 2010 table format. Moodle's own docs call Moodle XML 'the recommended import question type format' because it carries the maximum question data including per-answer feedback. GIFT is plain-text and covers MCQ, true/false, short answer, matching, missing-word and numerical. Aiken is the simplest — MCQ only, human-readable text. => IMPLICATION: Ship in this order: (1) a first-class CSV/JSON admin bulk format of our own with explicit LaTeX fields and image URLs; (2) GIFT import for coaching-centre teachers who already have Moodle habits (cheap parser, ~300 LOC); (3) Moodle XML import/export for interoperability and as a migration escape hatch. Skip Aiken (subset of GIFT), skip Blackboard/WebCT, skip QTI until a real customer asks.
- (high) H5P is MIT at the core but the PHP library is contaminated with GPL third-party code: h5p.org states 'The H5P software is licenced under the MIT license' (Joubel AS, 2015), but H5P's own licensing page admits third-party input-purification code (CKEditor4-derived and external XSS-protection code) inside the H5P PHP Library forces GPL on that component, and that H5P intends to make the GPL code optional. h5p-standalone (github.com/tunapanda/h5p-standalone) — which renders H5P content with no H5P server — is cleanly MIT. H5P has 50+ content types and integrates via LTI 1.3 across Canvas/Brightspace/Blackboard/Moodle/Totara. => IMPLICATION: If we use H5P at all, use h5p-standalone (MIT) in a WebView and never the PHP library. Realistically: ignore H5P. Its interaction types are web-desktop-shaped, and we need native RN interactions with LaTeX, not an HTML5 authoring ecosystem.
- (high) xAPI 1.0.3 is now IEEE 9274.1.1-2023 — the analytics event model to emit; Caliper is a parallel, not competing, spec: xAPI 1.0.3 was ratified as IEEE 9274.1.1-2023 (sometimes called xAPI 2.0), the first open-source e-learning spec to become an IEEE standard; the 2023 version tightens the JSON data model and RESTful LRS service rather than redesigning it, so 1.0.3 content keeps working, and adds contextAgents. 1EdTech's own position is that xAPI and Caliper are not equivalent and adoption is 'horses-for-courses' — xAPI replaces SCORM/IEEE CMI runtime tracking, Caliper is the 1EdTech Learning Analytics Framework. SCORM is LMS-bound packaging and irrelevant to a native app. => IMPLICATION: Adopt the xAPI *statement shape* (actor/verb/object/result/context) as our internal analytics event schema even if we never run an LRS — verbs like answered, attempted, completed, mastered map perfectly onto question attempts, and it future-proofs institutional integrations. Ignore SCORM entirely. Ignore Caliper unless an Indian university partner demands it. LTI 1.3 only matters if we become a tool inside someone else's LMS — PyLTI1p3 (MIT, github.com/dmitry-viskov/pylti1.3, Django/Flask adapters) is the reference if so.
- (high) ts-fsrs (MIT) is the direct adopt for spaced repetition in a TS/RN + Edge Function stack: github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs — MIT, 724 stars, implements FSRS-6, ESM/CJS/UMD, requires Node >= 20. Companion package @open-spaced-repetition/binding provides the optimiser: trains the 21-weight FSRS parameter vector from review logs and converts CSV. Active CI/CD, low open-issue count (2 issues / 4 PRs). => IMPLICATION: ADOPT. Runs identically in an Expo/RN client (for optimistic next-due computation) and in a Supabase Edge Function (for authoritative scheduling). MIT means zero licence friction. Store the 21-parameter vector per-student (or per-cohort until enough data), plus stability/difficulty/state/last_review on each (student, item) row.
- (high) fsrs-rs (BSD-3) and py-fsrs (MIT) cover the training/optimiser side; Anki itself is AGPL-3.0: github.com/open-spaced-repetition/fsrs-rs — BSD-3-Clause, 398 stars, 353 commits, exposes compute_parameters() to train FSRS parameters from review logs, SM-2 migration helpers, simulation, and an experimental Cost-ADR policy optimiser; bindings for C, Python, Node.js, Dart, PHP. github.com/open-spaced-repetition/py-fsrs — MIT, 455 stars, Python 3.10+, optimiser via `pip install "fsrs[optimizer]"`, JSON serialisation of Card/Scheduler. FSRS-Kotlin is MIT. Anki itself (github.com/ankitects/anki, 29.3k stars) is AGPL-3.0-or-later with BSD-3 contributions and MIT/Apache-2.0 vendored bits (MathJax is Apache-2.0) — its Rust scheduler lives in rslib. => IMPLICATION: Use ts-fsrs at runtime, fsrs-rs or py-fsrs in a nightly GCP batch job to re-fit parameters from accumulated review logs. Anki is AGPL — read the rslib scheduler for understanding, copy nothing. Note Anki bundles MathJax (Apache-2.0) which is also a safe LaTeX renderer choice for us.
- (high) FSRS beats SM-2 decisively on a ~727M-review benchmark — quantified: open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark: ~727M reviews from ~10,000 Anki users, ~349.9M reviews used after filtering outliers and same-day reviews. Reported: SM-2 log-loss 0.354 and retention RMSE 16.2% vs FSRS-5 log-loss 0.291 and RMSE 5.3%. FSRS-6 adds 2 parameters over FSRS-5 (one for same-day reviews, one controlling forgetting-curve shape) and shows 83.3% superiority over SM-17 across collections. Best-in-class in the benchmark is an RWKV neural model (log-loss 0.2773±0.0036, AUC 0.8329±0.0017) — i.e. FSRS is near the practical ceiling. Practitioner guidance: FSRS needs ~20-30% fewer reviews than SM-2 for equal retention; desired retention 0.80-0.95, 0.90 default. => IMPLICATION: Do not implement SM-2. Go straight to FSRS-6 with desired_retention configurable per student and raised (0.92-0.95) as the exam date approaches — an 'exam mode' that compresses intervals to guarantee recall on a fixed date (JEE Main Jan/Apr, NEET May, Advanced May) is a real product feature, not a gimmick.
- (medium) Applying SRS to a question bank: the 'card' should be the concept, not the MCQ: No peer-reviewed study found applying FSRS to competitive-exam MCQ banks specifically (unverified — treat as design judgement). Practical constraints: an MCQ is burned after 1-2 exposures (answer memorised, not concept), whereas FSRS assumes a stable item repeatedly re-tested. FSRS's 4-grade input (Again/Hard/Good/Easy) has no natural MCQ analogue. Practitioner writeups describe exam-prep mode as 'recall reliability over a fixed 6-12 week horizon with moderate-to-high retention targets and strict daily completion'. => IMPLICATION: Model the FSRS card as (student_id, sub_topic_id) or (student_id, concept_id), not (student_id, question_id). When a sub-topic comes due, serve a *fresh* unseen question of matching difficulty from that sub-topic. Derive the FSRS grade from outcome + time: correct & fast = Easy, correct & slow (>1.5x median) = Good, correct-after-flag or near-miss = Hard, wrong or timed-out = Again. Keep a separate per-question 'seen' ledger so questions are not repeated. This is the single highest-leverage differentiator vs Indian incumbents (Allen/Physics Wallah/Unacademy), none of which expose true SRS scheduling.
- (high) IRT libraries: py-irt (MIT), girth (MIT), catsim (BSD-3) — all permissive, all Python: py-irt (github.com/nd-ball/py-irt, MIT, 170 stars, 255 commits): Bayesian IRT via variational inference on Pyro/PyTorch, GPU-accelerated, supports 1PL/2PL/4PL with 3PL 'in the pipeline'; paper arXiv:2203.01282. girth (github.com/eribean/girth, MIT, 123 stars, 180 commits): Rasch/1PL/2PL/3PL dichotomous, Graded Response/Partial Credit/Graded Unfolding polytomous, multidimensional 2PL and GRM, JML/CML/MML estimation, ability estimation via ML/MAP/EAP, bootstrap CIs, polychoric correlations, missing-data support (bootstrap does not support 3PL or GGUM). catsim (github.com/douglasrizzo/catsim, BSD-3-Clause, Python 3.11+): reusable CAT engine with test initialisation, item selection, ability estimation and stopping rules, plus simulation; paper arXiv:1707.03012. => IMPLICATION: ADOPT girth for offline batch calibration (it is the most complete for classic 2PL/3PL and polytomous), py-irt if we ever need GPU-scale joint estimation, catsim to *simulate* an adaptive test policy before shipping one. All three are permissive — run them in a scheduled GCP job that writes a/b/c parameters back into Postgres. Do not attempt real-time IRT in Edge Functions.
- (high) How GRE/GMAT-style adaptive difficulty and 'predicted score' actually work: IRT + CAT is the dominant methodology behind GMAT, NCLEX, NREMT, ASVAB. Mechanism: a large item pool is calibrated on a pretest sample to fix item parameters; for a live candidate the item parameters are held fixed and only theta (ability) is solved for, typically updated after each response by Expected A Posteriori (EAP) with a standard normal prior over the full response pattern. Item selection maximises the item information function at the current theta estimate — the item expected to shrink the standard error most. Termination is when SE(theta) drops below a threshold, so a CAT reaches a given precision in far fewer items than a fixed-length test. 'Predicted score' is just theta mapped through the score-scale conversion of the target exam. => IMPLICATION: Our 'predicted JEE/NEET rank' feature = estimate theta per subject from responses on calibrated items, then map theta -> raw marks -> percentile using historical JEE/NEET cutoff tables. It does NOT require serving an adaptive test — it works on fixed mock tests too, which is the safer v1. Do adaptive *practice* (Elo-driven next-question) before adaptive *tests* (CAT), because CAT needs a well-calibrated pool with 3PL guessing parameters and exposure control.
- (high) Elo-based item difficulty is the practical cold-start alternative to IRT at our scale: Elo for adaptive assessment is well established: Pelánek, 'Applications of the Elo rating system in adaptive educational systems' (Computers & Education, 2016) concludes Elo is 'simple, robust, and effective' for adaptive educational systems; a 2016 study shows combining IRT with Elo yields estimates for both learner ability and item difficulty and recommends it specifically for practice systems where measurement duration is unimportant. 2025-2026 literature refines it: 'Psychometrics of an Elo-based large-scale online learning system' (Computers & Education: AI, 2025); Bolsinova et al., 'Keeping Elo alive' (Br. J. Math. Stat. Psychol., 2026) critiques Elo's measurement properties; a 2025 UMUAI paper investigates dynamic K-value approaches to balance stability vs flexibility. => IMPLICATION: ADOPT Elo for v1. One UPDATE per response, O(1), no batch job, converges in tens of responses per item, works from day one with zero calibration data. Use a decaying K (high K for items with <50 responses, low K after) per the 2025 dynamic-K work. Keep student rating per (subject, chapter) not global. Then run girth/py-irt monthly to produce proper 2PL parameters for items with >=200-500 responses and use those for the predicted-score model. This dual-track (Elo online + IRT offline) is the right architecture for 10k concurrent students.
- (high) Elo-MMR (MIT) ships a Codeforces-rating implementation; openskill.py (MIT) and skillratings (Rust) cover the rest: github.com/EbTech/Elo-MMR — MIT (except a deprecated C++ TrueSkill dir), Rust, implements Glicko all-pairs, BAR BT-all-pairs, Codeforces, Topcoder, TrueSkill-SPb and the Elo-MMR system itself; designed for multi-player *contests* (thousands of participants ranked in one event), which is exactly the shape of a mock-test leaderboard. github.com/vivekjoshy/openskill.py — MIT, 363 stars, 5 rating models incl. Plackett-Luce/Weng-Lin, 100% test coverage, C-compiled wheels, faster than TrueSkill (arXiv:2401.05451). github.com/atomflunder/skillratings — Rust, Elo/Glicko-2/TrueSkill and more. TrueSkill itself is patent-encumbered by Microsoft — openskill exists precisely to avoid that. => IMPLICATION: For a 10,000-student mock test, a single test is one multi-player contest, so Codeforces-style or Elo-MMR ranking is a better fit than pairwise Elo for the *leaderboard rating*. Keep the item-difficulty Elo and the student-contest rating as two separate systems. AVOID TrueSkill (patent); openskill.py is the clean MIT substitute.
- (medium) CTFd (Apache-2.0) is the licence-clean contest reference; DOMjudge (GPLv2+) is the scale reference: CTFd (github.com/CTFd/CTFd) — Apache-2.0, 6.8k stars, 2.7k forks, 1,716 commits, Python/Flask + MySQL/Postgres + Redis for caching and queues, Docker Compose deploy, dynamic scoring, team competitions, tie resolution, plugin/theme architecture. Third-party 2026 assessment: CTFd handles events reliably under ~1,000 concurrent participants out of the box — i.e. an order of magnitude below our 10,000 target without work. DOMjudge (github.com/DOMjudge/domjudge, docs v8.2.x) is GPLv2-or-later and powers many ICPC regionals and acts as backup at the ICPC World Finals — its judgehost/queue separation is the proven concurrent-contest architecture. => IMPLICATION: Study CTFd's dynamic-scoring and freeze/scoreboard-caching patterns (Apache-2.0, safe to copy). Note explicitly that CTFd's stock architecture is a ~1k-concurrent design — do not treat 'CTFd scales' as evidence for our 10k target. Steal DOMjudge's idea of decoupling submission intake from grading via a queue: writes go to Postgres/Redis fast, scoring and leaderboard materialisation happen asynchronously.
- (high) Redis sorted sets: concrete leaderboard capacity numbers for 10k concurrent: ZADD/ZREVRANK/ZREVRANGE are O(log N); ZRANGEBYSCORE-style range queries O(log N + M). Reported figures: ~100M members in a sorted set ≈ 6GB RAM; a single Redis instance sustains ~100k ZADD ops/sec on modern hardware and 100k+ ops/sec on a 100M-member set; 'millions of ranked members in roughly 500 MB of RAM'. Recommended key naming encodes scope + timeframe: leaderboard:global:alltime, leaderboard:global:weekly:2026-W22, leaderboard:region:na:daily:2026-05-28. ZREVRANGE with an offset around the user's own rank gives the 'players around you' view. => IMPLICATION: Our worst case is trivially small for Redis: 10,000 students x a few hundred tests is <10M members, well under 1GB. Use one ZSET per test (leaderboard:test:{id}), one per weekly streak board, one per chapter. Use ZREVRANK for 'your rank' and ZREVRANGE around rank for the neighbour window — never compute ranks in Postgres. Persist final standings to Postgres after the test closes and let the ZSET expire.
- (high) Safe Exam Browser is MPL-2.0 but has no Android build — hard blocker for our Android half: SEB is licensed under the Mozilla Public License. Available for Windows, macOS and iOS (iOS 9.3.5+); explicitly NOT available for Android or Chromebooks. On iOS, SEB uses Apple's Automatic Assessment Configuration (AAC), presented to the user as 'app self-lock', and the docs call AAC the recommended kiosk mode because it applies all restrictions (including keyboard and dictionary) automatically. SEB Server (github.com/SafeExamBrowser/seb-server) reached v2 with built-in Screen Proctoring, refined in 2.2; seb-screen-proctoring-server is MPL-2.0, Java/Spring, last updated July 2026. Moodle integrates via quizaccess_seb (GPLv3) since Moodle 3.9 with a 'Launch Safe Exam Browser' button. => IMPLICATION: IGNORE SEB as a dependency — no Android build, and it is a separate browser app, not something an RN app can embed. But ADOPT its iOS technique directly: call AutomaticAssessmentConfiguration / AEAssessmentSession from an Expo config plugin / native module for real OS-level lockdown during mock tests. MPL-2.0 is file-level copyleft so even if we did lift a file, only that file must stay open — much safer than AGPL.
- (high) Android has no consumer-grade equivalent to iOS assessment mode: Android Lock Task Mode (developer.android.com/work/dpc/dedicated-devices/lock-task-mode) genuinely prevents exit, but requires the app to be Device Owner or whitelisted by a DPC — a managed/enterprise provisioning step, not something a consumer installs from Play. Plain screen pinning looks similar but the user can exit at any time. Expo cannot implement real kiosk mode without native modules. Community options: github.com/Riuhou/react-native-kiosk-manager (npm v0.12.2, TypeScript, lock task + boot autostart), react-native-expo-kiosk (v1.1.0), github.com/temaivanoff/react-native-lock-task. For capture prevention: github.com/wn-na/react-native-capture-protection (blocks/detects screenshots, screen recording and app-switcher preview, Expo-compatible via config plugin but not Expo Go) and react-native-screenguard. => IMPLICATION: Design anti-cheat as a *risk score*, not a lock. Ship: (1) iOS AAC hard lock where available; (2) Android screen pinning as a soft nudge + react-native-capture-protection to kill screenshots and the app-switcher snapshot; (3) server-side signals — AppState background transitions with timestamps, response-time outliers, answer-change entropy, impossible-speed correct answers, duplicate device fingerprints, IP clustering. Server-authoritative timer must keep running while backgrounded, and every backgrounding must be logged to the attempt as an event.
- (medium) Moodle Proctoring plugin (GPLv3) — the OSS webcam-proctoring reference, still shipping in Nov 2025: quizaccess_proctoring (github.com/eLearning-BS23/moodle-quizaccess_proctoring, maintained by Brain Station 23), GPLv3-or-later. Captures a webcam still every 30 seconds (configurable), stores PNGs, blocks quiz access if the camera is denied, and post-hoc face-matches captures against a reference photo via the BS Face Matching API. A release supporting Moodle 4.3-5.0 landed 2025-11-06. Related commercial-integration plugins exist (quizaccess_autoproctor, ProctorLink). => IMPLICATION: Its interval-capture + post-hoc review pattern is the right low-cost model, but GPLv3 blocks reuse and its face-matching is an external paid API. For us: on-device face presence detection (ML Kit / TFLite, Apache-2.0) with only *events* uploaded (face_absent, multiple_faces) rather than images — far cheaper on Supabase Storage, far better for DPDP Act 2023 compliance with minors. Full webcam proctoring on consumer phones for JEE/NEET practice is likely product-inappropriate; reserve it for paid proctored mocks only.
- (high) Moodle App (Apache-2.0) is the best legally-safe mobile reference for offline attempt sync: github.com/moodlehq/moodleapp — Apache-2.0, Ionic + Angular, 11,552 commits, 983 stars / 2.5k forks, actively maintained. It implements offline quiz attempts with later synchronisation against a server-authoritative Moodle backend, which is precisely the hard problem for Indian users on flaky mobile data. => IMPLICATION: ADOPT-AS-REFERENCE. Apache-2.0 means we can read and even adapt its sync/conflict-resolution logic legally. Key pattern to copy: queue answer-save operations locally with monotonic sequence numbers and a client timestamp, replay on reconnect, and let the server reject anything past the authoritative deadline. This is the one large open-source codebase in this space we can legally borrow from.
- (medium) Named-but-not-found projects: 'Quizzes', 'Sparky', 'Answerly' have no identifiable OSS assessment repo: Unverified — repeated searches surfaced no open-source assessment platform matching these names; 'Sparky' resolves to SparkyLinux/Spark XMPP/Spark NLP, and 'Answerly' to no OSS repo. Adjacent 2025-2026 finds instead: jovVix (github.com/Improwised/jovVix, real-time live quiz with dynamic leaderboards), QST (github.com/bobb34/QST, GPLv2, v3.12.11 released 2026-06-20, STEM-oriented with MathJax/TeX/MathML/mhchem equation authoring), OpenQuiz (github.com/yerdaulet-damir/openquiz, Next.js + Supabase, flashcard/drill/exam/mistakes modes with an SRS strength score), cbthost/cbthost-exam-system (PHP CBT admin panel), Samkarya/online-exam-questions (JSON question DB with LaTeX, but a bespoke 'ExamOven Non-Commercial Open Source License' — non-commercial, unusable for us). => IMPLICATION: Deprioritise these. The only two worth 30 minutes: QST for its STEM equation-authoring UX (GPLv2, study only) and OpenQuiz because it is the same stack we are on (Next.js + Supabase) — check its Postgres schema for SRS state columns, but verify its licence before reading closely. Explicitly avoid any 'non-commercial' licensed question dataset.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- Adopt a permissive-only dependency policy for shipped code: MIT / BSD / Apache-2.0 only, MPL-2.0 acceptable (file-level copyleft), and hard-ban AGPL and GPL in the app bundle and in the Supabase/GCP backend. Enforce with an automated licence scan in CI (e.g. license-checker for npm, pip-licenses for the Python batch jobs) that fails the build on GPL/AGPL. The AGPL set to block by name: Canvas LMS, Open edX, TAO, TCExam, Frappe LMS, Anki. The GPL set: Moodle and all its plugins, ILIAS, Rogo/ExamSys, DOMjudge, QST, quizaccess_proctoring, quizaccess_seb.
- Adopt ts-fsrs (MIT, FSRS-6) now for spaced repetition, keyed on (student_id, sub_topic_id) rather than (student_id, question_id), deriving the 4-level grade from correctness + response time, and serving a fresh unseen question from the due sub-topic. Run fsrs-rs (BSD-3) or py-fsrs (MIT) nightly on GCP to re-optimise the 21-parameter vector per cohort. Expose a 'exam countdown mode' that raises desired_retention from 0.90 toward 0.95 as the JEE/NEET date approaches.
- Ship the dual-track difficulty architecture: online Elo (with decaying K — high K under ~50 responses per item, low K after, per the 2025 dynamic-K literature) for immediate cold-start item difficulty and adaptive practice selection; plus a monthly offline 2PL/3PL calibration with girth (MIT) for items with >=200 responses, feeding a theta-based 'predicted JEE/NEET percentile' that maps theta -> raw marks -> historical cutoff percentile. Keep student contest rating (Elo-MMR / openskill.py, both MIT) as a third, separate system for mock-test leaderboards — do not conflate it with ability theta.
- Model the attempt store on Moodle's question_attempt / question_attempt_step design (append-only step rows per interaction, each with state + fraction + submitted data) without copying any GPL code. This single decision buys server-authoritative timing, crash-resume, partial-answer persistence, review-mode replay and a tamper-evident audit trail, and it is what makes randomised per-student ordering and sectioned papers tractable. Implement 3 behaviours only: deferred feedback (mock), immediate feedback (practice), interactive-with-tries (learn) — plus optional CBM as a differentiator given JEE/NEET negative marking.
- For import/export, build in this order and stop when demand stops: (1) our own JSON/CSV admin bulk format with explicit LaTeX and image-URL fields; (2) GIFT parser for teacher-authored bulk text; (3) Moodle XML in/out as the interoperability escape hatch; (4) QTI 3.0 adapter only if an institutional or publisher deal requires it, and then wrap the MIT amp-up-io qti3-item-player in a WebView rather than writing a renderer. Skip Aiken, SCORM, Blackboard/WebCT entirely.
- Adopt the xAPI (IEEE 9274.1.1-2023) statement shape as the internal analytics event schema from day one — actor / verb / object / result / context, with verbs like attempted, answered, completed, mastered — even without running an LRS. It costs nothing now, makes admin analytics queryable in a standard vocabulary, and de-risks any future institutional integration. Ignore Caliper and SCORM.
- Implement anti-cheat as an asymmetric two-tier system: on iOS call AutomaticAssessmentConfiguration / AEAssessmentSession via an Expo config plugin for genuine OS-level lockdown (this is exactly what Safe Exam Browser does on iOS); on Android accept that Lock Task Mode needs Device Owner and is unreachable on consumer BYOD, so use screen pinning as a nudge plus react-native-capture-protection to block screenshots, recording and the app-switcher snapshot. Back both with server-side behavioural signals (backgrounding events, response-time outliers, impossible-speed correctness, device-fingerprint duplication) recorded as attempt events and surfaced as a risk score to ADMIN.
- Use Redis sorted sets as the only ranking surface — one ZSET per test, per weekly board, per chapter, keyed leaderboard:test:{id} and leaderboard:global:weekly:{iso-week} — with ZREVRANK for 'your rank' and ZREVRANGE around the user's rank for the neighbour window. At 10k students this is well under 1GB and far under the ~100k ops/sec single-instance ceiling. Materialise final standings into Postgres when a test closes and let the ZSET TTL out.
- Read the Moodle App (Apache-2.0) offline-sync implementation before designing our own offline attempt handling — it is the only large, mature, legally-borrowable mobile assessment codebase in this space, and offline resilience on Indian mobile networks is a first-order requirement, not a nice-to-have.
PITFALLS:
- AGPL contamination is the number one legal risk and it is not obvious to engineers: reading Moodle/Canvas/TAO/TCExam source to 'understand the schema' is fine, but copying a table definition verbatim, a grading function, or an XML parser into our backend triggers the AGPL network clause and would require open-sourcing our entire server. Clean-room the design; never paste. Also watch transitive contamination: H5P's core is MIT but its PHP library embeds GPL purification code, and Open edX's own wiki documents ongoing AGPL-incompatible dependency problems.
- Non-commercial and bespoke licences masquerading as open source: Samkarya/online-exam-questions ships under an 'ExamOven Non-Commercial Open Source License' — using that question data in a paid app is a licence violation. Any scraped or community JEE/NEET question dataset must be licence-audited before ingestion, and PYQ questions themselves carry copyright questions in India regardless of the repo licence.
- Treating FSRS as a drop-in for MCQs will fail: an MCQ is consumed after one or two exposures because the student memorises the option, not the concept. If the card is the question, the algorithm optimises for re-showing questions whose answers are already known, and retention metrics will look great while learning does not transfer. The card must be the concept/sub-topic, with a fresh item drawn each time and a separate seen-ledger to prevent repeats.
- Attempting real-time IRT (2PL/3PL) or a full CAT at launch will stall the product: 3PL needs a guessing parameter estimated from large samples, calibration needs roughly 200-500 responses per item before parameters stabilise, and CAT additionally needs item-exposure control or the pool leaks. With a cold-start bank there is nothing to calibrate. Elo first, IRT later, CAT possibly never — and never run IRT estimation inside a request-path Edge Function.
- Assuming CTFd or DOMjudge prove our concurrency story: independent 2026 assessment puts stock CTFd at reliable performance below ~1,000 concurrent participants, an order of magnitude under our 10,000 target, and DOMjudge's scale comes from a decoupled judgehost queue, not from the web tier. Also beware the thundering herd unique to sectioned timed tests — 10,000 students all submitting in the final 60 seconds and all polling a leaderboard at T+0 is a burst pattern that neither project's default config anticipates.
- Android kiosk expectations will be set wrong by demos: Lock Task Mode requires Device Owner provisioning (factory-reset-level enterprise enrolment) and is simply unavailable for a consumer Play Store install; plain screen pinning is user-exitable at will. Expo Go cannot host any of these native modules. Promising 'exam-proof lockdown on Android' in marketing or to coaching partners will not survive contact with reality — and Safe Exam Browser has no Android build at all to fall back on.
- Webcam proctoring on minors' devices is a compliance and trust hazard in India: the Moodle proctoring pattern uploads a PNG every 30 seconds, which for 10,000 students is enormous Storage cost and, under the DPDP Act 2023's stricter treatment of children's data, a significant liability. Prefer on-device inference emitting only events, and gate any image capture behind explicit, separately-consented paid proctored mocks.
- TrueSkill is patent-encumbered by Microsoft — if a leaderboard implementation reaches for it (or for a TrueSkill port), that is a patent exposure, not a licence one, and a licence scanner will not catch it. Use openskill.py or Elo-MMR (both MIT) instead.
- QTI 3.0 looks like the 'right' standard and is a classic scope trap: it is XML-heavy, carries maths as MathML 3 rather than LaTeX, has uneven LMS support even in 2026, and would force a lossy round-trip against our JSONB + LaTeX model. Building QTI support before a customer demands it burns weeks for zero user-visible value.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- What licence does yerdaulet-damir/openquiz carry? It is the closest stack match (Next.js + Supabase with an SRS strength score) and its Postgres schema for SRS state would be directly instructive, but the licence was not verified — confirm before reading it closely.
- Does ts-fsrs's @open-spaced-repetition/binding optimiser run in a Supabase Edge Function (Deno) runtime, or does it require a Node >=20 environment with native bindings? If native-only, parameter optimisation must live in a GCP Cloud Run job rather than in Supabase.
- Is there any peer-reviewed or industry evidence for FSRS applied to MCQ question banks rather than flashcards? Nothing was found; if none exists, the concept-level-card design should be validated with an internal A/B against a simpler leech/wrong-answer-requeue baseline before it is marketed as a differentiator.
- What is the current licence and maintenance state of Rogo/ExamSys under its new name at examsys-oss.nottingham.ac.uk — is the GPLv3 repo still receiving commits in 2025-2026, and does it have the item-analysis reports (facility, discrimination index) worth reimplementing?
- Does Apple's AutomaticAssessmentConfiguration require any entitlement, MDM supervision, or App Store review justification for a consumer education app, or can any app call AEAssessmentSession? SEB's docs imply 'approved developers' for some configurations — this needs confirming with Apple's current documentation before it is designed into the mock-test flow.
- Are there 2025-2026 open-source Indian-market-specific question banks or PYQ datasets with a genuinely permissive licence, and what is the copyright position on reproducing NTA JEE/NEET past papers in a commercial app under Indian law?
- For predicted-rank accuracy, which historical JEE Main/Advanced and NEET marks-to-percentile-to-rank mapping tables are usable, and are they redistributable? Theta estimation is the easy half; the score-scale conversion is the half that determines whether students trust the number.
- Does Supabase Realtime plus Postgres handle the end-of-test burst (10,000 concurrent submissions plus leaderboard subscriptions within ~60 seconds), or does the submission path need to terminate in Redis with asynchronous drain into Postgres? This is the single architectural question that the open-source contest systems (DOMjudge's queue separation) suggest we should answer before launch.
- Which specific FSRS-6 parameter set should be the cold-start default for a competitive-exam population, given that the published defaults are fitted on Anki flashcard reviews with a very different item mix and review cadence?

## supabase-scale
SUMMARY: Supabase can serve 10k+ concurrent JEE/NEET test-takers, but only if the architecture respects four hard constraints. (1) RLS is the dominant performance risk: Supabase's own troubleshooting doc shows unwrapped auth.uid() and unindexed policy columns taking policies from <0.1ms to 171ms/11,000ms/178,000ms; the fixes (wrap every function call in a scalar SELECT so the planner hoists it into an InitPlan, index every RLS predicate column and FK, add `TO authenticated`, push joins into SECURITY DEFINER helpers, and duplicate the RLS filter in the client query) are free and mandatory. (2) RBAC must live in a custom_access_token_hook writing a role claim into the JWT, never in user_metadata — Supabase's own `splinter` lint 0015 classifies RLS referencing user_metadata as ERROR because any client can call updateUser({data:{is_admin:true}}); app_metadata/JWT claims via the hook plus a server-owned user_roles table is the supported pattern. (3) Connection math is misunderstood: mobile clients hitting PostgREST/GoTrue over HTTPS do NOT consume one Postgres connection each (PostgREST owns a small internal pool), so a 4XL (16 vCPU / 64 GB / 480 direct connections / 3,000 pooler clients, ~$960/mo) is ample headroom for 10k concurrent exam-takers; only your own Cloud Run/Edge workers need Supavisor transaction mode on port 6543, which does not support prepared statements. (4) Realtime postgres_changes is the wrong primitive for leaderboards — it is single-threaded and caps near 4,000 msgs/sec with RLS, while Broadcast benchmarks at 224k msgs/sec across 32k clients; and Pro's default 500 peak connections must be raised (spend-cap off → 10,000; $10 per 1,000 peak connections). For exam data, use one narrow row per answer in a monthly/exam-range-partitioned table with BRIN on the timestamp, ON CONFLICT on a (attempt_id, question_id) idempotency key, batched 10–30s client syncs, READ COMMITTED with advisory locks for scoring rather than SERIALIZABLE. Storage cost is a non-issue (~180M answer rows ≈ 25–35 GB ≈ $4/mo at $0.125/GB); egress ($0.09/GB uncached, $0.03/GB cached, 250 GB included on Pro) and question-image delivery are the real variable costs, and Smart CDN + image transformations ($5 per 1,000 origin images beyond 100) handle those. GCP's role should be narrow and deliberate: Supabase is AWS-only (India = ap-south-1 Mumbai), so put GCP pieces in asia-south1 (Tier 1 Cloud Run pricing) and accept cross-cloud egress on both sides — FCM for push (mandatory for RN/Expo Android anyway), Cloud Run + Cloud Tasks (500 dispatches/sec/queue, 5,000 concurrent, $0.40/M ops) for async scoring, BigQuery ($6.25/TiB scanned, $0.02/GB active storage) for analytics. Self-hosting Supabase on GCP is not worth it below roughly $500–1,000/mo of managed compute spend.
FINDINGS:
- (high) Supabase's official RLS performance doc: six concrete fixes with measured deltas: supabase.com/docs/guides/troubleshooting/rls-performance-and-best-practices-Z5Jjwv lists, with benchmark numbers: (1) index RLS columns — `create index userid on test_table using btree (user_id)` → 171ms to <0.1ms on 100k rows, 'over 100x'; (2) wrap functions in SELECT — `(select auth.uid()) = user_id` and `(select is_admin())` → 11,000ms to 10ms, and 178,000ms to 12ms; (3) add the same filter in the application query (.eq('user_id', userId)) → 171ms to 9ms; (4) wrap join-table lookups in SECURITY DEFINER functions → 178,000ms to 12ms; (5) restructure joins as `team_id in (select team_id from team_user where user_id = auth.uid())` rather than `auth.uid() in (...)` → 9,000ms to 20ms; (6) specify `TO authenticated` on the policy → 170ms to <0.1ms. A 1M-row team-lookup policy went from >2 minutes to 2ms. => IMPLICATION: Every policy on questions, attempts, responses, notes must be written in this exact shape from day one. Codify it as a migration lint: no bare auth.uid()/auth.jwt()/custom function in USING/WITH CHECK, every policy names TO authenticated or TO anon, every column referenced in a policy carries a btree index, and every FK is explicitly indexed (Postgres does not auto-index FKs).
- (high) Why the SELECT-wrap works: InitPlan hoisting, not caching: Postgres treats `(select auth.uid())` as an uncorrelated scalar subquery and hoists it into an InitPlan executed exactly once per query; every row check then compares against a literal. A bare auth.uid() in the policy is re-evaluated per row (100,000 invocations on a 100k-row scan). The same applies to any STABLE helper function. Only valid when the function's result does not depend on the row being checked. => IMPLICATION: Applies to your authorize()/has_exam_access() helpers too. Declare helpers STABLE (not VOLATILE) and always call them as (select fn(...)) inside policies. VOLATILE functions cannot be hoisted and will be called per row.
- (high) user_metadata in RLS is an ERROR-level security lint — the privilege-escalation trap: Supabase's `splinter` database advisor rule 0015_rls_references_user_metadata is level ERROR: 'Supabase Auth user_metadata is used to set metadata about the user on sign up. It is designed to be manipulated by the user themselves.' An authenticated client can call `supabase.auth.updateUser({ data: { is_admin: true } })`. raw_user_meta_data is user-writable via auth.update(); raw_app_meta_data is NOT user-writable and is the correct place for authorization data. Remediation per Supabase: 'There is no one-size-fits-all solution' — use immutable attributes or a server-controlled permissions table. => IMPLICATION: Never store the ADMIN/STUDENT role in user_metadata. Store it in a public.user_roles table owned by the service role, project it into the JWT via the custom access token hook, and read it as auth.jwt() ->> 'user_role'. Run the Supabase Security Advisor in CI and fail the build on lint 0015.
- (high) Supabase's canonical RBAC pattern: custom_access_token_hook + user_roles + role_permissions + authorize(): Official pattern: enums `app_role` and `app_permission`; tables public.user_roles(user_id uuid references auth.users, role app_role, unique(user_id, role)) and public.role_permissions(role, permission, unique(role,permission)); a `custom_access_token_hook(event jsonb) returns jsonb language plpgsql stable` that does `claims := jsonb_set(claims,'{user_role}',to_jsonb(user_role))`. Critical grants: `grant usage on schema public to supabase_auth_admin; grant execute on function public.custom_access_token_hook to supabase_auth_admin; revoke execute ... from authenticated, anon, public; grant all on table public.user_roles to supabase_auth_admin; revoke all on table public.user_roles from authenticated, anon, public;` plus a policy `for select to supabase_auth_admin using (true)`. The authorize() helper is `language plpgsql stable security definer set search_path = ''` and policies call it as `using ( (SELECT authorize('channels.delete')) )`. => IMPLICATION: Map directly: app_role = ('admin','student'); app_permission = ('questions.write','tests.publish','analytics.read','attempts.grade', ...). Note `set search_path = ''` on every SECURITY DEFINER function — omitting it is a search-path hijack vulnerability. Claim only appears in NEW tokens, so a role change requires a token refresh (default access token TTL 3600s) or an explicit refreshSession() call.
- (high) Compute tier table: connections, IOPS and price per tier (2026): Nano 0.5GB/60 direct/200 pooler/$0; Micro 1GB/60/200/~$10; Small 2GB/90/400/~$15; Medium 4GB/120/600/~$60; Large 8GB (2 dedicated cores)/160/800/~$110; XL 16GB (4 cores)/240/1,000/~$210; 2XL 32GB (8 cores)/380/1,500/~$410; 4XL 64GB (16 cores)/480/3,000/~$960; 8XL 128GB (32 cores)/490/6,000/~$1,870; 12XL 192GB/500/9,000/~$2,800; 16XL 256GB/500/12,000/~$3,730. Baseline IOPS scale 250 (Nano) → 80,000 (16XL); baseline disk throughput 5 MB/s → 2,375 MB/s. Max direct connections plateau at ~500 above 8XL — vertical scaling buys RAM/CPU/IOPS, not connections. => IMPLICATION: For 10k concurrent exam-takers on the Data API, 2XL (~$410/mo) is the realistic floor and 4XL (~$960/mo) is the safe target for a peak test window; you can schedule the upgrade for the exam window and scale back down. Because connection count plateaus at 500 direct, do NOT plan an architecture that needs one DB connection per student.
- (high) PostgREST/GoTrue do not consume one Postgres connection per mobile client: Mobile clients talk HTTPS to PostgREST (Data API), GoTrue (Auth), Storage and Realtime — these are stateless HTTP services that maintain their own small internal pools to Postgres. Supavisor's per-tier 'pooler client connections' quota (1,500 at 2XL, 3,000 at 4XL) applies to raw Postgres wire-protocol clients (your Cloud Run workers, admin tooling, ETL), not to REST users. Supavisor transaction mode (port 6543) proxies many client connections onto a smaller pool of server connections and closes idle server connections after 5 minutes; session mode (port 5432) pins one backend per client for the whole session and can queue clients for up to a minute. Pool Size is a single setting shared across both ports; running Supavisor and the dedicated PgBouncer simultaneously with pool size 30 can produce up to 60 backend connections. => IMPLICATION: Route 100% of student traffic through the Data API / Edge Functions, not through direct Postgres. Reserve Supavisor transaction mode (6543) for Cloud Run scoring workers and set their client-side pool small (e.g. 2–5 per instance x max instances <= pool size). This is the single biggest lever that makes 10k concurrent affordable.
- (high) Transaction mode breaks prepared statements — a silent ORM/driver failure: Supabase docs: 'Transaction mode does not support prepared statements. To avoid errors, turn off prepared statements for your connection library.' Concretely: node-postgres is fine by default; Prisma requires `?pgbouncer=true` (and `statement_cache_size=0` for some drivers); asyncpg requires `statement_cache_size=0`; JDBC requires `prepareThreshold=0`. Session mode (5432) does support prepared statements. Direct connections and the dedicated pooler are IPv6 by default (IPv4 needs a paid add-on); the shared Supavisor pooler is IPv4-only on all tiers. => IMPLICATION: If any GCP-side worker uses Prisma/asyncpg against port 6543 without disabling prepared statements you get intermittent 'prepared statement "s0" already exists' errors that only appear under concurrency — i.e. exactly during the exam. Bake the flag into the connection string and load-test at concurrency > pool size.
- (medium) Supavisor scale ceiling is not your bottleneck: Supabase's Supavisor benchmark: 1,003,200 simultaneous client connections across two 64-core nodes; 250,000 on a single 16-core ARM node; 20,000 QPS sustained with 400 direct DB connections in the pool. Latency at 5,000 QPS: median 2ms / p95 3ms / p99 23ms. At 20,000 QPS: median 18.4ms / p95 46.9ms / p99 68ms. Supavisor adds ~2–3ms per query versus PgBouncer. (Benchmark published 2023-08-11 — older source, but Supavisor is still the shipped pooler in 2026.) => IMPLICATION: Budget ~2–5ms of pooler overhead on every query from GCP workers. If your scoring path does 10 round-trips per attempt, that's 20–50ms of pure pooler tax — collapse scoring into a single PL/pgSQL function call or a single batched statement.
- (high) Realtime: postgres_changes is single-threaded and caps ~4,000 msgs/sec with RLS; Broadcast is 50x faster: Supabase Realtime benchmarks: WebSocket Broadcast — 32,000 concurrent users, 224,000 msgs/sec, 64,000 channel joins, median 6ms / p95 28ms / p99 213ms. Database Broadcast — 80,000 users, 10,000 msgs/sec, median 46ms / p95 132ms / p99 159ms. Broadcast with RLS auth — 50,000 users, >150,000 msgs/sec, median 19ms. Large scale — 250,000 users, >800,000 msgs/sec. But Postgres Changes: 'Database changes are processed on a single thread to maintain the change order'; a Large→16XL DB supports up to 200,000 clients at only 0.05 changes/sec without RLS, and RLS configurations 'max out around 4,000 total messages/second'. => IMPLICATION: Never build the live leaderboard or 'students currently in exam' counter on postgres_changes. Use Broadcast (preferably Broadcast-from-Database triggers with an explicit topic per test) or, better, compute leaderboards in Redis and push periodic Broadcast snapshots every 5–10s. Reserve postgres_changes for low-frequency admin views only.
- (high) Realtime quotas will block a 10k-concurrent test on default Pro settings: Concurrent peak connections: Free 200, Pro 500, Pro with spend cap DISABLED 10,000, Team 10,000, Enterprise 10,000+. Messages/sec and channel joins/sec: Free 100, Pro 500, Pro no-spend-cap 2,500, Team 2,500. Channels per client: 100. Max payload: Broadcast 256 KB Free / 3,000 KB Pro+; postgres_changes 1,024 KB. Refusal errors are `too_many_channels`, `too_many_connections`, `too_many_joins`. Billing: peak connections quota 500 on Pro/Team, overage $10 per 1,000 peak connections; Realtime messages 5M included on Pro/Team, overage $2.50 per 1M messages. => IMPLICATION: Turning off the spend cap is a hard prerequisite before any 10k-student mock test — otherwise you are capped at 500 concurrent sockets and 500 joins/sec, and 10,000 students all joining in the first 60 seconds of a scheduled test will trip too_many_joins. Even at 10,000 the joins/sec limit of 2,500 means you must stagger channel joins with client-side jitter. Cost at 10k peak: ~$95/mo of peak-connection overage.
- (high) Edge Functions hard limits: 256 MB memory, 2s CPU time, 400s wall clock: Max memory 256 MB. Max CPU time 2s per request (actual on-CPU time; async I/O excluded). Wall clock: 150s Free, 400s paid. Request idle timeout 150s → 504. Max function size 20 MB (CLI-bundled) / 5 MB (server-bundled). Functions per project: Free 100, Pro 500, Team 1000. Log message max 10,000 chars; log threshold 100 events per 10s. Cold starts improved dramatically in July 2025: average 870ms → 42ms, p95 8,502ms → 86ms, p99 15,069ms → 460ms, worst case 24,300ms → 1,630ms; 'spikes > 1s' fell from 47% to 4%. => IMPLICATION: 2s CPU time rules out scoring a 180-question paper with heavy per-question logic in an Edge Function if it involves CPU-bound work; it is fine for I/O-bound orchestration. Put bulk scoring in a Postgres function (no CPU limit beyond statement_timeout) or on Cloud Run (60-min timeout). p99 cold start of 460ms is still visible on a 'submit test' tap — keep the submit path on the Data API (a single RPC) rather than an Edge Function.
- (high) Auth rate limits will throttle a 10k-signup burst — several are NOT configurable: Built-in email provider: 2 emails per hour, project-wide (only lifted with custom SMTP). OTP sends project-wide: 30/hour default (configurable). Per-user OTP/magic-link/signup-confirmation/password-reset: 60-second cooldown (configurable). Verification /auth/v1/verify: 360/hour per IP, NOT configurable. Token refresh /auth/v1/token: 1,800/hour per IP, NOT configurable. MFA challenge/verify: 15/hour per IP, NOT configurable. Anonymous sign-ins: 30/hour per IP, NOT configurable. Mechanism is a token bucket with max capacity 30 requests, so short bursts of up to 30 are absorbed. Configurable limits live in Dashboard > Authentication > Rate Limits. CAPTCHA (hCaptcha / Cloudflare Turnstile) is togglable under Settings > Authentication > Bot and Abuse Protection and protects signup, sign-in and password reset. => IMPLICATION: Three actionable consequences: (a) custom SMTP (Resend/SES/Postmark) is mandatory before launch — the default 2 emails/hour makes any real signup flow impossible; (b) the per-IP limits are dangerous in India where large numbers of students share a carrier CGNAT IP or a coaching-centre WiFi — 360 verifies/hour and 1,800 token refreshes/hour per IP can throttle an entire coaching batch sitting in one room; (c) raise the project-wide OTP/hour limit well above 30 before a signup campaign, and enable Turnstile to stop OTP-burning bots (each wasted OTP is real money in India).
- (medium) India phone OTP economics: use the Send SMS Hook with an Indian DLT provider, not Twilio: Indian SMS OTP delivered cost 2026: ₹0.10–₹0.45. MSG91 ~₹0.15 (some sources ₹0.15–0.20), Gupshup ~₹0.17, Kaleyra ~₹0.18, Twilio India ~₹0.45–0.63 with forex surcharge — MSG91 vs Twilio saves 60–70%. TRAI DLT registration is mandatory: ~₹5,900 principal-entity + ~₹5,900 header, amortise over 12 months. Supabase supports a custom Send SMS Hook (Postgres function or HTTP endpoint) so any Indian provider can be wired in without leaving Supabase Auth. => IMPLICATION: At 10,000 signups with ~1.4 OTPs each: MSG91 ≈ ₹2,100 (~$25) vs Twilio ≈ ₹6,300–8,800 (~$75–105). More importantly, DLT-registered transactional routes on Jio/Airtel/Vi/BSNL deliver far more reliably in India than international routes. Budget the ₹11,800 one-time DLT cost and a 1–2 week registration lead time into the launch plan.
- (high) Supabase is AWS-only — India = ap-south-1 Mumbai; GCP is a different cloud with cross-cloud egress: Supabase runs exclusively on AWS. The India region is 'South Asia (Mumbai), ap-south-1'.
