General regions (East US, Central EU, Southeast Asia) do not support read replicas or API management. Cloud Run's Indian regions are asia-south1 (Mumbai, Tier 1 pricing) and asia-south2 (Delhi, Tier 2); asia-south1 is generally available for standard Cloud Run (the 'by invitation only' note applies specifically to L4 GPU configurations). Cloud Run egress to internet ~$0.12/GB; Supabase egress $0.09/GB uncached, $0.03/GB cached. => IMPLICATION: Pin the Supabase project to ap-south-1 and all GCP services to asia-south1. AWS-Mumbai <-> GCP-Mumbai is a short hop but every byte is billed on the egress side of each cloud. Design GCP workers to pull compact result sets and push aggregates, never to stream raw answer rows across clouds. Do NOT put question images in GCS if they are also in Supabase Storage — pick one origin.
- (high) Storage, Smart CDN and image transformations for question images: Smart CDN caches assets including signed URLs, and auto-invalidates on update/delete (including transformed variants), with up to 60s global propagation. Image transformation happens at the CDN edge with automatic format negotiation (JPEG to WebP for Chrome). Billing: charged per DISTINCT ORIGIN image transformed per billing period, unlimited transformations per origin image; 100 origin images included on Pro/Team; $5 per 1,000 origin images beyond, billed in whole packages (1,001 images = $10). Max file size: 50 MB on Free, up to 500 GB configurable on Pro+ (global setting, per-bucket can be lower). Egress: Storage counts toward the $0.09/GB uncached / $0.03/GB cached buckets, 250 GB each included on Pro. => IMPLICATION: A question bank of 50,000 images costs ~$250/mo in transformation fees at $5/1,000 origin images — non-trivial. Mitigate by pre-generating 2–3 fixed widths at upload time into a derived bucket (one-time transformation cost) rather than transforming on read, and by serving via signed URLs with long expiry so Smart CDN cache hits dominate ($0.03/GB not $0.09/GB). Also cache aggressively in the RN client (expo-image disk cache) — offline-capable question images are a product win and an egress win.
- (high) Full 2026 Supabase pricing sheet at this scale: Pro $25/mo including $10 compute credits; Team $599/mo including $10 credits; Enterprise custom. MAU: 50,000 included on Free, 100,000 on Pro/Team, overage $0.00325/MAU (counted as distinct users who log in or refresh a token in the cycle). DB storage: 500 MB Free / 8 GB Pro, overage $0.125/GB/mo. Egress: 5 GB Free / 250 GB Pro-Team uncached AND a separate 250 GB cached quota; $0.09/GB uncached, $0.03/GB cached; egress is charged for Database (PostgREST + Supavisor), Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime and Log Drains. Realtime messages 5M included Pro/Team, $2.50/1M over. Realtime peak connections 500 included, $10/1,000 over. Image transformations 100 origin images, $5/1,000 over. PITR $100/mo per 7-day window. Compute after the $10 credit: Small $5, Medium $50, Large $100, XL $200, 2XL $400, 4XL ~$950. => IMPLICATION: Realistic monthly bill for a 10k-concurrent exam product on Pro: $25 base + $400–950 compute + ~$100 PITR + ~$95 realtime peak-connection overage + egress. If egress runs 1.5 TB/mo (question images + API payloads) that adds roughly $110–170. Estimate $700–1,400/mo. Team ($599) only becomes worth it for SOC2/SSO/priority support, not for quota headroom.
- (medium) Answer-row storage is cheap — the schema decision should be driven by query shape, not cost: Derived estimate (unverified arithmetic, not from a source): a narrow answer row (attempt_id bigint, question_id bigint, chosen_option smallint, is_marked bool, time_spent_ms int, answered_at timestamptz, plus 24-byte tuple header) is ~70–90 bytes on disk, ~120–150 bytes with alignment and a PK btree entry. One JEE Main sitting = 10,000 students x 180 questions = 1.8M rows, roughly 250–350 MB including indexes. 100 test events/year at that scale = 180M rows, roughly 25–35 GB, about $3–4.50/mo at $0.125/GB/mo. => IMPLICATION: Do not choose a JSONB blob per attempt to 'save space' — the saving is worth single-digit dollars. Choose one row per answer because it gives you per-question analytics, per-topic difficulty calibration, partial-submit durability, and cheap idempotent upserts. Use JSONB only as a denormalised snapshot column on the attempt row for fast 'resume test' reads.
- (high) Partitioning: declarative RANGE + pg_partman, with the composite-PK constraint: Supabase supports Postgres native declarative partitioning and ships pg_partman. Pattern: `CREATE TABLE public.responses (id bigint, answered_at timestamptz not null, ..., primary key (answered_at, id)) PARTITION BY RANGE (answered_at)`, then `create_parent()` with control column, type 'range', interval and start date — create_parent takes a brief ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock. Hard constraint: 'the partitioning column must be included in any unique index (including the primary key)'. For large existing tables, copy in batches by time range during a maintenance window. pg_partman maintenance must be scheduled (via pg_cron / Supabase Cron) so future partitions are pre-created and retention applied. Reported benefit: 5–20x faster queries via partition pruning. => IMPLICATION: Partition `responses` by RANGE on answered_at monthly (or by test_id list-partition if tests are the dominant access pattern). Your idempotency unique key must therefore include the partition column, e.g. (answered_at, attempt_id, question_id), or you partition on a column already in the key. Schedule `partman.run_maintenance_proc()` on pg_cron hourly and pre-create at least 3 future partitions before any big exam — a missing partition means every insert fails mid-exam.
- (medium) BRIN vs BTREE for the responses table: On append-only tables where rows arrive in time-correlated physical order, a BRIN index on the timestamp is 'dozens to hundreds of times smaller' than an equivalent btree — one measurement puts BRIN at ~0.1% of the btree's size — with much lower per-row update cost. BTree wins on small/selective result sets; BRIN pulls ahead as result sets grow (e.g. 1M rows out of 10M–100M). Standard recommendation is a hybrid: BRIN on the timestamp of large cold partitions, BTREE on the identifier columns used for point lookups. => IMPLICATION: On `responses`: BTREE on (attempt_id, question_id) for the hot in-exam read/upsert path (this is the idempotency key anyway), BRIN on answered_at for analytics scans over historic partitions. Do NOT add a BTREE on answered_at — it is pure write amplification during the exam, which is exactly when you are IOPS-constrained. Every extra index on `responses` costs write throughput at 10k-concurrent insert rates.
- (medium) Concurrency: READ COMMITTED + advisory locks + ON CONFLICT beats SERIALIZABLE for scoring: Postgres SSI (SERIALIZABLE) performs comparably to snapshot isolation on read-intensive workloads and far better than 2PL, but under multi-writer contention serialization-failure rates track (w-1)/w for w concurrent writers, and every aborted transaction must be retried by the application — READ COMMITTED achieves the highest commit throughput with no aborts. `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` does not take a row-level lock on the conflicting row, making it substantially cheaper than DO UPDATE for idempotency-key-style writes where you only need the row to exist. Advisory locks (pg_try_advisory_xact_lock) let you serialize on a key of your choosing — e.g. 'score_attempt_<id>' — without locking data, and work when the workflow spans multiple tables or external calls. => IMPLICATION: Concrete design: answer sync = `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (attempt_id, question_id) DO UPDATE SET chosen_option=EXCLUDED.chosen_option WHERE responses.client_seq < EXCLUDED.client_seq` (a monotonic client sequence number makes late/duplicate/out-of-order syncs safe). Scoring = READ COMMITTED transaction guarded by `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock(hashtext('score:'||attempt_id))`; if the lock is not acquired the job is already running — return early, do not retry-storm. Reserve SERIALIZABLE for the coins/rewards ledger where double-spend matters, wrapped in an explicit retry loop on SQLSTATE 40001.
- (high) PostgREST returns max 1,000 rows by default — silent truncation, no error: Supabase projects cap Data API responses at 1,000 rows by default (Dashboard > Integrations > Data API > Settings > Max rows). Requests exceeding it are silently truncated — no exception is thrown. Reported real-world failure mode: 'Pages render with missing data silently.' Supabase recommends keeping the value low to bound payload size from accidental or malicious requests. Use .range() for pagination. => IMPLICATION: A 180-question paper fits, but 'fetch all my past attempts', 'admin exports all responses for a test' (1.8M rows) and leaderboard queries will silently truncate. Keep max-rows at 1,000, force .range() pagination everywhere, and route admin bulk export through an Edge Function/Cloud Run job that paginates server-side and streams a CSV to Storage.
- (medium) Documented 1k to 10k failure modes on Supabase (May 2026 field report): Reported breakages with triggers: (1) RLS quadratic — hits at ~100k rows in busy tables, typically crossed before 10k MAU; fixes measured 179ms to 9ms and 11,000ms to 10ms. (2) Direct connection pool saturation — Micro has 60 direct connections while a serverless front end can autoscale to 30,000 concurrent executions; 'looks like a database outage in logs during lunch-hour peaks'; fix is Supavisor 6543. (3) 1,000-row PostgREST ceiling with no error. (4) Cache-hit-ratio collapse when the working set exceeds shared_buffers — 'latency doubles, then triples' with no slow-query signature; threshold is cache hit ratio dropping below 99%. (5) Background jobs on the request path — webhooks/emails run inside user-facing functions, long jobs stall short ones. Reported fixes: materialized views 12s to 200ms and 8.4s to 180ms; composite indexes 4.2s to 48ms. => IMPLICATION: Add a cache-hit-ratio alert (pg_statio_user_tables, alert below 99%) — this is the failure mode with no obvious signal. Pre-compute leaderboards and analytics dashboards as materialized views refreshed by pg_cron, never as live aggregate queries during a test. Move every non-user-facing job (scoring, notification fan-out, streak recomputation) off the request path into pgmq/Cloud Tasks before the first 10k test.
- (high) Read replicas: available but useless for Auth/Storage/Realtime: Read replicas: 'Requests to other Supabase products, such as Auth, Storage, and Realtime, aren't able to use a Read Replica or its API endpoint' and 'all Auth requests are handled by the Primary, even when sent over the load balancer endpoint.' Since 2025-04-04 the load balancer uses geo-routing to the closest available database rather than round-robin; GET requests can go to replicas, non-GET always goes to Primary. Replication lag exists and is dashboard-monitored. Supabase's own guidance: reach for replicas 'after you reach the largest compute size of 16XL or earlier if you need workload isolation or geographic distribution'. => IMPLICATION: Do not buy replicas to solve exam-time load — writes and Auth (the 10k-login burst) still land on the primary. Replicas are worth it only for isolating heavy admin analytics queries away from the exam-serving primary, or if you later serve students outside India. Replication lag also makes them wrong for 'show me the answer I just saved'.
- (high) GCP async layer: Cloud Tasks vs Pub/Sub numbers: Cloud Tasks: max 500 dispatches/sec per queue, max 5,000 concurrent tasks per queue, 1 MiB per task including headers/metadata, ~$0.40 per million operations ($40/billion), supports per-queue rate limiting, scheduled/delayed delivery and task-level dedup. Pub/Sub: 10 MB max message, priced on monthly data volume, no per-queue dispatch-rate control on push subscriptions, better for fan-out to multiple consumers. Cloud Run: default concurrency 80, configurable to 1,000 per instance; default revision max instances 100; request timeout default 300s, max 3,600s; free tier 180,000 vCPU-s + 360,000 GiB-s + 2M requests/mo; Tier 1 rates $0.000024/vCPU-s, $0.0000025/GiB-s, $0.40/M requests; asia-south1 is Tier 1. => IMPLICATION: Use Cloud Tasks (not Pub/Sub) for post-exam scoring: you need exactly the per-queue dispatch throttle to avoid 10,000 scoring jobs stampeding Postgres. Set max dispatches/sec to ~50 and max concurrent to ~20, matched to your Supavisor pool size. 1.8M answer rows will not fit in a 1 MiB task — pass attempt_id references, never payloads. Alternative with zero extra infra: Supabase Queues (pgmq) + Supabase Cron (pg_cron) + Edge Functions, which keeps everything in-database and avoids cross-cloud egress entirely.
- (high) Push notifications: FCM v1 is mandatory for RN/Expo Android; Expo push caps at 600/sec: Legacy FCM API deprecated 2023-06-20, removed 2024-06-21 — FCM HTTP v1 credentials are required for Android. Expo Push Service: hard throughput limit of 600 notifications/second per project, max 100 notifications per request; free with no per-notification fee and no volume cap; expo-server-sdk-node handles chunking and throttling. From Expo SDK 53 onward push notifications do not work in Expo Go on Android — a development build is required. => IMPLICATION: 600/sec means a broadcast to 10,000 students takes ~17 seconds minimum via Expo Push — acceptable for 'results are out' but not for a synchronised 'test starts now' signal. For time-critical exam-start signalling use Realtime Broadcast (in-app) with push as fallback. If you outgrow Expo Push, go direct to FCM v1 (Android) + APNs (iOS) from a Cloud Run worker; you need the FCM service account key regardless.
- (medium) BigQuery and Memorystore costs for the analytics/rewards layer: BigQuery 2026: 1 TiB queries + 10 GiB storage free per month; on-demand $6.25/TiB scanned; capacity/slot pricing $0.04–$0.10 per slot-hour; active storage $0.02/GB/mo, long-term (untouched 90+ days) $0.01/GB/mo. Memorystore for Redis Cluster is available in asia-south1 (Mumbai) and bills per GiB-hour of provisioned capacity; Basic M1 tier (1–4 GiB) around $0.049/GiB-hr (region-specific rates require the pricing calculator). Committed use discounts are available for Memorystore Redis Cluster. => IMPLICATION: 180M answer rows is roughly 25–35 GB in BigQuery, about $0.50–0.70/mo storage. The risk is on-demand scan cost: a naive full-table scan costs ~$0.20 per run, but an unpartitioned dashboard hitting it hourly is ~$150/mo. Partition and cluster the BigQuery table by exam date + subject and always use partition filters. For Redis, a 4 GiB Memorystore node at ~$0.049/GiB-hr is roughly $140/mo — compare against Upstash serverless Redis, likely cheaper for bursty exam-window leaderboard traffic.
- (medium) Self-hosting Supabase on GCP: not worth it below ~$500–1,000/mo of managed spend: A production workload at 32 GB RAM / 100 GB storage runs $200–400/mo on Supabase Cloud; comparable bare metal (e.g. Hetzner Ryzen 5 3600, 64 GB RAM, 2x NVMe) is ~39 EUR/mo, with GCP roughly in between. But self-hosted Supabase requires monthly updates with service restarts and brief downtime, whereas Cloud updates are automatic zero-downtime. Field guidance: migrate off managed when the bill 'consistently exceeds $500–$1,000/mo dominated by compute (not features)', when the workload is heavy OLAP or sustained high-write, or under regulatory constraints; raw VM alternatives cost ~$150–200/mo but add 5–10 hours/month of DevOps. => IMPLICATION: For a JEE/NEET app the answer is clearly managed Supabase for now: self-hosting forfeits PITR, automatic zero-downtime upgrades, Supavisor HA, Smart CDN and the Realtime cluster — and you would own a Postgres failover runbook during a live exam. Revisit only if compute alone exceeds ~$1,000/mo. If data-residency pressure appears (Indian DPDP Act), note ap-south-1 already keeps data in India.
- (high) JWT signing keys moved to asymmetric — affects how GCP services verify tokens: Asymmetric JWT support shipped as opt-in in 2025; all NEW Supabase projects use asymmetric JWTs by default from 2025-10-01, with full transition of all projects expected by late 2026. Default algorithm RS256, optionally ECC (ES256, more performant) or Ed25519. Public keys are exposed via a JWKS endpoint; rotated public keys remain in the JWKS so existing JWTs stay verifiable. Benefit: no shared secret, and verification is local — no network call to Supabase Auth via getUser(). => IMPLICATION: Cloud Run scoring/admin services should verify student and admin JWTs locally against the Supabase JWKS endpoint (cache the JWKS, respect kid rotation) rather than calling getUser() per request — that removes a network round-trip and an Auth rate-limit surface from every GCP-side request. Confirm the project is on asymmetric keys before writing verification code; an older project may still be on HS256.
- (high) Postgres-level knobs Supabase exposes and the ones you must set: Customisable via CLI/SQL: max_connections (CLI only, requires DB restart), work_mem (CLI + SQL, no restart), session_replication_role (CLI only), statement_timeout (CLI + SQL, e.g. `alter database "postgres" set "statement_timeout" TO '60s';`). Command form: `supabase postgres-config update --config <param>=<value> --project-ref <ref> --experimental`. Warning from docs: 'CLI changes permanently overwrite default settings' — reset commands will not restore originals; 'Inappropriate settings can lead to severe performance degradation or project instability.' PITR is recommended once the database exceeds 4 GB. Load testing with k6 is Supabase's own recommendation in the production checklist. OTP expiry should be set to 3600s (1 hour) or lower. => IMPLICATION: Set a per-role statement_timeout: short for `authenticated` (e.g. 8s — a student query should never run longer) and longer for `service_role` (e.g. 120s for scoring/export). This is your primary defence against one pathological query pinning a connection during an exam. Do NOT raise max_connections to 'fix' pooling — RAM per backend is the real limit and you will OOM the instance. Run a k6 test simulating 10,000 virtual users doing login, fetch paper, 180 answer syncs, submit — against a staging project at the same compute tier, before the first real test.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- Write an RLS style guide and enforce it in CI: every policy must (a) name a role with TO authenticated / TO anon, (b) wrap every function call in a scalar subquery — (select auth.uid()), (select public.authorize('x')) — (c) reference only columns that carry a btree index, and (d) push any cross-table check into a `SECURITY DEFINER ... STABLE ... SET search_path = ''` helper rather than a join inside the policy. Add a migration test that runs Supabase's Security Advisor / splinter and fails on lint 0015 (RLS references user_metadata) and on any table with RLS disabled in the public schema.
- Implement RBAC exactly as Supabase's official pattern: enums app_role ('admin','student') and app_permission; server-owned public.user_roles and public.role_permissions; a custom_access_token_hook injecting user_role into the JWT; grants to supabase_auth_admin with explicit REVOKE from authenticated/anon/public. Never read role from user_metadata. For 'admin bypasses RLS', do NOT ship the service_role key to any client — instead add a permissive policy `USING ((select public.authorize('questions.write')))` scoped to the admin role, or expose admin write paths only through Edge Functions / Cloud Run holding the service key server-side.
- Keep 100% of student traffic on the HTTP surfaces (PostgREST Data API, GoTrue, Storage, Realtime) so 10k concurrent students never map to 10k Postgres connections. Use Supavisor transaction mode (port 6543) only for your own GCP workers, disable prepared statements in those drivers (Prisma `?pgbouncer=true`, asyncpg `statement_cache_size=0`, JDBC `prepareThreshold=0`), and size each worker's client pool so total workers x pool <= the project Pool Size. Provision 2XL as the baseline and pre-scale to 4XL (480 direct connections, 3,000 pooler clients, 20,000 IOPS) for scheduled exam windows, then scale back.
- Model exam writes as one narrow row per answer in `responses`, RANGE-partitioned monthly on answered_at via pg_partman with maintenance on pg_cron and at least 3 future partitions pre-created. Index only what the hot path needs: BTREE on (attempt_id, question_id) — which doubles as the idempotency key — and BRIN on answered_at for analytics. Client batches answer syncs every 10–30s (and on every navigation away) sending a monotonic client_seq; server does `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (attempt_id, question_id) DO UPDATE ... WHERE responses.client_seq < EXCLUDED.client_seq` so retries, duplicates and out-of-order arrivals are all safe. Score under READ COMMITTED guarded by pg_try_advisory_xact_lock; reserve SERIALIZABLE with an explicit 40001 retry loop for the coins/rewards ledger only.
- Build leaderboards and live counters on Realtime Broadcast plus Redis, never on postgres_changes (single-threaded, ~4,000 msgs/sec ceiling with RLS). Before any 10k-student test: disable the Pro spend cap to lift peak connections from 500 to 10,000, and add client-side random jitter (0–60s) to channel joins because joins/sec is capped at 2,500. Budget ~$95/mo of peak-connection overage at 10k.
- Harden Auth before launch: configure custom SMTP (the built-in provider is 2 emails/hour project-wide), wire an Indian DLT-registered SMS provider (MSG91 ~₹0.15 vs Twilio ~₹0.45–0.63) through the Send SMS Hook, start DLT registration early (~₹11,800, multi-week lead time), raise the configurable project-wide OTP/hour limit well above the 30 default, set OTP expiry <=3600s, and enable Cloudflare Turnstile or hCaptcha on signup/sign-in/password-reset. Explicitly test the CGNAT/coaching-centre scenario: the non-configurable per-IP limits (360 verifies/hr, 1,800 token refreshes/hr) can throttle a whole classroom on one IP.
- Keep GCP narrow and co-located in asia-south1 (Tier 1 Cloud Run pricing) against a Supabase project in ap-south-1: FCM v1 for push (mandatory for Expo/Android anyway; Expo Push caps at 600/sec so use Realtime Broadcast for exam-start signalling), Cloud Run + Cloud Tasks for async scoring with the per-queue dispatch rate throttled to match your Supavisor pool (e.g. 50/sec, 20 concurrent), and BigQuery partitioned+clustered by exam date for analytics. Verify JWTs locally against the Supabase JWKS endpoint rather than calling getUser() per request. Do NOT split object storage across GCS and Supabase Storage — one origin only, to avoid paying cross-cloud egress on question images.
- Instrument the two failure modes that have no obvious signal: (a) alert when the buffer cache hit ratio drops below 99% (pg_statio_user_tables) — this is what makes latency 'double, then triple' with no slow-query entry; (b) alert on Supavisor client-connection saturation and on pg_stat_activity wait events. Set a per-role statement_timeout (authenticated ~8s, service_role ~120s) so one pathological query cannot pin a backend mid-exam. Run a k6 load test at the target compute tier simulating 10,000 VUs doing login, fetch paper, 180 answer syncs, submit — before the first real test.
- Precompute rather than aggregate live: materialized views refreshed by pg_cron for leaderboards, chapter-wise accuracy, and admin dashboards (reported gains 12s to 200ms and 8.4s to 180ms). Keep PostgREST max-rows at 1,000 and force .range() pagination everywhere; route admin bulk exports (1.8M rows per test) through a paginating Cloud Run job that streams CSV into Storage rather than through the Data API.
- Pre-generate 2–3 fixed image widths at upload time into a derived bucket instead of transforming on read — the transformation bill is $5 per 1,000 distinct origin images per billing period (only 100 included), so a 50,000-image bank transformed on read costs ~$250/mo. Serve via long-expiry signed URLs so Smart CDN cache hits dominate ($0.03/GB cached vs $0.09/GB uncached), and cache aggressively on-device with expo-image for offline practice.
- Stay on managed Supabase. Self-hosting on GCP only pencils out above roughly $500–1,000/mo of compute-dominated spend, and it costs you PITR, zero-downtime upgrades, Supavisor HA and Smart CDN — plus you would own a Postgres failover runbook during a live exam. Enable PITR ($100/mo per 7-day window) as soon as the DB exceeds 4 GB, and do not buy read replicas to solve exam load (writes and all Auth still hit the primary); use them only to isolate heavy admin analytics.
PITFALLS:
- Storing the ADMIN role in user_metadata. Any client can call supabase.auth.updateUser({ data: { is_admin: true } }) and escalate. Supabase's own splinter lint 0015 flags this as ERROR. Role must come from a server-owned table projected into the JWT by the custom access token hook, or from app_metadata written only via the Admin API.
- Bare auth.uid() / helper functions inside RLS policies. Re-evaluated once per row: measured 171ms to <0.1ms, 11,000ms to 10ms, and 178,000ms to 12ms after wrapping in (select ...). Also: unindexed RLS predicate columns and unindexed foreign keys (Postgres does not auto-index FKs) — a 1M-row team policy went from >2 minutes to 2ms once indexed and wrapped. This does not show up in dev with 1k rows.
- Prepared statements against Supavisor transaction mode (port 6543). Docs are explicit that transaction mode does not support them. Prisma without ?pgbouncer=true, or asyncpg/JDBC without statement_cache_size=0 / prepareThreshold=0, produce intermittent 'prepared statement already exists' errors that appear ONLY under concurrency — i.e. during the live exam, not in testing.
- Building live leaderboards or 'students online' on Realtime postgres_changes. Changes are processed on a single thread to preserve order and RLS-filtered configurations top out around 4,000 msgs/sec regardless of compute size; a Large-to-16XL DB is documented at only 0.05 changes/sec sustained for 200k clients. Broadcast does 224,000 msgs/sec at 32k clients. Wrong primitive means the leaderboard silently lags then stalls.
- Launching on Pro with the spend cap ON. That pins Realtime to 500 peak connections and 500 channel joins/sec — 10,000 students joining a scheduled test in the first minute will get too_many_connections / too_many_joins. Turning the cap off raises it to 10,000 connections and 2,500 joins/sec, but a 10,000-student simultaneous join still needs client-side jitter to stay under 2,500/sec.
- The built-in email provider's 2 emails per hour, project-wide. Not a per-user limit. Without custom SMTP a signup campaign delivers essentially zero confirmation emails. Separately, per-IP limits that CANNOT be raised (360 verifies/hr, 1,800 token refreshes/hr, 15 MFA/hr, 30 anonymous signups/hr) will throttle an entire coaching centre or CGNAT-shared carrier IP — a very real scenario in India.
- PostgREST's silent 1,000-row cap. No exception is raised; pages simply render with missing data. Hits 'all my past attempts', leaderboards, and any admin export. Every list query needs explicit .range() pagination and an integration test asserting counts past 1,000.
- Doing CPU-bound scoring inside an Edge Function. Hard limits are 256 MB memory and 2 seconds of actual CPU time per request (async I/O excluded); wall clock is 400s on paid plans but the CPU limit bites first. Scoring 180 questions x 10,000 attempts belongs in a Postgres function or Cloud Run (60-min timeout), not an Edge Function.
- Missing pg_partman future partitions. If maintenance is not scheduled on pg_cron and the next period's partition does not exist, every INSERT into `responses` fails — mid-exam, for every student, simultaneously. Also remember the native-partitioning constraint: the partition key must be part of every unique index including the primary key, which changes the shape of your idempotency key.
- Reaching for SERIALIZABLE to make scoring 'safe'. Under w concurrent writers serialization-failure rates track (w-1)/w and every abort must be retried in application code; with 10,000 concurrent submits you get a retry storm. READ COMMITTED plus a per-attempt advisory lock plus ON CONFLICT gives correctness without the abort rate. Note ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE takes a row lock; DO NOTHING does not.
- Assuming read replicas fix exam-time load. Auth requests always go to the primary even through the load balancer, Storage and Realtime cannot use replicas at all, and all writes go to the primary. Replication lag also breaks read-your-own-write ('where is the answer I just saved?').
- Cross-cloud data movement between Supabase (AWS ap-south-1) and GCP (asia-south1). Supabase is AWS-only. Every byte pulled out of Supabase is billed at $0.09/GB uncached, and Cloud Run internet egress is ~$0.12/GB. Streaming raw answer rows to a GCP worker for scoring, or mirroring question images into GCS, converts a free in-database operation into a recurring bandwidth bill — and adds a network partition to your exam critical path.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- Exact Supabase pooler behaviour for the Data API path at 10k concurrent: how many Postgres backends does PostgREST hold per compute tier, and is that db-pool value tunable on the platform? Not documented publicly — needs a support ticket or an empirical pg_stat_activity measurement under k6 load.
- Does Supabase apply a request-rate limit (not just Auth rate limits) on the PostgREST Data API per project or per IP? No published number found; 10k students each firing an answer-sync every 10s is ~1,000 req/s sustained plus burst, and it is unclear whether anything upstream of Postgres throttles that.
- Actual sustained insert throughput of the partitioned responses table with RLS enabled on a 2XL vs 4XL. All published Supabase numbers are Realtime or pooler benchmarks; the write path with RLS + partition routing + BTREE upsert has no published figure and must be measured.
- Real cold-start and p99 latency of Supabase Edge Functions from Indian mobile networks against an ap-south-1 project in 2026. The 42ms average / 460ms p99 figures come from a July 2025 post with unspecified region; Indian 4G RTT will dominate and needs measurement from real devices.
- Whether Supabase Auth's per-IP limits (360 verify/hr, 1,800 refresh/hr) can be raised for an enterprise contract or via an IP allowlist. Docs say 'not customizable'; whether Enterprise gets an exception is unknown and is a material risk for coaching-centre deployments.
- Exact asia-south1 (Mumbai) pricing for Memorystore for Redis Cluster per GiB-hour — Google publishes per-region rates only via the calculator. Also whether Upstash serverless Redis is cheaper for the bursty exam-window leaderboard pattern than a provisioned Memorystore node.
- Whether Supabase Queues (pgmq) can sustain the enqueue rate for 10,000 scoring jobs submitted within a few minutes, and whether unlogged queues (faster writes, no crash durability) are acceptable for scoring jobs. No published throughput benchmark found.
- Whether the spend-cap-off Realtime limit of 10,000 peak connections is a hard ceiling or soft; docs say 'contact support for customization on paid plans' but give no achievable number, which matters if you exceed 10,000 simultaneous test-takers.
- Indian DPDP Act implications of routing student data through GCP asia-south1 and AWS ap-south-1 (both in-country) versus Supabase's control-plane and log storage locations, which are not documented per-region.
- Whether the project is already on asymmetric JWT signing keys (RS256/ES256 + JWKS) or still on the legacy shared HS256 secret — this changes how GCP-side services must verify tokens and whether getUser() network calls can be eliminated.

## react-native-math
SUMMARY: Verified against the npm registry and GitHub APIs on 2026-07-25. The React Native client baseline for this app is RN 0.86 / Expo SDK 57 (released 2026-06-30), New-Architecture-only — the legacy Paper renderer was deleted in RN 0.82, so every library choice must be Fabric-native. Expo SDK 57 pins react-native-webview 13.16.1, @shopify/flash-list 2.0.2, react-native-svg 15.15.4, react-native-reanimated 4.5.0, @shopify/react-native-skia 2.6.2, expo-sqlite ~57.0.1. For math, every "native" RN math library is dead: react-native-math-view is ARCHIVED (94 stars, last push 2024-09-05, v3.9.5 from 2021), react-native-mathjax-html-to-svg last published 2023, react-native-katex is a 52-star hobby wrapper. The realistic 2026 architecture is: author in LaTeX, pre-render server-side with KaTeX 0.18.1 (measured 0.103 ms/expression, 4.5 KB HTML / 829 B gzip for a typical JEE integral; 652 B for MathML-only), store LaTeX source as the canonical column plus a cached render, and display with exactly ONE WebView per screen that renders the whole question — never one WebView per FlatList row, since WebViews cost 150–200 MB each and are throttled to ~3 concurrent instances in production micro-frontend systems. MathML is now "widely available" in both WKWebView and Android WebView (Chrome 109+, iOS Safari 5+), which makes shipping KaTeX's MathML-only output (205–484 B gzipped) a genuinely viable ultra-light path, though Chromium's MathML Core is still incomplete for complex layouts. Chemistry is solved by KaTeX's mhchem contrib (33.7 KB / 8.4 KB gzip) for \ce{} equations and reaction arrows; skeletal structure drawing needs smiles-drawer (197 KB min / 59 KB gzip) because RDKit.js ships a 6.6 MB WASM binary and entered "maintenance transition" on 2026-04-07. On the admin side TipTap 3.29.0 with the now-MIT @tiptap/extension-mathematics plus MathLive 0.110.0 as the virtual math keyboard is the strongest pairing — but note extension-mathematics still peer-deps katex ^0.16 || ^0.17, not the 0.18 line. Offline-first: PowerSync @powersync/react-native hit 2.0.0 on 2026-07-21 and is the documented, server-authoritative Supabase pairing (free tier 2 GB synced/month, 50 concurrent connections — far too small for 10k concurrent students; Pro is $49/mo for 1,000 connections + $30 per additional 1,000). WatermelonDB is effectively stalled (last push 2025-08-11). Motion should be Reanimated 4.5.x CSS animations with Material 3 tokens (150–400 ms, emphasized-decelerate cubic-bezier(0.05,0.7,0.1,1)), Skia canvases forced to SurfaceView via opaque, and a hard cap of ~100 simultaneously animated components on low-end Android.
FINDINGS:
- (high) react-native-math-view is archived and dead — do not use: GitHub repo ShaMan123/react-native-math-view is ARCHIVED (verified via GitHub API 2026-07-25): 94 stars, last push 2024-09-05, 10 open issues. npm latest is 3.9.5 published 2021-08-28 — nearly 5 years stale. It predates Fabric/New Architecture entirely and cannot work on RN 0.82+ where the legacy bridge was removed. => IMPLICATION: Rule it out at architecture time. Any tutorial or LLM suggestion recommending it is >2 years out of date. Same for react-native-mathjax-html-to-svg (1.1.0, 2023-06-30), react-native-mathjax-text-svg (1.0.14, 2023-11-30), react-native-mathjax-svg (0.9.9, 2021), react-native-latex (0.5.0, 2020).
- (high) There is no native (non-WebView) LaTeX renderer for React Native in 2026; it is an open feature request: software-mansion/react-native-enriched-markdown issue #109 (opened 2026-02-25) and react-native-enriched issue #439 both request native math via SwiftMath (iOS) and AndroidMath (Kotlin port of iosMath). Both are open, unassigned enhancements. The issue text itself states developers must 'rely on complex workarounds like embedding WebViews with MathJax/KaTeX or using SVG-based libraries'. => IMPLICATION: Plan for a WebView-based or pre-rendered-image renderer. Do not budget engineering time waiting for a native RN math view to land; treat SwiftMath/AndroidMath bridging as a >4-week custom native-module project if you ever want it.
- (high) KaTeX 0.18.1 (2026-07-19) is current; 0.18.0 introduced a BREAKING CSS class-prefix change: npm registry: katex latest = 0.18.1 published 2026-07-19T08:40:11Z. 0.17.0 (2026-05-22) changed the internal __defineFunction API (no more props wrapper). 0.18.0 (2026-07-17) added CSS class prefixing for KaTeX elements — 'users who apply custom styles or have allowlists targeting KaTeX's internal classes must update their selectors.' GitHub: KaTeX/KaTeX 20,258 stars, last push 2026-07-23, MIT. => IMPLICATION: Pin katex exactly and version the CSS you ship into the WebView alongside it. If you write custom .katex-* CSS overrides for dark mode / font sizing, 0.18 will silently break them. Also blocks a straight upgrade because tooling still peer-deps 0.16/0.17.
- (high) Measured: KaTeX server-side render is 0.103 ms/expression; output is 0.8–1.7 KB gzipped: Benchmarked locally with katex@0.18.1 on Node 22.21.0 (1000 iterations). Per-expression renderToString = 0.103 ms. Output sizes (HTML+MathML, displayMode, raw / gzip): x^2+y^2=z^2 → 2,313 B / 481 B; JEE definite integral \int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin^3 x}{\sin^3 x+\cos^3 x}dx → 4,510 B / 829 B; 3x3 determinant → 5,635 B / 921 B; Maxwell/Lorentz-force physics line → 14,096 B / 1,658 B; \ce{2H2 + O2 -> 2H2O} → 6,212 B / 1,166 B; \ce{CH3COOH + H2O <=> CH3COO^- + H3O^+} → 9,935 B / 1,298 B. => IMPLICATION: Pre-rendering the entire question bank server-side is cheap: 100,000 expressions ≈ 10 seconds of CPU. A 90-question JEE Main paper with ~3 expressions each ≈ 270 renders ≈ 28 ms server-side, ~250 KB gzipped over the wire. Do it in a Supabase Edge Function on question INSERT/UPDATE and cache the HTML in a column.
- (medium) KaTeX MathML-only output is 3–8x smaller than HTML+MathML and MathML is now widely available in both WebViews: Measured with output:'mathml': simple 319 B (205 B gz), JEE integral 652 B (325 B gz), determinant 1,172 B (372 B gz), physics 1,134 B (484 B gz), \ce{} equilibrium 1,636 B (415 B gz). caniwebview.com (data updated 2026-07-18, W3C WebView CG) marks MathML 'Widely available': WKWebView supported, Android WebView supported, Chrome for Android 109+, iOS Safari 5+. => IMPLICATION: MathML-only is the lightest possible payload and needs zero KaTeX CSS/fonts in the client. BUT Chromium ships only MathML Core (limited) vs Firefox's full support, so complex JEE Advanced layouts (matrices, multi-line aligned, stretchy \ce arrows) may render inconsistently on Android. Recommended use: MathML as a fallback/accessibility layer and for search indexing, HTML+CSS KaTeX as the primary visual path.
- (high) MathJax 4.1.3 SVG output is ~2.5x larger and ~11x slower than KaTeX HTML: Benchmarked mathjax-full tex2svg with fontCache:'local' (200 iterations): 1.14 ms per expression vs KaTeX's 0.103 ms. SVG output sizes (raw / gzip): simple 4,408 B / 1,910 B; JEE integral 10,677 B / 3,936 B; determinant 8,323 B / 3,131 B; physics line 18,300 B / 6,432 B; \ce{2H2+O2->2H2O} 5,543 B / 1,586 B. npm mathjax latest = 4.1.3 (2026-07-03); MathJax 4.0.0 shipped 2025-08-04 and added a dedicated mhchem font so stretchy reaction arrows finally render correctly in both CHTML and SVG. => IMPLICATION: Use MathJax only where you specifically need SVG (e.g. rendering a question into a PNG/PDF for sharing, or into react-native-svg without any WebView). For the in-app reader, KaTeX wins on every axis. MathJax's mhchem font fix is the one reason to prefer it for very complex chemistry arrows.
- (medium) The FlatList-of-50-WebViews problem: each WebView costs 150–200 MB; production systems cap at ~3 live instances: Documented production data: 'crash reporting often reveals WebViews consuming 150-200MB each', mitigated by a WebViewManager enforcing a default limit of 3 concurrent WebViews with LRU eviction, yielding 35–45% memory reduction per WebView and >90% reduction in crash/white-screen incidents. Instantiating a WebView is described as 'an exceptionally heavy operation'. Separately, react-native-webview issue #3168 documents an Android memory leak where changing onMessage adds a BatchedBridge callback that is never released. => IMPLICATION: Never render one WebView per question row. On a 4 GB Android device (the 2026 India entry-level baseline), 5 live WebViews will OOM. The architecture must be: one WebView per visible screen, or zero WebViews with pre-rendered images.
- (medium) Expo DOM Components ('use dom') are a WebView, and they do NOT support OTA updates: Expo SDK 57 bundles @expo/dom-webview ~57.0.1. Docs: the 'use dom' directive renders the component in an embedded WebView communicating over an async JSON bridge; only serializable props cross; function props must be async; children cannot be passed; native views cannot be embedded inside; 'DOM components only support standard JavaScript, which is slower to parse and start up than optimized Hermes bytecode'; explicitly 'currently don't support over-the-air updates'. Expo recommends them for 'rich-text rendering, markdown, or auxiliary routes — not as a replacement for native views'. => IMPLICATION: DOM components are the ergonomically nicest way to embed a KaTeX renderer (you write real React DOM + katex), and the docs literally name rich-text rendering as the use case. But if question-bank hotfixes must ship via EAS Update, the no-OTA limitation is disqualifying for the core reader. Verify against SDK 57 release notes before committing; if confirmed, use a plain react-native-webview with an HTML template you can OTA.
- (high) A fully offline, self-contained KaTeX WebView asset bundle is 556 KB raw / 643 KB with base64-inlined fonts: Measured from the katex@0.18.1 tarball: katex.min.js 271,715 B (76,191 B gzip), katex.min.css 24,727 B (3,605 B gzip), 20 .woff2 fonts totalling 259,792 B (largest: AMS-Regular 28,076 B, Main-Regular 26,272 B, Math-Italic 16,440 B). Full dist/fonts dir (ttf+woff+woff2) is 1.1 MB — ship woff2 only. mhchem.min.js adds 33,706 B (8,425 B gzip). => IMPLICATION: Bundling KaTeX + mhchem as a local asset for a single offline WebView costs ~590 KB of app size (or ~643 KB as one inlined HTML file). That is acceptable and means zero network dependency for math rendering — critical for patchy Indian networks and for offline mock tests. Load via source={{ html, baseUrl }} or an Android asset:// / iOS bundle path; never a CDN.
- (high) mhchem is the correct chemistry-equation solution: 33.7 KB, first-class in both KaTeX and MathJax: KaTeX ships contrib/mhchem/mhchem.min.js (33,706 B; 8,425 B gzip) adding \ce{} and \pu{}. Reaction arrows are native KaTeX extensible arrows since PR #1078, which added \xrightleftarrows, \xrightequilibrium, \xleftequilibrium — user syntax \ce{A<-->B}, \ce{A<=>>B}, \ce{A<<=>B}. Load order matters: katex.min.js, then mhchem.min.js, then auto-render. \cf is removed; only \ce is supported. Verified rendering \ce{[Cu(NH3)4]^{2+} + 4H+ ->[\Delta] Cu^{2+} + 4NH4+} → 10,244 B / 1,384 B gz. => IMPLICATION: All NEET/JEE inorganic and physical chemistry equations, equilibria, oxidation states, subscripts/superscripts and reaction conditions above/below arrows are covered by mhchem with zero extra infrastructure. Give admins a \ce{} snippet button in the editor. No separate chemistry renderer is needed for equations.
- (high) RDKit.js is 6.6 MB of WASM and lost its maintainer in April 2026 — do not ship it in the app: Measured from @rdkit/rdkit@2025.3.4-1.0.0 (published 2025-07-07): RDKit_minimal.wasm = 6,914,823 B (2,058,729 B gzip), RDKit_minimal.js = 128,073 B (30,320 B gzip). README states 'IN MAINTENANCE TRANSITION (April 7th 2026)' — @michelml no longer maintains the repo or npm releases; @greglandrum is seeking a new maintainer. GitHub rdkit/rdkit-js: 241 stars, BSD-3-Clause, 39 open issues. => IMPLICATION: Never bundle RDKit into the mobile client. If you need SMILES → skeletal structure images (organic chemistry mechanisms, IUPAC naming questions), run RDKit server-side (Python RDKit in a GCP Cloud Run job) and store the resulting SVG/PNG in Supabase Storage, served via expo-image. Alternative pure-JS client option: smiles-drawer 2.4.1 (2026-06-26), 197,128 B min / 59,366 B gzip — 35x smaller than RDKit and viable inside the same WebView.
- (medium) Ketcher is the admin-side structure editor, but it is heavy and web-only: ketcher-react 3.17.1 published 2026-07-16; GitHub epam/ketcher 847 stars, last push 2026-07-24, Apache-2.0, but 1,059 open issues. It is a full 2D chemical structure editor for the browser (React), not React Native. => IMPLICATION: Use Ketcher in the ADMIN web console only (authors draw a structure → export MOL/SMILES → server renders to SVG). Do not attempt to embed it in the RN app. Its very large open-issue count argues for treating it as an optional authoring nicety, not a load-bearing dependency.
- (high) MathLive 0.110.0 is the right virtual math keyboard for admin authoring — 843 KB min (227 KB gzip) + 296 KB fonts: npm mathlive latest = 0.110.0 published 2026-06-09. GitHub arnog/mathlive: 2,113 stars, last push 2026-07-24, MIT, 145 open issues. Measured package: mathlive.min.mjs 842,878 B (227,080 B gzip), mathlive-ssr.min.mjs 396,585 B (111,464 B gzip), mathlive-static.css 13,154 B, 20 fonts 296 KB, sounds 240 KB. 800+ LaTeX commands, mobile-ready virtual keyboards, export to LaTeX, MathML, ASCIIMath, Typst, or MathJSON. Companion @cortex-js/compute-engine 0.93.0 (2026-07-23) does symbolic evaluation. => IMPLICATION: MathLive is a web component — it belongs in the admin web console (or an admin-only WebView inside the app). Its LaTeX output feeds directly into the KaTeX pipeline. Its MathJSON export is the ready-made portable AST if you later want structured search/equivalence checking. Do not ship MathLive to students; 227 KB gzip is pure authoring weight.
- (high) TipTap 3.29.0's Mathematics extension is now MIT, but it peer-deps katex ^0.16||^0.17 — not 0.18: npm @tiptap/extension-mathematics@3.29.0 (published 2026-07-24) — license MIT, peerDependencies { katex: '^0.16.4 || ^0.17.0', '@tiptap/core': '3.29.0', '@tiptap/pm': '3.29.0' }. Tiptap open-sourced 10 formerly Pro extensions under MIT (Mathematics among them). GitHub ueberdosis/tiptap: 37,767 stars, last push 2026-07-24, 827 open issues. Compare: lexical 0.48.0 (2026-07-16, facebook/lexical 23,704 stars) has no first-party math extension; slate 0.126.0 (2026-07-09); quill 2.0.3 (2024-11-30, stale). => IMPLICATION: TipTap 3.29 + @tiptap/extension-mathematics + MathLive as the input surface is the recommended admin stack. But you must run katex 0.17.x in the admin editor while the reader may want 0.18.x — either pin both to 0.17.x for now, or accept a version split and keep the SSR renderer and editor on separate katex installs. Quill is effectively frozen; Lexical would require writing your own math node.
- (high) Expo SDK 57 (2026-06-30) pins specific library versions that differ from npm 'latest' — this is the real compatibility matrix: From expo/expo sdk-57 bundledNativeModules.json (123 entries): react-native 0.86.0, react-native-webview 13.16.1 (npm latest is 14.0.1, with 15.0.0 and 16.0.0 already published), @shopify/flash-list 2.0.2 (npm latest 2.3.2), react-native-svg 15.15.4 (latest 15.15.5), react-native-reanimated 4.5.0, react-native-worklets 0.10.0, react-native-gesture-handler ~2.32.0, @shopify/react-native-skia 2.6.2 (latest 2.10.0), expo-sqlite ~57.0.1, expo-image ~57.0.1, expo-router ~57.0.8, expo-updates ~57.0.10, @react-native-async-storage/async-storage 2.2.0. SDK 57 = React 19.2. expo prebuild now clears and regenerates native dirs by default. => IMPLICATION: Use `npx expo install` and accept these pins; do not chase npm latest. Notably react-native-webview is 2 majors behind on Expo — check for the fixes you need before assuming 13.16.1 has them. FlashList 2.0.2 vs 2.3.2 is 8 months of bugfixes you would be forfeiting; consider an explicit override after testing.
- (high) The legacy architecture is gone: RN 0.82 removed the bridge, so all libraries must be Fabric-native: Timeline: New Architecture stable in 0.76 (Oct 2024); legacy architecture frozen June 2025; 0.81/Expo SDK 54 were the last with a legacy interop path; 0.82 is the first version running entirely on the New Architecture; legacy removed from the codebase by 0.83/Expo SDK 55. RN 0.86 (2026-06-11) ships with no user-facing breaking changes vs 0.85; deprecates ViewUtil.getUIManagerType. FlashList v2.x is New-Architecture-only and 'will not run on old architecture'. Reanimated 4.x is New-Architecture-only; Reanimated 3.19.5 (2025-12-22) is the last 3.x and is no longer actively maintained. => IMPLICATION: Any library whose last release predates ~mid-2025 is a compatibility risk by default. This is the single cheapest filter for evaluating the many stale math/chemistry RN packages.
- (high) FlashList v2.3.2 is the list to use — JS-only, no size estimates, but has documented gotchas: npm @shopify/flash-list latest 2.3.2 (2026-06-10). GitHub Shopify/flash-list: 7,149 stars, last push 2026-07-24, MIT, 137 open issues. v2 removed estimatedItemSize/estimatedListSize/estimatedFirstItemOffset; MasonryFlashList replaced by a `masonry` prop; added useLayoutState, useRecyclingState, useMappingHelper, useFlashListContext, maxItemsInRecyclePool, onStartReached, maintainVisibleContentPosition. Shopify claims up to 50% less blank area vs v1. Known issues: maintainVisibleContentPosition is ON by default and causes items to move on data re-order (fix: maintainVisibleContentPosition={{disabled:true}}); horizontal lists ignore contentContainerStyle padding; issue #1966 — infinite render loop ('Exceeded max renders without commit') on specific Android devices (Oppo, Samsung) when nested in a ScrollView; issue #2050 — maintainVisibleContentPosition bug when initial data doesn't fill the screen; dynamic-height items (late-loading images) leave blank space unless useLayoutState is used. => IMPLICATION: For the question-palette / practice-feed screens use FlashList v2 with getItemType keyed on question type (text-only vs has-math vs has-image) so recycling pools stay homogeneous. Explicitly disable maintainVisibleContentPosition on the question list. Never nest it in a ScrollView. Use useLayoutState in any row whose height changes after mount.
- (high) Reanimated 4.5.3 with CSS animations is the animation baseline; Moti is abandoned and Reanimated-3-only: npm react-native-reanimated latest 4.5.3 (2026-07-22); dist-tag legacy 4.3.3; nightly 4.6.0. GitHub software-mansion/react-native-reanimated: 10,928 stars, last push 2026-07-24. Reanimated 4.0 stable shipped mid-2025 with the CSS Animations API (keyframes on Animated.View style props) and CSS Transitions API. Compatibility table: 4.6.x supports RN 0.83–0.86 and requires react-native-worklets 0.12.x; Expo SDK 57 pins reanimated 4.5.0 + worklets 0.10.0. Moti latest is 0.30.0 published 2025-01-29, GitHub nandorojo/moti last push 2025-03-11 — README still says 'powered by Reanimated 3'; issue #391 requests Reanimated 4 support and is open. => IMPLICATION: Do not adopt Moti. Use Reanimated 4's CSS Animations/Transitions API directly — it is more declarative than Moti was, is officially maintained, and is what Expo pins. Restrained motion is easier to express with CSS transitions (transition-property/duration/timing-function) than with imperative springs.
- (high) Hard performance ceiling: animate ≤100 components on low-end Android, ≤500 on iOS: Official Reanimated performance guide: 'animate no more than 100 components simultaneously on low-end Android devices and no more than 500 on iOS'. Prefer transform/opacity/backgroundColor over top/left/width/height (the latter force per-frame layout recalculation). Never read shared values from the JS thread — it blocks JS until sync completes. The docs acknowledge New Architecture animation performance regressions vs Legacy, mitigated by feature flags DISABLE_COMMIT_PAUSING_MECHANISM and USE_COMMIT_HOOK_ONLY_FOR_REACT_COMMITS, RN 0.80+ with Reanimated 4.2.0+, and debugOptimized build variants on Android (RN 0.82+). For complex scenes, use Skia rather than many animated RN components. => IMPLICATION: Concrete budget for the design bar: a leaderboard or question-palette grid must not animate every cell. Animate the container, stagger at most ~20 children, and use a single Skia canvas for anything richer (streak rings, coin bursts, progress arcs).
- (high) Skia on low-end Android: the single biggest fix is forcing SurfaceView via the `opaque` prop: Margelo's Pingo case study on a sub-$100 120 Hz Android device: TextureView compositing meant 'the GPU touches the canvas pixels two or three times every frame' (Skia render → HWUI sample → SurfaceFlinger composite), producing 79% of frames flagged for slow bitmap uploads and 85% janky frames at 120 Hz. Switching the Canvas from TextureView to SurfaceView via the `opaque` prop delivered the overwhelming majority of the improvement (hardware overlay compositing, no per-frame texture upload); RenderThread CPU fell 50–65%. Second cause: usePathValue/usePathInterpolation worklets that re-dirty their own outputs, continuously re-recording an idle canvas — disabling idle canvases cut frame time from 17–32 ms to ~9 ms. Third: store patterns minting fresh objects per poll, invalidating useMemo and forcing Skia re-recordings. npm @shopify/react-native-skia latest 2.10.0 (2026-07-23); GitHub 8,468 stars, last push 2026-07-23. => IMPLICATION: Three mandatory rules for this app: (1) set opaque on every Skia Canvas; (2) unmount or freeze Skia canvases that are off-screen or idle (e.g. the streak ring on a scrolled-away card); (3) never create new object identities per render in the store that feeds Skia. These are the difference between 60 fps and 15 fps on a Redmi/Realme.
- (high) Material 3 motion tokens give exact, restrained values to standardise on: From material-components-android docs/theming/Motion.md — Easing: Standard cubic-bezier(0.2,0,0,1); Standard Decelerate (0,0,0,1); Standard Accelerate (0.3,0,1,1); Emphasized Decelerate (0.05,0.7,0.1,1); Emphasized Accelerate (0.3,0,0.8,0.15); Linear (0,0,1,1); Emphasized is a two-segment path, not a single cubic-bezier. Durations: Short1 50 ms, Short2 100, Short3 150, Short4 200, Medium1 250, Medium2 300, Medium3 350, Medium4 400, Long1 450, Long2 500, Long3 550, Long4 600, ExtraLong1–4 700/800/900/1000 ms. => IMPLICATION: Publish a motion token file mirroring these: use Short3/Short4 (150–200 ms) for state changes (option select, toggle), Medium2/Medium3 (300–350 ms) for screen and sheet transitions, Emphasized Decelerate for entering elements, Emphasized Accelerate for exiting, Standard for in-place property changes. Never use spring bounce >1.0 overshoot; no durations over 400 ms in the test-taking flow where students are time-pressured. Cap the entire motion vocabulary at ~5 tokens to keep it professional.
- (high) PowerSync + Supabase is the documented, server-authoritative offline pairing; @powersync/react-native hit 2.0.0 on 2026-07-21: npm @powersync/react-native latest 2.0.0 (2026-07-21; previous 1.35.9 on 2026-07-10 — a major bump 11 days before this research). @powersync/op-sqlite 0.9.15 (2026-07-10), @powersync/attachments 3.0.0, @journeyapps/react-native-quick-sqlite 2.5.2. GitHub powersync-ja/powersync-js: 693 stars, last push 2026-07-24, Apache-2.0, 42 open issues. Supabase lists PowerSync as an official partner. Setup ≈10–15 min: create a PowerSync Postgres user with replication privileges, a Postgres publication, connect the instance, enable Supabase auth. Sync Streams (SQL-like YAML) control which rows reach which device. Documented caveat: Supabase WAL growth on idle instances requires config adjustment on hobby projects. => IMPLICATION: This is the lowest-risk offline path for a Supabase backend and the one to default to. Sync Streams map cleanly onto the content hierarchy — sync only the chapters/topics a student has downloaded, plus their own attempts, never the whole question bank.
- (high) PowerSync conflict resolution is server-authoritative — the client never merges, which fits offline test submission exactly: Writes go to local SQLite, are placed on an upload queue, and are uploaded by a developer-defined uploadData() function. 'Because of the server-authoritative nature of PowerSync, whatever is written to the backend database will automatically be reflected on all clients (without overwriting local changes that have not yet been uploaded). The client never has to resolve conflicts locally. Only once all the local changes have been acknowledged by the server, and the data for that new checkpoint is downloaded by the client, does the client advance to the next checkpoint.' Custom write checkpoints (async writes via a message queue, transaction.complete(writeCheckpointId)) are Team/Enterprise-tier only. => IMPLICATION: For offline answers: write the answer row locally with a client-generated UUID and a monotonic client_seq + client_timestamp; on upload, a Supabase Edge Function / Postgres function applies exam-integrity rules (reject if the attempt window has closed, take first-write-wins per (attempt_id, question_id), recompute score server-side). Since the server is authoritative, the corrected state replicates back automatically. Do NOT trust client timestamps for the exam timer — that stays server-authoritative and separate from sync.
- (high) PowerSync pricing breaks at your 10,000-concurrent-student scale-target: Free: 2 GB synced/month, 500 MB hosted, 50 peak concurrent connections, projects deactivate after 1 week idle. Pro $49+/mo: 30 GB synced included ($1/GB over), 10 GB hosted ($1/GB over), 1,000 concurrent connections included, $30 per additional 1,000 connections. Team $599+/mo: same data/connection rates, SLA, uptime guarantee, RBAC, AWS private endpoints. Enterprise: custom. Open Edition is free and source-available (Postgres, MongoDB, MySQL, SQL Server) with the core sync engine, Sync Rules and all client SDKs; Enterprise Self-Hosted adds custom write checkpoints + SOC 2. => IMPLICATION: 10,000 concurrent students = 10,000 concurrent connections = Pro at $49 + 9 × $30 = ~$319/mo in connection charges alone, before data. Model this early. The Open Edition self-hosted on GCP is a credible cost lever, but you then own scaling the sync service — and custom write checkpoints are NOT in Open Edition.
- (high) WatermelonDB is stalled; Legend-State's stable release is 2 years old and its Supabase plugin is beta: @nozbe/watermelondb latest 0.28.0 published 2025-04-07; next 0.28.1-0 published 2025-07-24; GitHub Nozbe/WatermelonDB 11,751 stars but last push 2025-08-11 — ~11 months of no activity as of 2026-07-25, with 302 open issues. @legendapp/state latest (stable dist-tag) is 2.1.15 from 2024-08-30; the actively developed line is 3.0.0-beta.48 (2026-07-12) — i.e. v3 has been in beta for ~22 months. Supabase's own Legend-State guide is dated 2024-09-23 and uses @legendapp/state@beta with AsyncStorage persistence and required created_at / updated_at / deleted columns plus a trigger; changesSince:'last-sync'. => IMPLICATION: WatermelonDB's popularity is legacy popularity — do not pick it for a new RN 0.86 app despite the many Supabase+WatermelonDB tutorials. Legend-State v3 is attractive (fine-grained reactivity, first-party Supabase sync plugin, MMKV/AsyncStorage persistence) but you would be shipping a beta and its persistence is AsyncStorage, not SQLite, which is wrong for a 50k-question bank. Reserve Legend-State for UI/session state, not the question corpus.
- (high) expo-sqlite 57 now has libSQL sync + changeset APIs, and Expo publishes an official local-first guide: expo-sqlite ~57.0.1. It exposes sync push/pull/checkpoint methods against a remote libSQL server, session-extension changeset APIs (create changeset sync/async, invert changeset), and expo-sqlite/localStorage/install as a drop-in localStorage. Turso Offline Sync is explicitly 'beta quality and not yet recommended for production… no durability guarantees during the beta period.' Expo's official local-first guide lists Legend-State, TinyBase, expo-sqlite, Yjs, Prisma (early access), Jazz, LiveStore, Turso, Instant, RxDB — and warns 'tools available today are still in their early stages', so builders should expect to solve emerging problems themselves. Notably PowerSync and ElectricSQL are not in that list. Alternative driver: @op-engineering/op-sqlite 17.1.2 (2026-07-05), GitHub OP-Engineering/op-sqlite 1,023 stars, last push 2026-07-14, only 5 open issues — the fastest RN SQLite driver and PowerSync's own backing store via @powersync/op-sqlite. => IMPLICATION: Two viable stacks: (A) PowerSync + @powersync/op-sqlite (managed sync, server-authoritative, costs money at scale); (B) expo-sqlite or op-sqlite + a hand-rolled pull/push sync against Supabase using updated_at watermarks + soft deletes (free, but you own conflict semantics). Given exam integrity is server-authoritative anyway, (B) is defensible for the read-mostly question bank and (A) is over-engineering if attempts are simply POSTed. Do not build on Turso Offline Sync while it is beta.
- (high) Hermes V1 costs 25–30% more Android RAM — a real problem on 4 GB Indian devices: Expo SDK 57 known issue: 'importing react-native-reanimated can increase your app's memory usage by 25–30%' due to Hermes V1 changes; workaround is enabling worklets bundle mode. Corroborated by react-native issue #57059: '[Android] Higher memory usage on 0.85.x vs 0.83.x with the new Hermes (~25–30% more RAM, same app)'. Hermes V1 became the default engine for iOS and Android in RN 0.84 (rewritten compiler, new bytecode format, improved JIT, Hades concurrent GC). Hermes V1 is not Static Hermes: it does not compile JS to a native binary, does not use type information, and JIT is not yet enabled. => IMPLICATION: Enable worklets bundle mode in SDK 57 and measure RSS on a 4 GB device before and after. Combined with WebView memory this is the app's tightest constraint. Verify .hbc files are actually in the release bundle if startup regresses.
- (medium) India 2026 device reality: entry-level collapsed to 8% share and base models are regressing to 4 GB RAM: Entry-level smartphone share in India fell from 18% a year earlier to 8% in early 2026; sub-USD 100 shipments dropped 59% YoY in Q1 2026 due to AI-driven memory-price inflation. Memory is now 43% of the bill of materials for sub-Rs 30,000 phones. The 4–8 GB RAM segment was 42.3% of the market in 2025, and 2026 low-end base models are expected to return to 4 GB. Network side: average monthly data use per user crossed 31 GB (up from 27.5 GB in 2024, 18% 5-yr CAGR); 5G is ~47% of mobile broadband traffic nationally, 58% in metros; 892 M active 4G devices of which 383 M are 5G-capable; Jio averaged 122.9 Mb/s download (Jul 2025–Jun 2026). => IMPLICATION: Target device profile: 4 GB RAM, 720p–1080p, Snapdragon 4-series / Dimensity 6000-series, Android 13–15. Bandwidth is actually decent and cheap (31 GB/month) — so a 3–6 MB chapter download of pre-rendered question content is fine, but RAM is the scarce resource. Optimise for memory (WebView count, image decode size, Hermes RSS), not for bytes over the wire.
- (medium) EAS Update economics: MAU-based, and full-bundle downloads unless bundle diffing is enabled: Tiers: Free $0 — 1,000 MAU / 100 GiB bandwidth; Starter $19/mo — 3,000 MAU / 100 GiB; Production $199/mo — 50,000 MAU / 1 TiB; Enterprise custom — 1,000,000+ MAU / 40 TiB. An MAU is a unique user who downloads at least one update that month; three downloads = one MAU but you pay bandwidth for all three. Overage on Starter+: $0.10/GiB edge bandwidth, $0.05/GiB storage; each additional MAU includes 40 MiB of edge bandwidth. By default every OTA update ships the entire JS bundle — a 12 MB bundle re-downloads fully for a one-line change. Bundle diffing is in beta (SDK 55+, Hermes only). Expo SDK 57 pins expo-updates ~57.0.10. Separately, Expo DOM components reportedly do not support OTA updates. => IMPLICATION: At 10,000 students, Production ($199/mo, 50,000 MAU / 1 TiB) is the right tier. With a 12 MB bundle and 4 updates/month, 10,000 users × 4 × 12 MB ≈ 480 GB — inside 1 TiB but not by much. Crucially: ship question-bank content through Supabase/CDN JSON, NOT through the JS bundle. Use EAS Update only for UI and logic fixes. Enable bundle diffing once it leaves beta.
- (high) expo-image is the correct image layer; recyclingKey is mandatory inside recycled lists: expo-image ~57.0.1 in SDK 57 (npm latest 57.0.1, 2026-07-15). Wraps SDWebImage on iOS and Glide on Android — off-JS-thread decode, disk persistence, request deduplication, and downsampling to target dimensions rather than decoding the full source. cachePolicy 'memory-disk' is the recommended default. The recyclingKey prop resets view content when a view is recycled and is described as 'very important in lists'. SDK 57 added cache seeding/reading methods to expo-image. Known bug: expo/expo #26177 — cachePolicy 'disk' uses as much memory as 'memory'. => IMPLICATION: For image-based questions (scanned PYQs, diagrams, circuit figures) use expo-image with cachePolicy='memory-disk', an explicit recyclingKey={question.id}, a blurhash/thumbhash placeholder, and server-side pre-resizing to the exact display width. Combined with FlashList recycling this is the pattern that keeps a 90-question paper scrollable on 4 GB. Also use expo-image's new cache-seeding API to pre-warm a downloaded chapter's images for offline mocks.
- (medium) Shared element transitions: iOS-only zoom transition in expo-router; cross-platform version still behind a flag: Expo SDK 55+ exposes Apple's iOS 18 Zoom Transition through expo-router's Link component — 'an alpha API available on iOS only… the API is subject to breaking changes', gracefully falling back to normal navigation on Android and iOS 17 and earlier. Cross-platform gesture-driven native shared element transitions are announced as coming to Expo Router via Reanimated but are 'not production-ready yet and behind a feature flag'. react-native-shared-element has not shipped in Expo Go since SDK 49. => IMPLICATION: Do not design the navigation choreography around shared element transitions — the majority of your users are on Android where it does not exist. Achieve continuity instead with matched-position fades and a Reanimated 4 CSS transition on transform/opacity, which works identically on both platforms and is cheaper.
- (medium) react-native-enriched-html is a genuinely native rich-text option, but it has no math and was just renamed: Software Mansion's react-native-enriched (0.8.1, 2026-06-16) has been renamed to react-native-enriched-html (1.0.1, 2026-07-13); 'the original package will not receive any further updates or bug fixes'. It is a fully native rich text input with synchronous styling, live style detection and HTML parse/serialize; New Architecture (Fabric) only; supports RN 0.81–0.85 (note: 0.86 not yet listed); Android + iOS, Web experimental. Sibling react-native-enriched-markdown 0.7.4 (2026-07-09) renders Markdown as native text. Math support is an open request (#109/#439). For comparison, react-native-render-html is dead (6.3.4, 2022-01-24); its engine @native-html/transient-render-engine is at 12.0.1 (2026-05-07). react-native-marked 8.1.1 (2026-07-13) is a maintained markdown-to-native renderer. => IMPLICATION: Best-of-both architecture: render non-math prose natively (react-native-enriched-html or react-native-marked → real RN Text, selectable, themable, cheap) and drop into the single shared WebView or a pre-rendered image only for the math/chem fragments. This avoids paying WebView cost for the ~40% of JEE/NEET questions that are pure text.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- STORAGE FORMAT — store LaTeX source as the single canonical column, plus derived caches. Schema: questions.body_latex TEXT (canonical, authored via TipTap+MathLive, always editable and diffable), questions.body_html TEXT (KaTeX renderToString output, regenerated by a Supabase Edge Function trigger on write — measured 0.103 ms/expr so regenerating the whole bank is seconds), questions.body_mathml TEXT (KaTeX output:'mathml', 205–484 B gz, used for accessibility + Postgres full-text search after stripping tags), questions.plain_text TEXT (LaTeX stripped to searchable words, feeds a tsvector GIN index), and optionally questions.body_ast JSONB (MathJSON from MathLive) only if you later need symbolic answer-equivalence checking. Rationale: LaTeX is compact, human-editable, diff-friendly and is what admins actually type; HTML is a disposable cache you can regenerate when KaTeX or your theme changes; pre-rendered SVG/PNG is a dead end for the primary path because it is 2.5x larger, 11x slower to produce, unsearchable, un-restylable, and breaks dark mode and font scaling. Never make SVG or HTML the source of truth.
- MATH RENDERING ARCHITECTURE — 'one WebView per screen, never per row'. Build a single persistent <WebView> component (`MathCanvas`) mounted once per question-viewing screen, loading a locally bundled, fully offline HTML asset (~590 KB: katex.min.js 272 KB + katex.min.css 25 KB + 20 woff2 fonts 260 KB + mhchem.min.js 34 KB; no CDN, no network). Push question HTML into it via injectJavaScript / postMessage as the student navigates, and receive measured content height back via onMessage to size the container. Render everything that is NOT math — stems, options, tags, chrome, timer, palette — as real React Native views so the WebView holds only the typeset fragment. For the practice FEED (a FlashList of many questions) do not use a WebView at all: display the server-pre-rendered HTML in react-native-enriched-html / react-native-marked, or fall back to a pre-rendered PNG/SVG served from Supabase Storage via expo-image with recyclingKey. Prototype-validate this on a 4 GB Redmi/Realme before committing.
- OFFLINE-FIRST — split the problem in two rather than adopting one heavyweight sync framework. (1) Question bank = read-only, server-owned: sync with expo-sqlite (SDK 57) or @op-engineering/op-sqlite 17.1.2 using a simple updated_at watermark pull per (chapter, exam) with soft deletes; wrap it in a 'Download chapter for offline' UX with a visible size estimate. (2) Student writes (answers, notes, attempt state) = queue + server-authoritative reconcile: write locally with a client-generated UUID plus client_seq and client_timestamp, POST the queue to a Supabase Edge Function on reconnect, and let the server enforce first-write-wins per (attempt_id, question_id), reject writes after the attempt window closed, and recompute the score. Adopt PowerSync 2.0.0 only if you find you need Sync Streams' row-level fan-out; budget ~$319/mo at 10k concurrent connections, or self-host Open Edition on GCP (noting custom write checkpoints are Enterprise-only). Explicitly reject WatermelonDB (11 months stale) and Turso Offline Sync (beta, no durability guarantees).
- ADMIN AUTHORING — TipTap 3.29.0 + @tiptap/extension-mathematics (now MIT) + MathLive 0.110.0 as the virtual math keyboard, in a WEB admin console, not inside the RN app. MathLive gives 800+ LaTeX commands, a genuinely usable mobile virtual math keyboard, and export to LaTeX / MathML / MathJSON. Pin katex to 0.17.x in the editor because extension-mathematics still peer-deps ^0.16.4 || ^0.17.0. Give authors one-tap snippets for \ce{}, \pu{}, \frac, \int, \vec, matrix and equilibrium arrows. For chemical structures, embed ketcher-react 3.17.1 in the admin console only, export MOL/SMILES, and render server-side with Python RDKit to SVG stored in Supabase Storage — never ship the 6.6 MB RDKit WASM to the client. If you need in-question inline structures, use smiles-drawer 2.4.1 (59 KB gzip) inside the same math WebView.
- MOTION SYSTEM — publish a 5-token motion file and forbid ad-hoc animation. Durations: 150 ms (state change: option select, chip toggle), 200 ms (small enter/exit), 300 ms (screen/sheet transition), 350 ms (modal, bottom sheet), and nothing over 400 ms anywhere in the timed-test flow. Easing: standard cubic-bezier(0.2,0,0,1) for in-place property changes, emphasized-decelerate cubic-bezier(0.05,0.7,0.1,1) for entering, emphasized-accelerate cubic-bezier(0.3,0,0.8,0.15) for exiting. Implement with Reanimated 4.5.x CSS Transitions/Animations (declarative, easier to optimise), not Moti (abandoned, Reanimated-3-only). No bounce, no overshoot >1.0, no spring for anything a student sees while a timer is running. Stagger at most ~20 children, 20–30 ms apart. Respect prefers-reduced-motion / AccessibilityInfo.isReduceMotionEnabled and collapse everything to a 100 ms opacity fade.
- PERFORMANCE BUDGETS AND GUARDRAILS — enforce as CI/QA checks on a 4 GB Android reference device: at most 1 live WebView per screen (assert in dev); ≤100 simultaneously animated components; every Skia Canvas has opaque set (SurfaceView, not TextureView — this alone cut RenderThread CPU 50–65% and janky frames from 85% in the Margelo case study); no Skia canvas mounted while off-screen or idle; FlashList v2 with getItemType keyed on question archetype, maintainVisibleContentPosition={{disabled:true}} on the question list, useLayoutState in any row whose height changes after mount, and never nested inside a ScrollView (issue #1966 crashes Oppo/Samsung); expo-image everywhere with cachePolicy='memory-disk' + recyclingKey + server-side pre-resizing; enable worklets bundle mode to claw back the 25–30% Hermes V1 Android RAM regression.
- VERSION PINNING POLICY — take Expo SDK 57's pins as the baseline (react-native 0.86.0, react-native-webview 13.16.1, @shopify/flash-list 2.0.2, react-native-svg 15.15.4, react-native-reanimated 4.5.0, react-native-worklets 0.10.0, @shopify/react-native-skia 2.6.2, expo-sqlite/expo-image/expo-router/expo-updates ~57.x) and use `npx expo install` rather than chasing npm latest. Consider deliberate, tested overrides for @shopify/flash-list (2.0.2 → 2.3.2, 8 months of bugfixes) and react-native-webview (13.16.1 is two majors behind npm's 14.0.1/15/16 — audit whether the memory-leak and Android fixes you need are in 13.16.1). Pin katex exactly and treat a KaTeX minor bump as a breaking change requiring visual regression tests, because 0.18.0 renamed internal CSS classes.
- SHIP QUESTION CONTENT OVER THE NETWORK, NOT IN THE JS BUNDLE. EAS Update bills per-MAU with full-bundle downloads by default (a 12 MB bundle re-downloads entirely for a one-line change; diffing is beta on SDK 55+/Hermes only). At 10k students, Production tier is $199/mo for 50,000 MAU / 1 TiB. Serve the question bank as versioned JSON/HTML from Supabase Storage behind a CDN with content-hash filenames, and reserve EAS Update strictly for UI and logic hotfixes. Also verify before adopting Expo DOM Components ('use dom') that the documented 'no OTA update support' limitation still holds in SDK 57 — if it does, that rules them out for the core reader despite being the ergonomically nicest KaTeX host.
PITFALLS:
- The classic killer, quantified: one WebView per FlatList/FlashList row. Production reports put each WebView at 150–200 MB, and mature systems cap live instances at ~3 with LRU eviction. With India's 2026 entry-level baseline regressing to 4 GB RAM, 5 simultaneously mounted WebViews will OOM-kill the app mid-test. FlashList recycling makes it worse, not better, because recycled rows tear down and re-instantiate WebViews during fast scroll — and WebView instantiation is 'an exceptionally heavy operation'. Additionally react-native-webview issue #3168 documents an Android leak where every change to the onMessage prop adds a BatchedBridge callback that is never released — exactly what happens when a recycled row rebinds its handler.
- Adopting an abandoned math library because it ranks well in search. react-native-math-view is ARCHIVED (last push 2024-09-05, npm 3.9.5 from 2021), react-native-mathjax-html-to-svg last published 2023-06-30, react-native-mathjax-text-svg 2023-11-30, react-native-mathjax-svg 2021, react-native-latex 2020, react-native-render-html 2022-01-24, Quill 2024-11-30, Moti 2025-01-29 (repo last push 2025-03-11, still Reanimated-3-only). All of these predate the removal of the legacy bridge in RN 0.82 and/or Fabric-only Reanimated 4, so they range from unmaintained to structurally incompatible. Any library whose last release predates mid-2025 should be presumed broken on RN 0.86.
- Silent version-lock traps in the math toolchain. @tiptap/extension-mathematics@3.29.0 peer-deps katex '^0.16.4 || ^0.17.0' — it does not accept katex 0.18.x. Meanwhile katex 0.18.0 (2026-07-17) introduced CSS class prefixing that breaks any custom .katex-* selectors, and 0.17.0 broke the internal __defineFunction API. If the admin editor and the mobile renderer install different katex majors you will get subtly different typesetting between authoring preview and student view — the worst possible bug class for an exam product. Decide the katex version once, globally, and gate upgrades behind visual regression tests.
- Treating pre-rendered SVG or PNG as the storage format. MathJax SVG is 2.5x larger and 11x slower to produce than KaTeX HTML (measured: 3,936 B vs 829 B gzip and 1.14 ms vs 0.103 ms for a typical JEE integral). Worse, images are unsearchable, cannot be restyled for dark mode or dynamic type, cannot reflow at different widths, and cannot be edited — every typo fix requires a re-render pipeline run. Images are a legitimate FALLBACK for the feed, never the canonical store. Symmetrically, storing HTML as canonical locks you into today's KaTeX version and destroys editability.
- Assuming MathML alone is enough on Android. caniwebview marks MathML 'widely available' in both WKWebView and Android WebView, but Chromium ships only MathML Core, which is an incomplete subset; Firefox has strong support and Chrome does not. Complex JEE Advanced constructs — matrices/determinants, multi-line aligned equations, stretchy mhchem equilibrium arrows, large radicals — are exactly where MathML Core degrades. Ship KaTeX HTML+CSS as the visual path and use MathML only as an accessibility/search layer.
- Skia rendering into a TextureView on low-end Android. The Margelo Pingo investigation on a sub-$100 120 Hz device found 79% of frames flagged for slow bitmap uploads and 85% janky frames purely from TextureView compositing (GPU touching canvas pixels 2–3x per frame across Skia → HWUI → SurfaceFlinger). Also: usePathValue and usePathInterpolation can create worklets that re-dirty their own outputs, re-recording an idle canvas forever (17–32 ms frame time on a canvas showing nothing). Both bugs are invisible on a flagship and catastrophic on the devices your users actually own.
- The Hermes V1 memory regression compounding with WebView memory. Expo SDK 57's own known-issues section states importing react-native-reanimated can raise Android memory 25–30% under Hermes V1 (corroborated by react-native issue #57059 comparing 0.85.x to 0.83.x). Reanimated is unavoidable in this app. Combined with a WebView and image caches on a 4 GB device, you can hit the Android per-process heap limit. Enable worklets bundle mode and measure RSS on the reference device from week one, not at launch.
- PowerSync's free tier (50 concurrent connections, 2 GB/month, projects deactivate after 1 week idle) will pass every demo and fail the first real mock test. The stated scale target is 10,000+ concurrent students; that is 10,000 concurrent connections, i.e. Pro at $49 + 9 × $30 = ~$319/mo in connection charges before any data cost. Custom write checkpoints — the feature you would want if attempts are processed asynchronously via a queue — are Team/Enterprise or Enterprise-Self-Hosted only, not in the free Open Edition.
