- Designing navigation around shared element transitions. expo-router's zoom transition is an alpha, iOS-18-only API that silently falls back to a plain push on Android and iOS 17; the cross-platform Reanimated implementation is still behind a feature flag and 'not production-ready'. Since the overwhelming majority of Indian JEE/NEET users are on Android, any choreography that depends on shared elements will be seen by almost nobody and will make the two platforms feel like different products.
- FlashList v2 defaults that break exam UX: maintainVisibleContentPosition is ON by default and causes items to move when data re-orders (e.g. when a randomised question palette re-sorts, or answers sync in), and issue #1966 reports an infinite render loop / crash on specific Oppo and Samsung devices when FlashList v2 is nested inside a ScrollView — a very natural thing to do for a 'scrollable question with a palette below'. Also, rows whose height changes after mount (late-loading diagrams) leave blank gaps unless useLayoutState is used.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- Does Expo SDK 57 still block OTA updates for DOM components ('use dom')? The docs page says they 'currently don't support over-the-air updates', but the page is not SDK-versioned. This single fact decides whether DOM components or a raw react-native-webview hosts the KaTeX renderer. Verify against the SDK 57 changelog and an actual EAS Update test build.
- What is the real measured memory and first-paint cost of ONE persistent WebView loading the 590 KB local KaTeX bundle on a 4 GB Android device (Redmi 13C / Realme C-series class)? The 150–200 MB figure comes from micro-frontend WebViews loading full web apps; a static local HTML page with KaTeX should be far cheaper, but this must be measured before the architecture is locked.
- Why does Expo SDK 57 pin react-native-webview to 13.16.1 when npm has 14.0.1 latest and 15.0.0/16.0.0 published? Is 13.16.1 chosen for Fabric stability, and do the Android memory-leak fixes (issue #3168) and any 0.86 compatibility fixes exist in 13.16.1 or only in 14+?
- Is there a maintained Fabric-compatible bridge to SwiftMath (iOS) and AndroidMath (Android)? react-native-enriched-markdown issue #109 shows a linked PR #119 marked closed — did that PR actually land native math rendering, or was it closed unmerged? If a native math text run exists, it would remove the WebView entirely and is worth a week of evaluation.
- Does @tiptap/extension-mathematics have a roadmap for katex 0.18 support, given 0.18.0's CSS class-prefix breaking change? If not, how long can the admin editor safely stay on katex 0.17.x while the renderer is on 0.18.x, and are the rendered outputs pixel-identical?
- At 10,000 concurrent students, what is the actual PowerSync bill (connections + GB synced) versus the engineering cost of a hand-rolled expo-sqlite/op-sqlite watermark sync against Supabase? Needs a modelled comparison including the Open Edition self-hosted path on GCP and its lack of custom write checkpoints.
- How does FlashList v2.3.2 behave with rows containing pre-rendered KaTeX HTML of highly variable height (1 line to 15 lines) under fast fling on a 4 GB device — specifically whether useLayoutState is sufficient or whether server-supplied height hints per question are needed to avoid blank areas?
- Is smiles-drawer 2.4.1 output quality acceptable for NEET/JEE organic chemistry (stereochemistry wedges, aromatic rings, reaction mechanisms) compared to RDKit's renderer? If not, is server-side RDKit → SVG the only acceptable path, and what is the storage/CDN cost for a few thousand structures?
- What is the practical file-size and startup cost of the release APK/AAB with the KaTeX asset bundle, Reanimated, Skia, WebView and expo-image included, and does it stay under the thresholds where Play Store install conversion drops in India? Needs a real EAS build measurement, including per-ABI splits.
- Does Reanimated 4's CSS Animations API have measurably lower overhead than the imperative useAnimatedStyle path on low-end Android, as Software Mansion implies ('easier to optimize because the team understands more details about what attributes of which components are being animated')? Unverified — needs a benchmark on the reference device before standardising the whole motion system on it.

## gamification-legal
SUMMARY: The legal ground shifted decisively under India's Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, 2025 (Act 32 of 2025, assented 22 Aug 2025), whose implementing Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Rules, 2026 were notified 22 Apr 2026 and came into force 1 May 2026. The Act bans "online money games" — any online game where a user pays fees/deposits money or "other stakes" (expressly including virtual coins and tokens equivalent or convertible to money) expecting monetary or other enrichment — irrespective of skill or chance, with up to 3 years' imprisonment and ₹1 crore fine for offering, and up to 2 years / ₹50 lakh for advertising it. It expressly carves out "online social games" offered solely for recreation, entertainment or skill development, which may charge subscription or one-time access fees so long as those are not stakes; law-firm commentary consistently places edtech quizzes and gamified learning in this bucket. The Supreme Court on 27 May 2026 (DGGI v. Gameskraft, 2026 INSC 595, Pardiwala & Mahadevan JJ) held 28% GST applies to the full face value of stakes and that skill vs chance is irrelevant once money is staked, upholding ~₹2.5 lakh crore retrospective demands — but the constitutional challenge to the 2025 ban itself is still pending before a larger bench, so this is a live area. Separately, random-draw giveaways are chance-based: they escape the Lotteries (Regulation) Act, 1998 only if there is no consideration (s.2(b) requires "purchasing tickets"), and the Prize Competitions Act, 1955 caps prizes at ₹1,000/month and 2,000 entries in the states where it applies. Tax bites even on free giveaways: s.194B mandates 30% (31.2% with cess) TDS on winnings from "any game of any sort", and from 1 Apr 2025 the aggregate ₹10,000 threshold was replaced by a per-single-transaction ₹10,000 test. The bigger sleeper risk for this specific product is the DPDP Act 2023 + DPDP Rules 2025 (notified 13 Nov 2025, substantive obligations enforceable 13 May 2027): every user under 18 is a "child", requiring verifiable parental consent, and s.9(3) prohibits tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising directed at children outright — regardless of consent — with penalties up to ₹200 crore. On the product side, meta-analyses show gamified learning works at roughly d≈0.57 (37 RCTs, 182 effect sizes) but leaderboard design is the swing factor: relative/bucketed leaderboards raise engagement while absolute all-class rankings demoralise the bottom, which matters acutely for a cohort where NEET-linked suicides rose from 4 (2021) to ≥32 (2025). Rajasthan's Coaching Centres (Control and Regulation) Act, 2025 already bans coaching centres from making assessment-test results public and from segregating batches by performance — a direct regulatory signal against public rank walls.
FINDINGS:
- (high) PROGA 2025 bans all online money games regardless of skill — the skill/chance defence is dead for staked play: Promotion and Regulation of Online Gaming Act, 2025 (Act No. 32 of 2025): passed Lok Sabha 20 Aug 2025, Rajya Sabha 21 Aug 2025, presidential assent 22 Aug 2025. s.2(i) defines 'online money game' as a game where a user pays fees or deposits money or other stakes in expectation of winning monetary or other enrichment, 'irrespective of whether such games are based on skill or chance'. s.5 prohibits offering/facilitating; s.6 prohibits advertising that directly or indirectly promotes participation; s.7 bars banks and payment systems from processing related transactions. Penalties: offering or facilitating financial transactions — up to 3 years imprisonment and/or up to ₹1 crore fine, cognizable and non-bailable; advertising — up to 2 years and/or ₹50 lakh; non-compliance with directions — civil penalty up to ₹10 lakh plus suspension/cancellation of registration. Extraterritorial: reaches services offered in India even if operated from abroad. => IMPLICATION: Any mechanic where a JEE/NEET student pays (subscription, entry fee, or spends purchased coins) and can win something of monetary value is a criminal offence, not a civil risk. Rules out paid-entry mock-test tournaments with cash/gadget prizes, 'buy coins to enter the contest', and any coin that is purchasable AND wins prizes.
- (high) 'Other stakes' expressly includes virtual coins and tokens — but only those 'equivalent or convertible to money': Trilegal's analysis quotes the statutory definition: other stakes means 'anything equivalent or convertible to money, including virtual coins and tokens.' The qualifier is convertibility/equivalence to money. Trilegal explicitly notes the Act does not resolve whether non-convertible virtual rewards in casual games fall outside the ban — an acknowledged ambiguity. The 2026 Rules, r.9, list the examination factors as payment requirements, expectation of enrichment, and 'monetization of in-game assets'. => IMPLICATION: The safe harbour for our coins is architectural, not contractual: coins must be non-purchasable, non-withdrawable, non-transferable, and redeemable only for in-app utility with no secondary market. If a coin can be bought with rupees or converted into anything with a rupee price, r.9 'monetization of in-game assets' pulls it toward money-game classification.
- (high) Edtech quizzes and gamified learning are explicitly categorised as 'online social games' — the permitted bucket: s.2(h) defines online social game as one offered solely for recreation, entertainment or skill development; subscription or one-time access fees are permitted provided they are not in the nature of a stake or wager. Practitioner commentary states: 'Educational games such as quizzes, polls (e.g., Kahoot), coding hackathons and other immersive, interactive, and gamified learning exercises offered on edtech platforms should operate as online social games.' The same commentary states the inverse plainly: 'knowledge contests that charge entry fees and distribute cash prizes are banned under the Act.' => IMPLICATION: Our core product (paid subscription + mock tests + non-cash rewards) sits in the permitted category by design. The line we must never cross is entry-fee-to-cash-prize. Subscription revenue is fine as long as no contest outcome is tied to having paid.
- (high) Online Gaming Rules 2026: registration is NOT universally required for social games, but obligations are: Notified 22 Apr 2026 by MeitY; in force 1 May 2026; 6 parts, 26 rules. Establishes the Online Gaming Authority of India (chaired by MeitY Additional Secretary, with MHA, MoF, MIB, MYAS, MoLJ representation), functioning as a digital office. Rule 8: determination of money-game status is triggered only by (a) Authority suo motu, (b) provider seeking e-sport designation, or (c) Central Government notification of a category. Rule 10: determinations are provider-specific and game-specific. Registration required only for e-sports and for social games/categories specifically notified by the Central Government; certificates valid up to 10 years and must be displayed prominently. Obligations that apply regardless: age verification/gating, parental controls, time limits, internal grievance redressal (r.20) with 30-day Authority appeal and 30-day disposal, and traffic data/metadata retention on India-located computer resources (rr.16-17). Banks must verify registration status for e-sports and social games before enabling transactions. => IMPLICATION: We do not need to pre-register today, but we must build: an age gate, parental controls, session time limits, a documented in-app grievance officer flow with 30-day SLA, and India-resident logging. Also: the Central Government can notify our category into mandatory registration at any time — architect for it. Bank verification of registration status is a payment-rail risk if our category ever gets notified.
- (high) Constitutional challenge to PROGA 2025 is still pending — do not treat the law as settled: Writ petitions filed in Karnataka, MP and Delhi High Courts were transferred to the Supreme Court on 8 Sep 2025. The 27 May 2026 ruling addressed only the GST question; the constitutional challenge to the ban itself (whether Parliament can prohibit skill-based games despite RMDC 1957 and K.R. Lakshmanan 1996 protecting them under Art. 19(1)(g)) is pending before a separate larger bench and remains unresolved as of July 2026. => IMPLICATION: The permitted/prohibited boundary could move in either direction within our product roadmap horizon. Build the rewards layer so it is legal under the strictest reading (current Act as written), and treat any liberalisation as upside, never as a plan.
- (high) Random-draw giveaways: chance-based, and legal only if there is genuinely zero consideration: Lotteries (Regulation) Act, 1998 s.2(b) defines lottery as a scheme for distribution of prizes by lot or chance to persons participating in the chances of a prize 'by purchasing tickets'. Three cumulative elements: chance + consideration + prize. Only State Governments may run lotteries; private companies, apps and portals are barred. Lucky draws where the essential element of consideration is missing (nothing charged to the participant) fall outside the definition — the analysis relied upon for government digital-payment lucky-draw schemes. => IMPLICATION: A random-draw giveaway is survivable ONLY if entry is free and unconditioned on any payment. If entry is limited to paid subscribers, or if coins spent to enter were ever purchasable, the consideration element revives and it becomes an unlicensed private lottery. Design rule: every giveaway must have an always-available, equally-weighted free entry route, published in the rules.
- (high) Prize Competitions Act 1955 is narrow but lethal where it applies: ₹1,000/month prize cap and 2,000-entry cap: s.2(d): 'prize competition' means any competition where prizes are offered for the solution of any puzzle based upon the building up, arrangement, combination or permutation of letters, words or figures. s.4: no person shall conduct a competition where the total value of prizes (cash or otherwise) offered in any month exceeds ₹1,000, or where entries exceed 2,000. s.5: competitions within those limits still require a licence from the state licensing authority. s.9: contravention punishable with up to 3 months imprisonment and/or fine up to ₹1,000. Territorial extent is limited to erstwhile Andhra, Bombay, Madras, Orissa, Uttar Pradesh, Hyderabad, Madhya Bharat, PEPSU, Saurashtra, all Part C States, plus Dadra & Nagar Haveli, Goa, Daman & Diu and Puducherry. => IMPLICATION: A national 'solve this Physics puzzle, win a tablet' contest is prima facie unlawful in Maharashtra, Gujarat, UP, Tamil Nadu, Andhra/Telangana, Odisha and MP successor territories — the ₹1,000/month cap makes any meaningful prize illegal, and 2,000 entries is trivially exceeded at 10,000 concurrent students. Avoid puzzle/word/figure-arrangement framing for anything with a prize; keep contests as 'assessment performance', not 'puzzle solution'.
- (high) Public Gambling Act 1867 s.12 skill exemption survives for non-staked play; RMDC and Lakshmanan set the predominance test: Public Gambling Act, 1867 outlaws common gaming houses; s.12 provides that no game of 'mere skill', wherever played, is covered. State of Bombay v. R.M.D. Chamarbaugwala (1957): games where skill predominates over chance are not gambling; competitions substantially dependent on skill are protected under Art. 19(1)(g). Dr K.R. Lakshmanan v. State of Tamil Nadu (1996): horse racing is a game of skill; 'gaming' means wagering on games of chance, not skill. => IMPLICATION: These remain our defence for skill-ranked leaderboards and merit-based contests — but only where nothing is staked. Post-PROGA, this line of authority no longer protects anything involving payment. Frame contests as merit-based recognition of academic performance, never as wagering.
- (high) SC 27 May 2026: 28% GST on full face value where money is staked; skill/chance irrelevant; retrospective: DGGI v. Gameskraft Technologies Pvt Ltd, 2026 INSC 595, Justices J.B. Pardiwala and R. Mahadevan, 27 May 2026. Held: real-money online games on uncertain outcomes (including fantasy sports, rummy, poker) constitute 'betting and gambling' for tax purposes regardless of skill content; 28% GST applies to the full face value of deposits/stakes, not platform commission or rake (₹28 on a ₹100 deposit, not ~₹2.80); operators are suppliers of actionable claims in gambling, not intermediaries; the 2023 valuation amendments apply retrospectively. Approx ₹2.5 lakh crore of cumulative demands upheld, including the ₹21,000 crore Gameskraft notice. => IMPLICATION: Confirms the tax authority's willingness to look through form to substance. Our defence must be that there is no staking and no actionable claim at all — free-earned, non-convertible coins are not deposits and generate no pooled prize fund. Never operate a coin 'pool' funded by users that pays out to winners; that structure is exactly what was taxed at face value.
- (high) GST on vouchers: Circular 243/37/2024-GST settles that voucher transactions are neither goods nor services, and breakage is not taxable: CBIC Circular No. 243/37/2024-GST, F.No. CBIC-20001/14/2024-GST, dated 31 Dec 2024. Para 3.4: where a voucher is an RBI-recognised prepaid instrument used to settle an obligation, it falls within 'money' (CGST s.2(75)) and is outside GST. Para 3.5-3.6: where not an RBI PPI, it is an actionable claim; per Schedule III entry 6, actionable claims other than 'specified actionable claims' (s.2(102A): betting, casinos, gambling, horse racing, lottery, online money gaming) are neither supply of goods nor services. Para 3.7: transactions in vouchers themselves are not a supply either way; GST applies to the underlying goods/services on redemption. Para 4.2.1: pure principal-to-principal trading of vouchers is not leviable to GST. Para 6.2: breakage (unredeemed vouchers) is not a supply and attracts no GST. => IMPLICATION: Direct read-across: issuing our own closed-loop coins for free creates no GST supply, and expired/unused coins create no GST liability. GST arises only on the underlying supply when a coin is redeemed for something we actually supply (e.g. a premium test series) — and if the coin was given free, the consideration is nil. This is the cleanest tax posture available and argues strongly for redemption-into-own-content over redemption-into-third-party vouchers.
- (high) TDS s.194B bites at 30% on ANY contest prize over ₹10,000 — including free giveaways and prizes in kind: Section 194B covers winnings from lottery, crossword puzzle, card game and 'other game of any sort', including game shows and entertainment programmes in electronic mode in which people compete to win prizes. Rate: 30% plus 4% health & education cess = 31.2% effective. Finance Act 2025 change effective 1 Apr 2025: the aggregate-per-financial-year ₹10,000 threshold was removed and replaced by a per-single-transaction ₹10,000 test. Where winnings are wholly in kind (or the cash part is insufficient), the payer must ensure tax has been paid before releasing the prize. Winnings are taxed under s.115BB at a flat 30% with no basic exemption and no deductions. Section 194BA (30% on 'net winnings' from online games, per Rule 133 and CBDT Circular 5/2023) covers online-game withdrawals — largely academic for us since PROGA bans that model. => IMPLICATION: A ₹15,000 tablet giveaway triggers a real ₹4,680 TDS obligation, PAN collection, Form 26Q filing and Form 16A issuance for a minor with likely no PAN. Hard design rule: cap any single prize at ≤₹10,000 in fair market value, or accept the full TDS compliance burden. Prefer prizes with high perceived and low monetary value (mentor call, printed certificate, named scholarship credit against our own subscription).
- (high) DPDP Act 2023 + Rules 2025: every JEE/NEET student under 18 is a 'child'; behavioural monitoring and targeted ads at children are banned outright: DPDP Rules 2025 notified 13 Nov 2025; substantive obligations enforceable 13 May 2027. 'Child' = anyone under 18 (broader than COPPA/GDPR). Rule 10: verifiable parental consent required before processing a child's data, verifiable via (a) a parent already verified as a platform user, (b) voluntarily provided identity documents, or (c) virtual tokens linked to verified identity services such as DigiLocker. DPDP Act s.9(3): tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising directed at children are prohibited regardless of parental consent. Limited exemptions exist for educational institutions where tracking is in the interest or safety of the child. Compliance stack includes age gates, parent-linked consent records, symmetric withdrawal, disabled ad personalisation, consent notices in 22 languages, and immutable audit logs. Maximum penalty referenced: ₹200 crore for children's-data breaches. => IMPLICATION: This is the constraint most likely to be underestimated. A large share of our users are 16-18. Behaviour-triggered retention mechanics — 'you're about to lose your 47-day streak', personalised coin offers, engagement-optimised push timing, cohort-targeted promotions — are arguably behavioural monitoring/profiling of children. Segment the user base by declared age from day one; ship a deterministic, non-personalised reward engine for under-18s (fixed rules, same for everyone, no ML-optimised nudge timing) and reserve any personalisation for verified 18+ users. Budget for verifiable parental consent infrastructure well before 13 May 2027.
- (high) Apple App Store 5.3.1/5.3.2 and 3.1.1 constrain contests and virtual currency mechanically: Guideline 5.3.1: sweepstakes and contests must be sponsored by the developer of the app. 5.3.2: official rules must be presented in the app and must make clear Apple is not a sponsor and is not involved in any manner. 5.3.3: apps may not use in-app purchase to buy credit or currency for use with real money gaming of any kind. 5.3.4: real money gaming apps require licensing in every location of use, must be geo-restricted, and must be free. 3.1.1: credits or in-game currencies purchased via IAP may not expire, and a restore mechanism is required; loot-box-style randomised virtual items purchased with money must disclose odds before purchase. 5.1.4(a): apps aimed primarily at kids should not include third-party analytics or third-party advertising. => IMPLICATION: Three concrete build requirements: (1) an in-app Official Rules screen naming us as sole sponsor and disclaiming Apple/Google involvement, for every giveaway; (2) if coins are ever sold via IAP they can never expire — a decisive argument for making coins strictly non-purchasable so we retain the right to expire them and control inflation; (3) no randomised paid rewards without odds disclosure — avoid loot-box framing entirely.
- (high) Google Play: RMG is out in India, and chance-based rewards in game apps are prohibited entirely: Google Play Real-Money Gambling, Games and Contests policy prohibits apps enabling users to wager, stake or participate using real money to obtain a prize of real-world monetary value, with carve-outs for licensed gambling apps, gamified loyalty programs and online crane games. For non-game apps offering chance-based loyalty rewards: official rules must be published within the app, odds must be disclosed for fixed-odds reward programs, and the app must specify a fixed number of winners, a fixed entry deadline and a prize award date. Game apps cannot offer chance-based loyalty rewards at all — only fixed-ratio systems. India context: the Play pilot for DFS and rummy ended 28 Sep 2023; the grace period ended 22 Jan 2026, after which those apps could remain only by removing all real-money functionality. => IMPLICATION: If we classify as a non-game (education) app we can run chance-based rewards, but must publish official rules in-app, disclose odds, and pre-commit to a fixed winner count, entry deadline and award date. This maps neatly onto the Lotteries Act 'no consideration' requirement — publish both in the same rules screen. Prefer deterministic, fixed-ratio coin rewards over randomised drops to stay compliant under either classification.
- (high) Rajasthan Coaching Centres Act 2025 bans making assessment results public and batch segregation by performance: The Rajasthan Coaching Centres (Control and Regulation) Act, 2025 (Act 16 of 2025), gazetted 26 Sep 2025. s.2(d): 'coaching centre' means a centre providing coaching for more than 100 students in any study programme or competitive examinations. s.8(1)(ii): no misleading promises or guarantee of rank or good marks. s.8(1)(v): no registration without a counselling system. s.12(vii): centres must create awareness that excessive ambition causes mental pressure. s.12(viii): 'coaching centre shall not make public the result of assessment test conducted by it' — results must stay confidential and be used for performance analysis, with counselling for deteriorating students. s.12(ix): 'There shall be no segregation of batches based on the performance of students.' s.14: mandatory counsellors/psychologists. s.19 penalties: ₹50,000 first violation, ₹2,00,000 second, cancellation of registration thereafter. s.22: prohibition of misleading advertisement. => IMPLICATION: Whether or not a pure app falls within 's.2(d) centre', this is the clearest statement of Indian regulatory intent on our exact mechanic: public ranking of students on internal assessments is now unlawful for coaching centres in Rajasthan (home to Kota). Any B2B/institutional distribution to Rajasthan coaching centres makes a public rank wall a direct compliance failure for our customer. Default the leaderboard to private/pseudonymous, make institutional rank publication an off-by-default admin toggle, and never segregate content access by performance tier.
- (high) CCPA Coaching Sector misleading-advertisement guidelines are already being enforced with real penalties: CCPA Guidelines for Prevention of Misleading Advertisement in Coaching Sector, 2024, notified 13 Nov 2024. Defines coaching as a centre providing coaching to more than fifty students. CCPA stated on 8 Jan 2024 that the guidelines apply to all coaching institutes 'whether online or physical', though the notified text omits the word 'online' — an acknowledged ambiguity. Mandatory disclosures where a successful candidate is featured: rank secured, name and duration of the course, and whether the course was paid, alongside the candidate's photograph. Written consent from the candidate, obtained after selection, is required before using their name, photograph, testimonial or video. Prohibited: false claims about courses/faculty/fees, false statements of selection numbers, ranks or success rates, guarantees of selection/job/salary, and creating artificial urgency or scarcity. Disclaimer font must match the claim font. Enforcement to date: 45 notices issued, ₹54.60 lakh in penalties imposed on 18 coaching institutes. => IMPLICATION: Two direct hits on our rewards marketing. First, 'artificial urgency or scarcity' is expressly prohibited — countdown timers on coin offers, 'only 3 slots left in this contest', and limited-time streak-save offers are enforcement targets. Second, if we ever advertise our own toppers' results, we need the full disclosure set plus post-selection written consent. Also note CCPA prosecutes edtech under Consumer Protection Act 2019.
- (high) CCPA Dark Patterns Guidelines 2023 list 13 named patterns, several of which are standard gamification defaults: Guidelines for Prevention and Regulation of Dark Patterns, 2023, notified by CCPA on 30 Nov 2023 — India's first formal legal definition of dark patterns, applicable to all platforms, advertisers and sellers. Definition: any deceptive design pattern in UI/UX designed to mislead or trick users into something they did not intend, subverting consumer autonomy, decision making or choice, amounting to misleading advertisement, unfair trade practice or violation of consumer rights. The 13 patterns: false urgency, basket sneaking, confirm shaming, forced action, subscription trap, interface interference, bait and switch, drip pricing, disguised advertisements, nagging, trick wording, SaaS billing, and rogue malware. CCPA subsequently issued an advisory requiring e-commerce platforms to conduct a self-audit for dark patterns within 3 months and publish a self-declaration. => IMPLICATION: Four of the 13 are gamification staples we must design against: (1) confirm shaming — never phrase a streak-freeze or notification opt-out as 'No, I don't care about my rank'; (2) false urgency — no fake countdowns on coin offers; (3) nagging — cap streak-reminder push notifications; (4) forced action — never gate solutions or analytics behind mandatory social sharing or referral. Run a documented dark-pattern self-audit of the rewards UI before launch and keep the artefact.
- (high) Subscription auto-renewal in India is governed by RBI e-mandate rules, not just app-store policy: RBI e-mandate framework for recurring payments: one-time registration with Additional Factor of Authentication (AFA); pre-transaction notification to the customer at least 24 hours before each debit, containing merchant name, amount and scheduled date, with an opt-out option; recurring debits up to ₹15,000 processed without AFA, raised to ₹1,00,000 for insurance premiums, mutual fund investments and credit card bill payments; any modification to a mandate requires fresh AFA; users must be able to modify or cancel the mandate at any time via a secure link in the notification. A consolidated Digital Payments – E-mandate Framework, 2026 further standardises notifications and cancellation flows. => IMPLICATION: If subscriptions are sold outside the app stores (our own web checkout / UPI autopay), we own the 24-hour pre-debit notification and one-click cancellation obligations directly. Combined with the CCPA 'subscription trap' dark pattern, cancellation must be as easy as signup, in the same number of taps. Never let accumulated coins or streaks be framed as something the user forfeits by cancelling — that is textbook confirm shaming plus subscription trap.
- (high) Gamified learning works at moderate effect size, but the element mix determines everything: 2025 meta-analysis in Educational Technology Research and Development consolidating 182 effect sizes across 37 randomised or quasi-randomised trials found a medium positive effect of gamified learning on learning outcomes, d = 0.566, with the 'Rules/Goals + Challenge + Mystery' combination yielding the highest impact. A 2025 meta-analysis in Psychology in the Schools (k = 41) on K-12 motivation found pooled g = 0.654, 95% CI [0.442, 0.866], I² = 88.92. A 2026 higher-education meta-analysis (22 studies) reports d = 1.12, 95% CI [0.42, 1.83], but with I² = 98.84%, indicating extreme heterogeneity and low reliability of the pooled estimate. => IMPLICATION: The evidence supports investing in structured goals, calibrated challenge and discovery — not in points and badges per se. For us that means: adaptive difficulty in practice sets, explicit chapter mastery goals, and 'unlock the next diagnostic insight' framing. Treat the d=1.12 figure as noise (I²≈99%); plan against d≈0.55-0.65.
- (high) Leaderboard type is the single highest-leverage design decision; absolute leaderboards demoralise the bottom: Systematic review of leaderboards in higher education (Li et al., Journal of Computer Assisted Learning, 2024, doi 10.1111/jcal.13077) and related experimental work find: relative leaderboards, where a student sees only nearby competitors rather than the full ranking, produce higher engagement, better performance and 'constructive competitiveness'; absolute leaderboards where every position is publicly visible discourage students at the bottom, exacerbate destructive competition, increase stress and cause disengagement because improvement feels impossible. Public ranking helps those already near the top and demoralises those who are not. Infinite/all-player leaderboards demotivate depending on performance and individual characteristics. Separate experimental work links leaderboard position to measured stress responses. => IMPLICATION: Mandatory design: bucketed cohorts of ~20-30 students of similar recent performance, showing only the local window; promotion/relegation between buckets rather than a global rank; pseudonymous display names; and a personal-best/percentile-improvement track that is always visible while the competitive track is opt-in. Never surface a global rank out of 10,000.
- (medium) The overjustification risk is real but narrow: rewards damage intrinsic motivation mainly for already-interesting free-choice tasks: Deci, Koestner & Ryan meta-analytic work (1999, 2001 Review of Educational Research) — note these are pre-2025 foundational sources — find that expected, tangible, task-contingent rewards undermine intrinsic motivation, with the effect appearing primarily when intrinsic motivation is operationalised as free-choice behaviour during unstructured time; on task-performance measures intrinsic and extrinsic motivation are additive. Negative effects are more serious for children than for college students. Verbal/informational feedback and unexpected rewards do not produce the undermining effect. => IMPLICATION: Reward completion of effortful, low-intrinsic-interest behaviours (attempting the hard PYQ set, reviewing wrong answers, writing a note on a mistake) — not the behaviours students already enjoy. Make rewards informational ('you cleared Rotational Motion at 82% — above your target') rather than purely controlling ('+50 coins'). Prefer unexpected/variable recognition over a fixed per-question coin drip. The 'more serious for children' finding matters given our under-18 majority.
- (medium) Duolingo's streak economy: the transferable parts are forgiveness mechanics and shared-currency coherence: Duolingo reported ~47.7M DAU and ~10.9M paid subscribers in 2025; ~32M DAU maintain a 7+ day streak. Streak freeze: free users capped at 2, up to 5 for long-streak users, with 3 additional freezes at every 100-day Streak Society tier; distributed via random chest drops, Daily Quest rewards, Streak Society milestones and league promotions; Super members are auto-granted a freeze when inventory hits zero. Repair paths: free 'Earn Back' by completing a small number of lessons within a short window (reported 3-day window), or paid instant repair for ~200 gems on iOS/Android. Retention data: at the 14-day threshold, users with freezes averaged 30.63 days vs 18.87 days without; Friend Streaks made users 22% more likely to complete their daily lesson; redesigning the day-7 animation lifted retention +1.7%; milestone share cards drove a 5-10x increase in organic sharing and 6M+ daily streak shares. Stated design principle: protection deploys without requiring a user decision, and forgiveness is 'bounded, not infinite'. => IMPLICATION: Copy: (a) auto-applied streak protection that never asks the user to make a loss-framed decision; (b) free effort-based repair (finish 2 practice sets within 48h) as the primary path; (c) XP-equivalent as a single shared currency feeding streak, league and achievements so the system reads as one thing. Do NOT copy: paid instant streak repair with purchased currency — that is a purchasable good tied to a competitive outcome and drags us toward PROGA/dark-pattern exposure. Milestone share cards are the highest-ROI, lowest-risk borrow.
- (high) The JEE/NEET cohort's mental-health baseline makes competitive pressure mechanics an ethical and reputational hazard: NEET-linked student suicides: 4 in 2021, 9 in 2022, 14 in 2023, 19 in 2024, at least 32 in 2025 — a record high; at least 93 since 2021, with ~40 in Kota alone, followed by Patna and Sikar; at least 14 already documented in 2026. NCRB recorded 13,999 student suicides in 2022, an 81.88% rise since 2011 (7,696). A 2023 Lokniti-CSDS study in Kota found more than 85% of students spend 6-7 hours daily in coaching classes, some stretching to 8, with reported increases in loneliness, mood swings, fatigue, anger, sadness and depression after moving to Kota. A Kota help desk received 373 complaints in its first two months. Research on gamified assessment shows game elements can reduce test anxiety in low-stakes formats, while visible leaderboard rankings increase anxiety and frustration for students who perform poorly. => IMPLICATION: Two hard product rules follow. First, all gamification must sit on low-stakes practice, where it demonstrably reduces test anxiety — never on the mock-test result screen, where it amplifies it. Second, ship an actual safety layer: a session-length cap with a real break prompt, an always-reachable helpline/counselling link, no push notifications between 23:00-06:00, and no notification that frames failure ('you dropped 400 ranks'). A single suicide connected to our leaderboard would be existential for the product regardless of legal position.
- (medium) Virtual currency economics: closed-loop 'walled garden' design is both the legal answer and the economic answer: Standard virtual-economy design treats currency flow as faucets (quest rewards, drops, activity payouts) vs sinks (repair costs, fees, consumables, cosmetics). If faucets exceed sinks, prices inflate and the currency loses meaning. The most effective sinks are ones players opt into because they perceive value, not forced taxes; durable sinks like upkeep, crafting fees and decay remove currency in proportion to activity and stabilise money velocity. To discourage money laundering and inflation, exchange rates between virtual and real currency must be stable, and a 'walled garden' design allows purchasing virtual currency but no cash-out. => IMPLICATION: Our sinks should be: streak freezes (consumable, ~50 coins), extra hint/step-reveal on a solution (~20), unlocking a deeper analytics report on a completed mock (~100), question-paper re-attempt with fresh randomisation (~75), profile cosmetics/avatar frames (~200-500), and donating coins to a class goal. Earn rates should be flat and deterministic, roughly 5-15 coins per meaningful study action, so a diligent student earns ~100-200/day. Build a hard cap on lifetime balance and an expiry (e.g. 180-day inactivity decay) — legal only because coins are never purchased, which is another reason not to sell them.
- (low) India EdTech Consortium self-regulatory code exists but its full text is not publicly verifiable: IAMAI formed the India EdTech Consortium (IEC) in January 2022 following government intervention and rising consumer complaints, with founding members including BYJU'S, Simplilearn, Unacademy, upGrad and Vedantu. Members committed to a common Code of Conduct and a two-tier grievance redressal mechanism. A 'Memorandum' guides members on ethical sales practices, marketing communication, financing/loans/refunds and transparent functioning. Commonly cited but NOT verified in the sources retrieved: a mandatory cooling-off/refund window and two-step verification before enrolment. UNVERIFIED — the full IEC code text was not obtainable from public sources during this research. => IMPLICATION: IEC membership is voluntary and reputational rather than legally binding; the binding obligations come from CCPA/Consumer Protection Act 2019 and the coaching-sector guidelines. Treat IEC alignment as a marketing/trust asset and as a template for the refund and grievance policy, but do not rely on it as a compliance shield. Obtain the actual code text from IAMAI before citing specific commitments publicly.
- (medium) ASCI education advertising guidelines apply to edtech platforms and constrain results-based marketing of our rewards: ASCI guidelines for advertising of educational institutions, programmes and platforms — ASCI's own guidelines index gives an effective date of 9 March 2023, while contemporaneous reporting states they became applicable 1 September 2023 following a consultation that closed 15 April 2023 (date discrepancy unresolved; treat 1 Sep 2023 as the operative applicability date). Scope covers universities, schools, colleges, coaching classes and EdTech platforms, with explicit focus on impact on children's physical and mental well-being. Requirements include: substantiation for any claim of being official/recognised/authorised/accredited/approved/registered/affiliated/endorsed; no advertising that enrolment guarantees high marks, rank or employment without substantiation; and a mandatory disclaimer that 'past record is no guarantee of future prospects'. Also prohibits depicting students compromising sleep or meals, and stereotyping based on academic performance. ASCI's influencer guidelines (latest addendum 6 March 2025) require upfront prominent disclosure, minimum 3 seconds of on-screen disclosure for videos ≤15 seconds, and disclosure by virtual influencers. => IMPLICATION: Directly limits how we market the rewards layer. We cannot advertise 'top the leaderboard, crack JEE'. We cannot depict a student studying through the night as aspirational — that is expressly called out and is also the exact behaviour a naive streak mechanic incentivises. Any 'our toppers' creative needs the 'past record is no guarantee' disclaimer. Referral-influencer campaigns need compliant disclosure labels.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- ADOPT THE CLOSED-LOOP COIN INVARIANT AS A HARD ARCHITECTURAL CONSTRAINT, ENFORCED IN THE DATABASE, NOT JUST IN POLICY. Coins must be: (1) never purchasable with money or IAP; (2) never withdrawable or convertible to cash, UPI, bank transfer, crypto, or third-party gift cards/vouchers; (3) never transferable between users (no gifting, no trading, no P2P); (4) redeemable ONLY for in-app utility we ourselves supply. In Supabase, enforce this with a coin_ledger table where every credit row carries an enum earn_reason drawn from a fixed whitelist of study behaviours, every debit row carries an enum sink_reason from a fixed whitelist of in-app goods, RLS denying INSERT of any purchase-origin credit, and a CHECK constraint plus DB trigger blocking any transfer between user_ids. Add a nightly assertion job that fails loudly if any coin ever has a rupee price attached. This single invariant is what keeps us inside PROGA 2025 s.2(h) 'online social game' and outside s.2(i) 'other stakes'.
- MAKE EVERY GIVEAWAY FREE-ENTRY, DETERMINISTIC-RULE, AND SUB-₹10,000. For each giveaway: publish an in-app Official Rules screen (satisfying Apple 5.3.2, Google Play, and evidencing absence of consideration) that names our entity as sole sponsor, states explicitly that Apple and Google are not sponsors and are not involved, gives a fixed number of winners, a fixed entry deadline and a fixed prize award date, and discloses odds if selection is random. Provide an always-available free entry path open to non-subscribers with identical weighting — this is what keeps the scheme outside Lotteries (Regulation) Act 1998 s.2(b), which requires 'purchasing tickets'. Cap fair market value of any single prize at ≤₹10,000 to stay under the s.194B per-transaction TDS trigger. Never describe a contest as solving a puzzle based on arrangement/combination/permutation of letters, words or figures — that wording pulls it into the Prize Competitions Act 1955 with its ₹1,000/month and 2,000-entry caps.
- DEFAULT PRIZES TO NON-MONETARY, NON-TRANSFERABLE RECOGNITION WE PRODUCE OURSELVES. Rank order of legal and tax safety: (1) in-app only — extra coins, a permanent profile badge, a named 'Chapter Champion' title, priority doubt-resolution slot; (2) our own service — 3 months of premium subscription credited to that account, non-transferable, no cash value (per CBIC Circular 243/37/2024-GST this is a voucher-type actionable claim, not a supply at issue, with GST only on the underlying supply at nil consideration); (3) an experience with negligible market value — a 30-minute 1:1 call with a subject mentor, a personalised performance report, a physical certificate; (4) LAST RESORT and only with full TDS machinery — physical goods. Never award cash, UPI transfers, or third-party gift cards. This keeps us clear of s.194B compliance, of the SC's Gameskraft 'actionable claim' reasoning, and of app-store RMG policies simultaneously.
- SHIP A BUCKETED, PSEUDONYMOUS, PERSONAL-BEST-FIRST LEADERBOARD — NEVER A GLOBAL RANK OUT OF 10,000. Concretely: assign each student to a league bucket of 20-30 peers matched on recent accuracy/attempt volume (Redis sorted sets keyed per bucket per week, which also solves the 10k-concurrent scaling problem); show only the local window with promotion/relegation between buckets; display pseudonymous handles by default with real-name display strictly opt-in and blocked for under-18s; make the primary always-visible metric a personal-improvement track (percentile delta vs your own last 5 attempts, chapter mastery %), with the competitive track opt-in and switchable off permanently in one tap. Add a floor rule: never render a message that tells a student they are last, that they dropped N ranks, or that peers overtook them. This follows Li et al. (2024) on relative vs absolute leaderboards and pre-empts the Rajasthan Act s.12(viii)/(ix) direction of travel.
- BUILD STREAKS HUMANELY AND FUND FORGIVENESS WITH EFFORT, NEVER MONEY. Auto-apply streak protection without asking the user to make a loss-framed decision (Duolingo's stated principle). Grant 2 free freezes, replenishing weekly, plus 1 bonus freeze at every 30-day milestone. Provide a free effort-based repair: complete 2 practice sets within 48 hours to restore. Explicitly implement 'planned rest': let a student schedule up to 4 rest days per month (exam days, illness, festivals) that preserve the streak with zero cost. Never sell an instant streak repair. Cap streak reminders at one per day, hard-muted 23:00-06:00, and never use confirm-shaming copy on opt-out (CCPA Dark Patterns 2023 lists confirm shaming and nagging among the 13). Reset messaging must be neutral and forward-looking, e.g. 'New streak started — day 1'.
- SEGMENT THE ENTIRE REWARDS ENGINE BY DECLARED AGE AND RUN A DETERMINISTIC, NON-PERSONALISED VARIANT FOR UNDER-18s. DPDP Act s.9(3) prohibits tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising directed at children regardless of parental consent, and the DPDP Rules 2025 (notified 13 Nov 2025, enforceable 13 May 2027) define child as under 18 — which is most of our user base. For under-18 accounts: fixed public earn rates identical for every user, no ML-optimised nudge timing, no personalised coin offers or promotions, no third-party analytics SDKs, no ad personalisation, and no behavioural profiling tables. Reserve any personalisation, cohort targeting, or engagement-optimised push scheduling for verified 18+ users. Start collecting date of birth at signup now and design the verifiable-parental-consent flow (parent-as-verified-user, or DigiLocker virtual token per Rule 10) on a roadmap that lands well before May 2027. Penalty exposure is up to ₹200 crore.
- TIE COIN EARNING TO EFFORTFUL, LOW-INTRINSIC-INTEREST BEHAVIOURS AND MAKE REWARDS INFORMATIONAL RATHER THAN CONTROLLING. Per the overjustification literature, do not pay students for what they already enjoy. Reward: reviewing every wrong answer with a written note, attempting a chapter's hardest PYQ tier, completing a full sectioned mock without pausing, returning to a previously failed topic and clearing it. Target roughly 5-15 coins per meaningful action, ~100-200/day for a diligent student. Pair every coin award with an informational sentence about what improved ('Rotational Motion accuracy 61% → 82%'), so the reward carries competence feedback rather than pure control. Balance faucets against sinks priced so a week of consistent study buys roughly one meaningful sink, add a lifetime balance cap and 180-day inactivity decay (legally safe only because coins are never purchased — Apple 3.1.1 forbids expiry of IAP-purchased currency, which is a further reason not to sell them).
- RUN THE REWARDS UI THROUGH A DOCUMENTED DARK-PATTERN SELF-AUDIT BEFORE LAUNCH AND KEEP THE ARTEFACT. Check explicitly against all 13 CCPA-named patterns, with special attention to: false urgency (no fake countdowns on coin offers or contest entries — also prohibited as 'artificial urgency or scarcity' by the CCPA Coaching Guidelines 2024), confirm shaming (no 'No, I don't care about my rank' opt-outs), nagging (notification frequency caps), forced action (never gate solutions, analytics, or contest entry behind mandatory referral or social sharing), and subscription trap (cancellation must take the same number of taps as signup). If subscriptions are sold outside the app stores, implement RBI e-mandate obligations directly: AFA at registration, 24-hour pre-debit notification carrying merchant name, amount and date with an opt-out, and a secure cancel/modify link in every notification. Never frame cancellation as forfeiting accumulated coins or streaks.
- DESIGN THE REFERRAL LOOP TO PAY IN UTILITY, WITH ANTI-FRAUD GATES AND NO CASH LEG. Referral rewards must be coins or in-app utility only — never cash, never third-party vouchers, never a share of subscription revenue, since a cash referral leg reintroduces real-world monetary value into a promotional mechanic. Gate the reward on a genuine activation event (referred user completes 3 full mock tests over ≥7 distinct days), not on install or signup, to defeat SIM-farm and self-referral abuse. Enforce one device per account, phone-number uniqueness, and a server-side per-account referral cap (e.g. 10/month). Compute and award referral coins exclusively in a Supabase Edge Function with service-role access — never trust the client — and log every award in the same immutable coin_ledger so the whole economy is auditable in one place.
- SHIP AN EXPLICIT STUDENT-WELLBEING LAYER AND KEEP GAMIFICATION OFF THE MOCK-TEST RESULT SCREEN. Given NEET-linked suicides rising from 4 (2021) to ≥32 (2025) and 13,999 NCRB student suicides in 2022, treat this as a product requirement, not CSR. Concretely: confine coins, streaks and leagues to low-stakes practice (where gamified assessment demonstrably reduces test anxiety) and present mock-test results in a calm, analytical, reward-free UI; add a session-length cap with a genuine break prompt and no penalty for taking it; expose a persistent, one-tap link to Tele-MANAS / a national helpline from the results and profile screens; suppress all notifications 23:00-06:00; never create a mechanic that rewards studying late or unbroken daily activity without rest; and never use ASCI-prohibited creative depicting students sacrificing sleep or meals. Add an admin-side analytics alert for sharp sustained performance decline that routes to human outreach rather than an automated 'you're falling behind' push.
PITFALLS:
- TREATING 'SKILL-BASED' AS A LEGAL DEFENCE. It no longer is where anything is paid. PROGA 2025 s.2(i) captures money games 'irrespective of whether such games are based on skill or chance', and the Supreme Court in DGGI v. Gameskraft (2026 INSC 595, 27 May 2026) held skill vs chance is irrelevant for GST once money is staked. Any internal argument of the form 'our contest is skill-based so it's fine' is obsolete post-August 2025 and must be struck from product and legal reasoning.
- RESTRICTING GIVEAWAY ENTRY TO PAID SUBSCRIBERS. This is the single most likely accidental violation. It reintroduces the 'consideration' element required by Lotteries (Regulation) Act 1998 s.2(b), converting a harmless lucky draw into an unlicensed private lottery — and only State Governments may run lotteries. It also arguably converts the subscription fee into a stake under PROGA s.2(i), since the user pays and expects enrichment. Equally dangerous variants: 'premium members get 3x entries', 'spend 500 coins to enter' where coins were ever purchasable, and 'entry requires an active subscription on draw date'.
- SELLING COINS VIA IN-APP PURCHASE. This one decision cascades into four separate problems at once: (a) purchased coins become 'other stakes' under PROGA if they can win anything, exposing us to 3 years imprisonment and ₹1 crore; (b) Apple 3.1.1 forbids IAP-purchased currency from ever expiring, destroying our inflation control and forcing perpetual liability; (c) Apple 5.3.3 bars IAP currency used with real money gaming of any kind; (d) it creates a GST supply and an arguable actionable-claim exposure under the Gameskraft reasoning. Sell subscriptions and content. Never sell the reward currency.
- AWARDING PRIZES ABOVE ₹10,000 WITHOUT TDS MACHINERY — ESPECIALLY PRIZES IN KIND TO MINORS. From 1 April 2025 the s.194B aggregate threshold was replaced by a per-single-transaction ₹10,000 test, and the rate is 30% + 4% cess = 31.2%. For wholly-in-kind prizes the payer must ensure tax has been paid before releasing the prize. A ₹40,000 laptop giveaway means ₹12,480 of tax, PAN collection, Form 26Q filing and Form 16A issuance — for a 17-year-old who probably has no PAN. Marketing will propose exactly this prize because it converts. Cap FMV at ₹10,000 or do not run it.
- SHIPPING A GLOBAL PUBLIC LEADERBOARD BECAUSE IT DEMOS WELL. Absolute all-participant leaderboards are shown to demoralise the bottom, exacerbate destructive competition, raise measured stress and cause disengagement — and 80%+ of 10,000 students are structurally in the bottom. Rajasthan's Coaching Centres Act 2025 s.12(viii)/(ix) already bars coaching centres from making assessment results public or segregating batches by performance, so a public rank wall is simultaneously a retention risk, a wellbeing risk, and a compliance failure for any Rajasthan institutional customer.
- BUILDING BEHAVIOURALLY-PERSONALISED RETENTION MECHANICS FOR A USER BASE THAT IS MOSTLY UNDER 18. Optimised push timing, personalised coin offers, churn-prediction-triggered nudges and cohort-targeted promotions are standard growth practice — and DPDP Act s.9(3) prohibits tracking, behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising directed at children outright, with no consent-based cure and up to ₹200 crore in penalties. Building the personalised engine first and retrofitting age segmentation before 13 May 2027 will be far more expensive than segmenting from day one.
- IMPORTING DUOLINGO'S MONETISED FORGIVENESS AND URGENCY MECHANICS WHOLESALE. Paid instant streak repair (~200 gems), countdown-pressured offers, and loss-framed 'your streak is about to die' interstitials are precisely what CCPA's Dark Patterns Guidelines 2023 name as false urgency, confirm shaming and nagging, and what the CCPA Coaching Guidelines 2024 prohibit as 'artificial urgency or scarcity'. Duolingo's transferable insight is auto-applied, effort-repayable, bounded forgiveness — not its monetisation of loss aversion. Copying the wrong half puts us in front of a regulator that has already fined 18 coaching institutes ₹54.60 lakh.
- ASSUMING THE LAW IS SETTLED AND HARDCODING AGAINST TODAY'S RULES. The constitutional challenge to PROGA 2025 is pending before a larger Supreme Court bench; the 2026 Rules empower the Central Government to notify categories of online social games into mandatory registration (rr.8-10) and to direct determinations at any time; and MeitY can issue directions to anyone facilitating an online game, including app stores, payment processors and advertisers. Build the rewards layer as a feature-flagged, independently disableable module with a documented kill-switch, so a notification or an adverse ruling is a config change rather than a re-architecture.
- REDEEMING COINS FOR THIRD-PARTY GIFT CARDS OR VOUCHERS AS A 'SAFE' MIDDLE GROUND. It is not safe — it is the worst option. An Amazon or Flipkart voucher has an unambiguous rupee face value, which makes the coin 'equivalent or convertible to money' and squarely within PROGA's 'other stakes'. It also creates GST and TDS characterisation questions that redemption into our own subscription simply does not. Keep every sink inside our own product boundary.
- RELYING ON IEC MEMBERSHIP OR ASCI ALIGNMENT AS A COMPLIANCE SHIELD. Both are voluntary self-regulation. The binding enforcement comes from CCPA under the Consumer Protection Act 2019 (already issuing notices and penalties in the coaching sector), MeitY and the Online Gaming Authority under PROGA, the Data Protection Board under DPDP, and the tax authorities. Self-regulatory alignment is a useful design template and a trust signal; it is not a defence.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- Does an edtech app that runs mock tests and leaderboards constitute an 'online game' at all within PROGA 2025 s.2? If it is not an online game, the entire money-game/social-game framework is inapplicable and only general law (lotteries, prize competitions, consumer protection) governs. If it IS an online game, the r.16-17 India-resident data-retention and r.20 grievance obligations bite. No source retrieved resolves this for a pure assessment platform — needs a written opinion from Indian gaming counsel (Trilegal, Khaitan, Cyril Amarchand, or Lakshmikumaran & Sridharan all publish in this area).
- Has the Central Government notified, or signalled intent to notify, any category of online social games for mandatory registration under the 2026 Rules? If 'educational/quiz platforms' were ever notified, banks would be required to verify our registration status before enabling transactions, which is a payment-rail dependency. Requires monitoring the MeitY gazette and Online Gaming Authority of India notifications on an ongoing basis.
- Does a subscription-gated leaderboard where the prize is only additional in-app content constitute 'paying fees in expectation of other enrichment' under PROGA s.2(i)? The definition says 'monetary or other enrichment' — the scope of 'other enrichment' is untested. A conservative reading would prohibit even non-monetary prizes for subscribers. Needs counsel; in the interim, keep contest entry open to free-tier users.
- What is the actual applicability of the CCPA Coaching Sector Guidelines 2024 to a pure online app? CCPA stated on 8 Jan 2024 they apply 'whether online or physical' but the notified text omits 'online', and the definition is centre-based ('providing coaching to more than fifty students'). Also unresolved: whether an app with no classes falls within Rajasthan's s.2(d) 'coaching centre' definition (>100 students). Both determine whether the ban on publishing assessment results reaches us.
- What is the full text of the India EdTech Consortium Code of Conduct — specifically whether it mandates a cooling-off/refund window, a two-step verification before enrolment of minors, and any provisions touching gamification or rewards? Could not be retrieved from public sources. Obtain directly from IAMAI before making any public claim of IEC alignment.
- Exact clause numbering and full text of the ASCI guidelines for advertising of educational institutions, programmes and platforms, and resolution of the effective-date discrepancy (ASCI's own index says 9 March 2023; contemporaneous reporting says applicable from 1 September 2023). The PDF was not retrievable. Needed before signing off any results-based marketing creative.
- Exact monetary penalties under the Consumer Protection Act 2019 for misleading advertisement as applied by CCPA (commonly cited as up to ₹10 lakh first offence and up to ₹50 lakh for subsequent, with 1-3 year endorser bans) — cited from memory here, not verified in the sources retrieved. Verify against the bare Act before relying on the figures for risk quantification.
- Whether streak-reminder push notifications timed by a model trained on a user's past engagement constitutes 'behavioural monitoring' of a child under DPDP s.9(3), or whether it falls within the educational-institution exemption for tracking in the interest of the child. This is the single most consequential unresolved question for our retention roadmap and should go to privacy counsel before the notification engine is built.
- Whether awarding coins that can be redeemed for a subscription extension creates a GST 'supply' at the point of redemption, and at what value (nil consideration vs open market value under CGST valuation rules for related/nil-consideration supplies). CBIC Circular 243/37/2024-GST resolves the voucher-transaction question but not the valuation of a free-earned redemption against our own service. Needs indirect-tax counsel.
- Current status and next listing date of the constitutional challenge to PROGA 2025 before the larger Supreme Court bench, and whether any interim orders have modified the operation of the Act since 1 May 2026. Should be tracked continuously via Supreme Court Observer for the life of the product.

## admin-authoring
SUMMARY: The admin side is the real product; the student app is the shop window. Three hard constraints dominate. (1) Immutability: every serious item bank (Moodle, Canvas, QTI-conformant systems) versions items rather than mutating them — a quiz/attempt must pin a specific item version, because psychometric statistics and score defensibility are only valid against the exact rendered version. (2) Ingestion economics: math OCR is the bottleneck, and the 2026 price/accuracy frontier is Mathpix Convert API at $0.002/image and $0.005/PDF-page (verified vendor pricing) versus open-source Surya 2 (650M params, olmOCR-bench 83.3, 5.35 pages/s on an RTX 5090) whose *weights* are Rail-M-licensed and free only for startups under $5M funding/revenue — a commercial licence is required past that, which most teams miss. Generic Document AI (Google Read $0.65/1k pages, Azure Read $1.50/1k pages) is the wrong tool: neither emits LaTeX. The realistic 2026 pipeline is a two-stage hybrid: Mathpix or Surya for layout+LaTeX, then a vision LLM pass to segment the page into structured question JSON (options, answer key, diagram crops). (3) Psychometrics as a first-class schema, not a report: QTI 3.0's Usage Data spec already gives a ready-made vocabulary (P-value, PTbis, A/B/C/D-Param, AIS, plus per-distractor NumberChoosingResponse/PercentChoosingResponse/AISResponse/PTbis-Response) that maps almost 1:1 onto Postgres tables. Empirical thresholds are well established (discrimination ≤0.20 poor, ≥0.35 excellent; non-functioning distractor <5% selection), but a 2025 study of 14,238 items found static thresholds caught only 85.7% of genuinely flawed items — so auto-flagging must route to a human reviewer queue, never auto-retire. The two-role RBAC model (ADMIN/STUDENT) is the single biggest architectural mistake in the brief: an item bank at 10k-student scale needs at minimum AUTHOR, REVIEWER, SUBJECT_LEAD (approver), OPS/SUPPORT and ANALYST, because "who approved this item" is the audit trail you need when a student disputes a mock-test key. Copyright is more favourable than expected for exam use specifically — Section 52(1)(i) of the Indian Copyright Act 1957 exempts reproduction "as part of the questions to be answered in an examination" — but that exemption does not cleanly extend to a commercial app republishing whole PYQ papers with solutions, and coaching-institute material is straightforwardly infringing. Bilingual is a hard NTA requirement (13 NEET languages; bilingual booklet with English authoritative on translation ambiguity), which means language must be a column on the item, not a separate item.
FINDINGS:
- (high) Mathpix Convert API: exact 2026 pricing and hard limits: Verified from vendor pricing page. Image service (v3/text, v3/latex, v3/batch): $0.002/image for 0–1M images, $0.0015/image beyond 1M. PDF service (v3/pdf): $0.005/page for 0–1M pages, $0.0035/page beyond. One-time $19.99 setup fee; $29 free testing credit on account creation; billed 1st of month. Strokes: free to 1K sessions, then $0.01/session. Limits: 5 MB JSON body for image endpoints, 512 KB for v3/strokes, 1 GB PDF upload, 10 MB image download from URL with 15s timeout; Files API allows 15 GB/file and 200,000 files/job. Quota exceeded returns HTTP 429 with error ID `http_max_requests`. Monthly quotas are configurable via `image_monthly_limit` and `page_monthly_limit`; the page quota is SHARED between v3/pdf and the Files API. Per-minute per-app and per-IP rate limits exist but vary by plan and are not published. Snip (end-user app) Pro from $4.99/mo; Files API from $1.00/1k pages. Mathpix states it processes >10M images/day at 99.9%+ uptime but publishes NO accuracy percentage. => IMPLICATION: Budget: ingesting 50,000 scanned coaching pages costs ~$250 via v3/pdf — trivially affordable, so build-vs-buy should not be decided on API cost. The real cost is human verification. The shared page quota between v3/pdf and Files API is a trap for a bulk-ingest admin tool that uses both. Because Mathpix publishes no accuracy figure, do not promise admins an accuracy SLA; instead instrument your own per-batch correction rate.
- (high) Open-source math OCR: real benchmark numbers, throughput, and a licence trap: olmOCR-Bench = 1,403 PDFs / 7,010 unit tests with strict visual-equivalence checks on LaTeX via KaTeX DOM bounding boxes. Current leaderboard (late 2025 / 2026): Infinity-Parser2-Pro 87.6, chandra-ocr-2 85.8, dots.mocr 83.9, surya-ocr-2 83.3, LightOnOCR-2-1B 83.2. Surya 2: 650M params, 90+ languages, returns equations wrapped in <math>...</math>, 5.35 pages/s on RTX 5090 (vllm, concurrency 128, 32 GB VRAM) vs 0.108 pages/s on Apple Silicon via llama.cpp. Marker (built on Surya): balanced mode 76.0 overall on olmOCR-bench and 83.5 on born-digital PDFs, fast mode 66.6; throughput on a single B200: 2.9 pages/s balanced, 7.4 fast, 23.7 fast-no-OCR; outputs Markdown/JSON/HTML/chunks with LaTeX equations; `--use_llm` and `--redo_inline_math` hybrid mode delegates inline math to Gemini/Claude/OpenAI/Ollama for highest quality. LICENCE TRAP: for both Surya and Marker the CODE is Apache 2.0 but the WEIGHTS are a modified AI Pubs Open Rail-M licence, free only for research, personal use, and startups under $5M funding/revenue — a paid commercial licence is required above that. pix2tex/LaTeX-OCR is MIT and unencumbered but reports only BLEU 0.88, normalised edit distance 0.10, token accuracy 0.60, and handles single formulas, not full pages. => IMPLICATION: A self-hosted Marker/Surya ingest worker on one GPU processes ~10k pages/hour — enough to digitise an entire coaching institute's archive in days. But the Rail-M weight licence means a funded ed-tech company must either buy a commercial licence or fall back to MIT-licensed pix2tex (formula-only, materially worse) or a paid API. Token accuracy of 0.60 for pix2tex means ~40% of formulas need an edit — unusable without a review UI. Budget for a GPU worker on GCP (the brief already includes GCP) rather than trying to OCR on Supabase Edge Functions, which have no GPU.
- (medium) Vision-LLM ingestion is now cost-competitive and is the only approach that produces structured question JSON in one pass: 2026 aggregator benchmarks: Gemini 3 Flash ~$0.50/M input + $3.00/M output tokens, working out to roughly $2.25 per 1,000 pages for OCR/extraction; a 200-page document costs ~$0.73 on Gemini vs ~$0.98 on Claude. At 1,000 × 50-page documents: Gemini ~$24, Claude ~$270, GPT ~$218; Batch APIs cut all of these ~50%. Claude Sonnet 4.6 reported at 97.6% field-extraction accuracy, ~3.8 points above Gemini on complex documents. These figures come from third-party comparison blogs, not vendor benchmark pages — treat the accuracy numbers as indicative, not audited. Crucially, dedicated OCR (Mathpix, Surya) returns a page of markup; a vision LLM can return {stem_latex, options[4], correct_index, chapter_guess, difficulty_guess, has_diagram, diagram_bbox, pyq_year} in one call. => IMPLICATION: The right architecture is two-stage, not either/or: (1) Mathpix or Surya for high-fidelity LaTeX and layout, (2) a vision-LLM pass over the page image PLUS the OCR text to segment into individual items and populate metadata. Feeding the LLM both the image and the OCR text materially reduces LaTeX hallucination versus image-only. At ~$2/1k pages the LLM stage is not the cost driver; it is a rounding error against editor salaries. Use Batch API for archival back-catalogue ingestion (50% saving, latency irrelevant) and sync calls only for the admin's interactive 'paste a page, get questions' flow.
- (medium) Generic Document AI (Google/Azure) is the wrong tool for STEM items — pricing confirms it is priced for forms, not formulas: Google Document AI 2026: Read API (OCR only) $0.65/1,000 pages; Layout and prebuilt models $10/1,000 pages; Custom Extraction $30/1,000 pages; neural model training $3/hour after 10 free hours/month. Azure Document Intelligence 2026: Read $1.50/1,000 pages; Layout $10/1,000; Prebuilt $10/1,000; Custom Extraction $30/1,000; same $3/hour training. Reported hidden overhead of 25–40% (GCP) and 25–35% (Azure) once storage, orchestration and 40–80 hours of integration engineering are counted. Neither service emits LaTeX or MathML for inline mathematics — they are optimised for invoices, receipts, W-2s and forms. => IMPLICATION: Do not use Document AI for question ingestion. It would be defensible only for a non-question admin flow — e.g. bulk-parsing student ID documents or institutional enrolment forms. The $30/1k Custom Extraction tier is 15× Mathpix's PDF rate for a worse result on mathematics. Since the stack already includes GCP, the sensible GCP role is GPU compute for a self-hosted Marker/Surya worker plus Cloud Storage, not Document AI.
- (high) QTI 3.0 Usage Data spec is a ready-made psychometrics schema — copy it verbatim into Postgres: The 1EdTech QTI 3.0 Usage Data & Item Statistics binding defines exactly the vocabulary an admin analytics layer needs. Ordinary (item-level) statistics: A-Param (IRT discrimination), B-Param (IRT difficulty), C-Param (guessing/lower asymptote), D-Param (dispersion), P-value (percent answering correctly — explicitly noted as group-dependent), AIS (average item score), PTbis (point-biserial), PHI, rbis (biserial), Polyserial, Score_Conversion, and Fifths_Table variants (Highest, Second_Highest, Middle, Second_Lowest, Lowest). Categorized (per-distractor) statistics: NumberChoosingResponse, PercentChoosingResponse, AISResponse, PTbis-Response. XML root element is `usageData` with `ordinaryStatistic` and `categorizedStatistic` children; attributes are name (required), context (URI identifying the population/administration the statistic was computed over), glossary, caseCount, stdError, stdDeviation, lastUpdated. Statistics attach via `targetObject` with identifier, optional partIdentifier, and objectType ∈ {test, testpart, section, item, outcome, interaction, choice}. => IMPLICATION: Model item statistics as (item_version_id, context_id, statistic_name, value, case_count, std_error, computed_at) rather than as columns on the questions table. The `context` attribute is the critical design lesson: the same item has different P-values for JEE Main vs NEET cohorts and for 2025 vs 2026 batches, so statistics must be scoped to a cohort/administration, never global. objectType also legitimises attaching statistics to a whole test, a section, or a single choice — which is exactly what distractor analysis needs. Adopting these names verbatim gives free QTI export later and stops the team inventing a private vocabulary.
- (high) QTI 3.0 deliberately EXCLUDES authored 'difficulty' from its metadata profile — authored difficulty and empirical difficulty must be separate fields: The QTI 3.0 Metadata binding profiles IEEE LOM but explicitly excludes the LOM fields 'Interactivity Type', 'Difficulty' and 'Typical Age Range' as unsuitable for assessment resources. QTI-specific metadata elements that ARE defined: itemTemplate (boolean — item varies by random/external factors), timeDependent (boolean — scoring considers attempt duration), composite (boolean — multiple interactions in one item), interactionType (unbounded enumeration of 22 types including choiceInteraction, textEntryInteraction, extendedTextInteraction), portableCustomInteractionContext, feedbackType (adaptive|nonadaptive|none), solutionAvailable (boolean), scoringMode (human|externalmachine|responseprocessing), and toolName/toolVersion/toolVendor (String256). LOM sections retained: General (Identifier, Title, Language, Description, Keyword), Lifecycle (Version, Status, Contribute), Technical, Educational (Learning Resource Type restricted to exercise/questionnaire, Context, Typical Learning Time), Rights (Cost, Copyright, Description), Classification. => IMPLICATION: The brief's content model tags questions by 'difficulty'. Split this into two columns: `authored_difficulty` (EASY/MEDIUM/HARD, set by the author, used for blueprint assembly before any attempt data exists) and `empirical_difficulty_p` (computed, per-cohort, authoritative once caseCount ≥ threshold). Admin UI should show both side by side — the delta is itself a quality signal, and a systematic gap tells you an author mis-calibrates. Also adopt Lifecycle.Status for the editorial workflow state and Rights.Copyright for provenance (see the copyright finding) — both are standard fields you would otherwise invent. `timeDependent` and `Typical Learning Time` map directly onto the per-question expected-time field you need for the student-facing time analytics.
- (high) Concrete item-flagging thresholds, and hard evidence that static thresholds alone are insufficient: Rezigalla et al. 2024 bands: difficulty index — easy ≥78%, acceptable 26–75%, difficult <25%. Discrimination index — poor ≤0.20, acceptable 0.21–0.24, good 0.25–0.34, excellent ≥0.35. Non-functioning distractor (NFD) defined as any distractor selected by <5% of examinees; distractor efficiency graded excellent (0 NFDs), good (1), acceptable (2), poor (3). Distractor efficiency correlates negatively with difficulty (r = −0.548, p = 0.000) and with discrimination (r = −0.259, p = 0.0476). A 2025 six-year study (Faculty of Medicine Siriraj Hospital, Mahidol University) analysed 14,238 items across 236 exams (2017–2022) using p-value < 0.25 OR point-biserial < 0 as auto-flag rules: 0.6% of all items ultimately required correction, but 25% of exams contained at least one flawed item. Detection performance: the combined thresholds caught ~85.7% of genuinely flawed items and MISSED 14.3% (which had p ≥ 0.25 and r_pb ≥ 0); p-value alone caught ~80%, point-biserial alone only ~50%. Authors conclude static thresholds are insufficient and must be paired with expert review. Separately, in CTT practice distractors are flagged when chosen by <5% (non-functioning) or >30% (over-attractive), and the correct option should have positive point-biserial while every distractor should have negative point-biserial — a distractor with positive point-biserial is the classic signature of a miskeyed item. => IMPLICATION: Ship an auto-flag rule engine with these exact defaults, but wire it to a REVIEW QUEUE, not to auto-retirement. Concretely: flag if (p < 0.25) OR (r_pb < 0) OR (any distractor r_pb > correct-option r_pb → suspected miskey) OR (any distractor selected <5% → dead distractor) OR (any distractor selected >30% AND is not the key → ambiguity). Gate all of it behind caseCount ≥ 100 for stable classical statistics. Set expectations with stakeholders using the 0.6%/25% figures: a mature bank yields very few broken items, but almost every full-length mock will contain one — so the SLA is 'flagged within 24h of the test closing', not 'zero flawed items'.
- (high) Test-level reliability: alpha ≥0.80 is the high-stakes bar, and SEM is arguably the better metric to surface to admins: Nunnally (1978) established 0.70 as the lowest acceptable reliability for basic research and 0.80 as adequate; >0.80 good, >0.90 excellent. For examinations the traditionally accepted minimum is 0.80, described as the benchmark below which an exam or elements within it should not fall. SEM (standard error of measurement) is a direct function of alpha: SEM = SD × sqrt(1 − alpha). Analysis of MRCP(UK) examinations argues SEM is a more appropriate quality measure than reliability for postgraduate assessment, because alpha is inflated by test length and by the spread of the candidate population. Note the 0.70 convention has been criticised as a flawed metric for diagnostic assessments. => IMPLICATION: Compute Cronbach's alpha (or KR-20 for dichotomous items) per test AND per section, and display SEM in raw-mark units next to it — SEM is what actually answers an admin's question 'is this student's rank trustworthy?'. Critical caveat for this product: alpha rises with item count, so a 25-question chapter practice set will show a much lower alpha than a 180-question full NEET mock, and admins WILL misread that as a quality regression. Either normalise via Spearman-Brown or refuse to display alpha below a minimum item count (~20). Also compute alpha on a per-cohort basis — a homogeneous top-rankers-only batch will show depressed alpha through range restriction, not through bad items.
- (high) Item versioning: Moodle's model is the reference implementation to copy: In Moodle 4.5 every edit or update to a question creates a new version; the version number is visible in the question bank, and 'History' in the Actions dropdown lists all versions, each of which can be previewed, edited and deleted independently. In Moodle 4.5 and earlier, questions live in one question bank per course organised into categories/subcategories at course, course-category or system context, and changes to a question are reflected everywhere it is used. Moodle 5.0 (2025) changed this substantially: a course can now hold multiple named question banks under Course navigation > More > Question banks, and questions previously held in course context are migrated into an auto-created '[Course name] shared question bank' on upgrade — an upgrade that has caused visible user reports of banks appearing empty or relocating. QTI 3.0 best practice reinforces the identity discipline: the identifier attribute must be a non-null unique string, and organisations should add organisational prefixes to identifiers to guarantee uniqueness across vendors. => IMPLICATION: Copy Moodle's version-history model, but make one improvement Moodle only partially enforces: a delivered test must reference a specific `question_version_id`, never a mutable `question_id`. Schema: `questions` (stable identity, slug with org prefix) → `question_versions` (immutable content, version_no, status, created_by, approved_by, published_at) → `test_questions` (pins question_version_id) → `attempts.responses` (pins question_version_id). This makes 'never mutate a question that has been attempted' a foreign-key invariant rather than a policy. Practical rule to encode: an edit that changes the stem, any option, or the key MUST fork a new version and reset all statistics; a metadata-only edit (chapter re-tag, PYQ year correction, typo in the solution text) may update in place. Also learn from Moodle 5.0's painful migration: get the bank-scoping model right before you have 50,000 items, because rehoming a question bank later is a support nightmare.
