- (high) Section 52(1)(i) of the Indian Copyright Act 1957 — exact statutory wording for the exam exemption: Verbatim: "the reproduction of any work—(i) by a teacher or a pupil in the course of instruction; or (ii) as part of the questions to be answered in an examination; or (iii) in answers to such questions" is not an infringement of copyright. This is the education/examination carve-out in Section 52(1)(i). Section 52 as a whole is a purpose-specific limitation covering private/personal use including research, criticism, review, reporting of current events, judicial proceedings and education — India follows enumerated 'fair dealing', not open-ended US-style 'fair use', so a purpose not listed is not exempt regardless of how transformative or non-commercial it is. => IMPLICATION: Three distinct legal postures, which the product must model as a `source_provenance` field on every item: (a) ORIGINAL — authored in-house, fully owned, no risk; (b) PYQ — a genuine previous-year JEE/NEET question. These are published by NTA at nta.ac.in/Downloads for free public download, and reproducing an exam question 'as part of the questions to be answered in an examination' sits closest to the statutory exemption. However, a commercial subscription app is NOT a teacher, and reproducing an entire past paper wholesale with commentary is publishing, not instruction — so treat PYQs as low-but-nonzero risk and never as certainly clear. (c) THIRD-PARTY COACHING MATERIAL (Allen, Aakash, FIITJEE, Resonance, PW question banks, and their solution PDFs) — original literary works owned by those institutes; copying is straightforward infringement with no plausible Section 52 defence for a competing commercial product. Enforce this in the admin UI: ingestion from a scanned coaching PDF must force the uploader to declare provenance, and items marked as third-party-sourced must be blocked from publish until rewritten and re-approved.
- (high) Bilingual/multilingual is a hard NTA requirement with specific semantics, not a nice-to-have: NEET UG is offered in 13 media: English, Hindi, Urdu, plus Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Kannada, Malayalam, Marathi, Odia, Punjabi, Tamil and Telugu. Candidates choosing English receive an English-only booklet; candidates choosing any other medium receive a BILINGUAL test booklet containing every question in both the chosen language and English. Official rule: "In case of any ambiguity in translation, the English version is considered final." Language availability is geographically restricted by centre state — Assamese only in Assam; Bengali in West Bengal, Tripura and Andaman & Nicobar; Gujarati in Gujarat, Daman & Diu, Dadra & Nagar Haveli; Kannada in Karnataka; Malayalam in Kerala and Lakshadweep; Marathi in Maharashtra; Odia in Odisha; Punjabi in Punjab, Chandigarh and Delhi; Tamil in Tamil Nadu and Puducherry; Telugu in Andhra Pradesh and Telangana; English/Hindi/Urdu nationwide. Medium is fixed at registration and cannot be changed. QTI 3.0 best practice for multilingual items is the xml:lang attribute plus bidirectional text (bdo) support, and shared passages are referenced via qti-assessment-stimulus-ref rather than duplicated per item. => IMPLICATION: Data model consequence: translations are versioned children of a question_version, NOT sibling questions — schema `question_version_translations (question_version_id, lang_code, stem, options[], solution, translated_by, reviewed_by, status)`, with a DB constraint that a translation cannot be published unless its parent English version is published, and that editing the English version invalidates (does not silently keep) all translations. Encode 'English is authoritative' literally: the answer key, marks and correct_index live ONLY on the English row; translations carry presentation text only. Render bilingually in the same screen (English block above regional block) to mirror the real booklet rather than as a language toggle — students sit the real exam in that format. JEE Main is bilingual English/Hindi plus regional options; NEET's 13 languages make full coverage a 13× editorial cost, so scope Hindi+English at launch and treat regional languages as a per-state expansion decision driven by the geographic restriction table above.
- (high) Blueprint-driven automated test assembly is a solved optimisation problem — use MILP, not ad-hoc SQL: Automated Test Assembly (ATA) is standard practice in large-scale standardised assessment. It formulates test construction as an optimisation problem with binary item-selection variables, solved by mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) or greedy heuristics. Constraints routinely encoded: content balancing (N items per chapter/topic), test length, target difficulty/information curve, item-type quotas, inter-form overlap, item exposure control, answer-key balancing (so the key is not disproportionately option C), and word count. The Big Shadow Test (BST) method with MIP is described as the most representative approach because it accommodates complex practical constraints. There is an active literature debate (van der Linden & Li 2016 and rejoinders) on whether heuristics remain necessary now that MIP solvers are fast. => IMPLICATION: For 10k concurrent students, the constraint set is: exactly N per chapter per the JEE/NEET blueprint, difficulty distribution (e.g. 30/50/20 easy/medium/hard by empirical p, falling back to authored_difficulty for uncalibrated items), zero items used in the student's last 3 tests, PYQ-year spread, and key balancing. This is a small MILP — a few thousand binary variables — solvable in well under a second with OR-Tools CP-SAT or HiGHS in a Python/Node worker; do NOT attempt it in Postgres. CRITICAL interaction with the brief's 'randomised question order per student': randomising ORDER is safe and preserves rank comparability. Randomising CONTENT (giving different students different items) breaks fair comparison and ranking unless you either (a) assemble parallel forms with matched statistical targets and then equate scores, or (b) keep the item set identical and shuffle only presentation order and option order. For a leaderboard product, the correct default is: identical item set for a given mock, shuffled item order and shuffled options per student, seeded deterministically by (test_id, user_id) so it is reproducible for dispute resolution. Store the seed, not the permutation.
- (medium) Two roles is insufficient — the argument for AUTHOR / REVIEWER / SUBJECT_LEAD / OPS / ANALYST: Serious assessment platforms separate authoring from approval as a matter of course. Item banking vendors emphasise that as items are revised and versioned it is imperative that changes in content AND the users who made them are tracked, because if a test is challenged as unfair it can be critical to maintain question versions and the construction history of each question. TAO ships Authoring, Reporting, Rostering & Delivery and Grader as distinct modules with distinct access. QTI 3.0 carries LOM Lifecycle.Status and Lifecycle.Contribute (contributor role + entity + date) as first-class item metadata. Moodle exposes item status (draft/ready) alongside version history. Note: I could not retrieve a vendor page enumerating a canonical role list — the specific five-role decomposition below is my synthesis, not a cited standard. => IMPLICATION: Argue FOR a richer role set, but implement it as capabilities, not as a role enum. Minimum viable decomposition: AUTHOR (create/edit DRAFT versions in own subject, cannot publish), REVIEWER (peer review, can request changes, cannot approve own authored items), SUBJECT_LEAD/SME (approve → PUBLISHED, retire items, sole holder of the publish capability for a subject), OPS/SUPPORT (triage student 'report this question' flags, cannot edit content, can escalate), ANALYST (read-only across all analytics, no content mutation), plus SUPER_ADMIN. The single non-negotiable rule is separation of duties: the author of a version cannot be its approver — enforce with a CHECK constraint (approved_by <> created_by), not UI logic. In Supabase this is roles in raw_app_meta_data (admin-controlled) feeding RLS policies; do NOT use raw_user_meta_data, which is user-modifiable. Item lifecycle states: DRAFT → IN_REVIEW → CHANGES_REQUESTED → APPROVED → PUBLISHED → FLAGGED → RETIRED, with RETIRED items remaining permanently readable because past attempts reference them.
- (high) Supabase RLS performance rules that determine whether the admin console survives a 50k-item bank: Documented Supabase benchmarks: wrapping auth functions in a subselect — `using ((select auth.uid()) = user_id)` instead of `using (auth.uid() = user_id)` — yields 94–99% improvement by enabling Postgres initPlan caching (evaluated once per statement, not once per row). Adding a btree index on any column referenced in a policy: 99.94% improvement. Always specifying `TO authenticated` on policies: 99.78% improvement by skipping policy evaluation for anon. Duplicating the policy's filter in the client query (`.eq('user_id', userId)` rather than a bare `.select()`): 94.74% improvement. Rewriting policies to avoid joins back to the source table — `WHERE team_id IN (SELECT team_id FROM team_user WHERE user_id = (SELECT auth.uid()))` instead of joining — 99.78% improvement. SECURITY DEFINER functions bypass RLS on their contents for privileged joins but must never be exposed in a public schema. Roles belong in raw_app_meta_data (admin-controlled), never raw_user_meta_data (user-modifiable). JWT claims are cached until token refresh, so role/permission changes do not take effect until the token is renewed. Supabase advises against policy lists exceeding ~1,000 items without redesign. auth.uid() returns null when unauthenticated, so `USING (auth.uid() IS NOT NULL AND ...)` avoids silent failures. => IMPLICATION: Two direct consequences. (1) The JWT caching behaviour means revoking an author's publish capability does not take effect until their token refreshes — for a destructive capability like PUBLISH or RETIRE, check the role against the database inside a SECURITY DEFINER RPC rather than trusting the JWT claim. (2) An admin browsing a 50k-item bank with per-subject RLS will hit the join-in-policy antipattern immediately; precompute a `user_subject_access(user_id, subject_id)` table and write the policy as `subject_id IN (SELECT subject_id FROM user_subject_access WHERE user_id = (select auth.uid()))`. Index question_versions on (subject_id, status, updated_at) — the admin list view's natural sort.
- (medium) Duplicate detection: three complementary layers, none sufficient alone: For text/LaTeX near-duplicates: MinHash + LSH estimates Jaccard similarity from compact signatures rather than O(n²) pairwise comparison; 2025 work reports SIMD vectorisation giving 7× speedup on the intersection step and end-to-end dedup latency dropping from hours to seconds. Industry practice combines approximate matching (MinHash LSH / Jaccard) for lexical near-duplicates with semantic matching via vector embeddings for meaning-level duplicates. For images (diagrams, scanned question crops): the imagehash library provides aHash (luminance), pHash (FFT/DCT structural), dHash (pixel gradient), wHash (wavelet), colorhash (HSV distribution, position-independent) and a crop-resistant variant; default hashsize=8 gives a 64-bit hash compared by Hamming distance with a tunable threshold; the defining property is that similar inputs produce similar hashes, unlike cryptographic hashing. No quantified accuracy figures are published for imagehash — thresholds must be tuned empirically on your own corpus. => IMPLICATION: Implement three layers at ingest time, all surfaced as a 'possible duplicate' warning in the authoring UI rather than a hard block. Layer 1 — exact: SHA-256 of a normalised stem (LaTeX canonicalised, whitespace/case stripped, numbers preserved) catches straight re-uploads instantly. Layer 2 — lexical near-duplicate: MinHash-LSH over character 5-grams of the normalised stem, Jaccard ≥ ~0.8, which catches the classic 'same PYQ, different coaching institute's transcription'. Layer 3 — semantic: pgvector embedding of stem+options with cosine ≥ ~0.92, which catches numerically-varied clones of the same physics problem (same setup, different values) — a very common pattern in Indian coaching material and arguably a feature (variant families) rather than a defect. For scanned/image questions, dHash or pHash at hashsize=16 with a Hamming threshold you calibrate on ~500 known pairs. Model the outcome as a `question_relations` table with kind ∈ {EXACT_DUPLICATE, NEAR_DUPLICATE, VARIANT_OF, SUPERSEDES} so 'variant family' becomes exploitable in test assembly (never put two variants of the same problem in one paper).
- (medium) TAO and the open-source item-banking landscape as a build reference: TAO (taotesting.com) splits into Authoring ('build feature-rich online assessments based on open education standards'), Reporting, Rostering & Delivery, and Cloud Services on GCP/AWS, plus TAO Advance (delivery engine), TAO Grader (manual scoring) and TAO Insights (API data access). Licensing is a Community Edition (free, open source) plus three commercial tiers — Accelerate, Ignite, Enterprise — priced by user volume. Marketing explicitly names 'easy tools, 21st-century item banking, and smart workflows', custom interactions, multimedia, accommodations and WCAG compliance. The site does not publish which QTI version is supported nor an explicit author/reviewer/approver role breakdown. => IMPLICATION: TAO Community Edition is the closest open-source analogue to what the admin side must become, and is worth installing purely to steal its authoring IA (item form layout, bank navigation, test-part/section builder) rather than to adopt as a dependency — it is PHP/Java and cannot be embedded in a Supabase + React Native stack. The four-module split (Authoring / Reporting / Rostering & Delivery / Grading) is a good top-level navigation model for the admin console. Note the separate 'Grader' module: even in a pure-MCQ product you will eventually need manual review of numeric-answer (JEE Main NAT-type) responses where tolerance and significant-figure rules are contested.
- (high) QTI 3.0 structural concepts worth adopting even without full QTI compliance: QTI 3.0 requires a test to contain at least one testPart and every part to contain at least one section, with nested sections supported — this maps directly onto sectioned JEE/NEET papers. Choice-order randomisation is a native `shuffle` attribute on choiceInteraction. Non-linear/adaptive progression uses branch rules and preconditions. Shared reading passages or shared diagrams are referenced via `qti-assessment-stimulus-ref` and packaged once, shared across many items, rather than duplicated. The spec notes that the time a candidate is given to respond 'is important for the psychometric properties' of an item, and delivery systems enforce attempt duration limits. QTI 3.0's headline changes over 2.2 are transform-free authoring-to-delivery, HTML5-native markup, integrated APIP accessibility, and native Computer Adaptive Testing support; Portable Custom Interactions (PCI) cover technology-enhanced item types. => IMPLICATION: Adopt the testPart → section → item hierarchy verbatim; JEE Advanced papers have per-section marking schemes (partial marking, negative marking varying by section) which need a section-level scoring policy object anyway. Adopt shared stimulus as a real entity: JEE Advanced comprehension/paragraph questions and NEET assertion-reason sets share a stem across 2–3 items, and duplicating that stem is how banks drift out of sync. Do not attempt full QTI XML compliance — it is enormous — but do make the internal JSON schema mechanically convertible to QTI, because institutional buyers (schools, coaching chains) will eventually ask for import/export, and retrofitting is far more expensive than aligning names now.
RECOMMENDATIONS:
- Make item immutability a database invariant, not a convention. Three tables: `questions` (stable identity, org-prefixed slug per QTI identifier guidance), `question_versions` (immutable content blob, version_no, status, created_by, approved_by with CHECK approved_by <> created_by, published_at), and `test_questions` / `attempt_responses` that both foreign-key to `question_version_id`. Rule to encode in the API layer: any change to stem, options, or key forks a new version and zeroes statistics; metadata-only changes (chapter re-tag, PYQ year, solution typo) may update in place. A RETIRED version is never deleted — past attempts depend on it. This single decision is what makes the platform defensible when a student disputes a rank.
- Build ingestion as a two-stage hybrid with a mandatory human verification gate, and instrument the correction rate. Stage 1: Mathpix v3/pdf at $0.005/page (or a self-hosted Marker/Surya GPU worker on GCP at ~2.9–5.35 pages/s, subject to the Rail-M commercial licence above $5M revenue) to get LaTeX + layout. Stage 2: a vision LLM over the page image PLUS the stage-1 text, emitting {stem_latex, options[], correct_index, chapter_guess, difficulty_guess, has_diagram, diagram_bbox, pyq_year, source_provenance} — use the Batch API (50% cheaper) for archival back-catalogue and sync calls for the interactive admin flow. Every extracted item lands in status=DRAFT with a side-by-side original-crop vs rendered-LaTeX diff view. Track `edits_per_ingested_item` as the north-star metric for the whole content operation; it is the only honest measure of whether ingestion is actually saving money.
- Adopt the QTI 3.0 Usage Data vocabulary verbatim for psychometrics, and scope every statistic to a context. Table: `item_statistics(question_version_id, context_id, statistic_name, value, case_count, std_error, std_deviation, computed_at)` with statistic_name drawn from {P-value, PTbis, AIS, A-Param, B-Param, C-Param} and a parallel `distractor_statistics` using {NumberChoosingResponse, PercentChoosingResponse, AISResponse, PTbis-Response}. context_id identifies the cohort/administration — the same item legitimately has different p-values for a JEE Main cohort and a NEET cohort. Recompute nightly via a scheduled job over Postgres (these are cheap aggregate queries), gated on case_count >= 100. Separately, keep `authored_difficulty` and `empirical_difficulty_p` as distinct columns, since QTI 3.0 deliberately excludes authored difficulty from its metadata profile — the delta between them is itself an author-calibration signal.
- Ship the auto-flag rule engine on day one, wired to a human review queue. Default rules, all requiring case_count >= 100: p < 0.25 (too hard / possibly broken); p > 0.90 (giveaway, no discrimination); point-biserial < 0 (negative discrimination); any distractor with point-biserial exceeding the keyed option's (suspected MISKEY — highest severity, page the subject lead); any distractor selected by <5% (dead distractor); any non-key distractor selected by >30% (ambiguous item). Publish the honest expectation to stakeholders using the Siriraj 14,238-item evidence: these thresholds catch ~85.7% of genuinely flawed items and miss ~14.3%, so student 'report this question' flags are not a redundant channel — they are the recall layer that catches what statistics cannot see. Merge both signals into one triage inbox with severity ordering, and record disposition (RETAIN / REVISE / RETIRE / RESCORE) as an auditable event. RESCORE must be a real, implemented operation — dropping a bad item and recomputing every affected student's score and rank — because you will need it within the first three mocks.
- Expand RBAC to capability-based authorisation with enforced separation of duties: AUTHOR, REVIEWER, SUBJECT_LEAD (sole publisher for a subject), OPS/SUPPORT (flag triage, no content mutation), ANALYST (read-only), SUPER_ADMIN. Store roles in Supabase raw_app_meta_data (admin-controlled), never raw_user_meta_data (user-modifiable). Because JWT claims are cached until token refresh, verify destructive capabilities (PUBLISH, RETIRE, RESCORE) inside a SECURITY DEFINER RPC against the live database rather than trusting the token claim. Item lifecycle: DRAFT → IN_REVIEW → CHANGES_REQUESTED → APPROVED → PUBLISHED → FLAGGED → RETIRED. Apply Supabase's documented RLS performance rules throughout — subselect-wrapped auth.uid() (94–99% faster), TO authenticated on every policy (99.78%), indexes on every policy-referenced column (99.94%), and a precomputed user_subject_access table to avoid joins inside policies (99.78%).
- Separate order randomisation from content randomisation, and be explicit about which one the leaderboard can survive. For any ranked mock: identical item set for all students; shuffle item order within section and option order within item, seeded deterministically by hash(test_id, user_id) so any student's exact paper is reproducible months later for dispute resolution — store the seed, never the materialised permutation. Redis is the right place for the seed-to-permutation cache and the leaderboard, not for the source of truth. Only introduce genuinely different item sets (parallel forms) if you also implement score equating against a common anchor set; otherwise ranks are not comparable and you will be unable to defend them. For unranked practice, content randomisation is free and desirable.
- Implement blueprint-driven auto-assembly as a real MILP, not SQL heuristics. Model it with OR-Tools CP-SAT or HiGHS in a Python worker on GCP (a few thousand binary variables solves in well under a second). Constraints: exact item count per chapter per the JEE/NEET blueprint, difficulty distribution over empirical p with fallback to authored_difficulty for uncalibrated items, exclusion of anything the student saw in their last 3 tests, PYQ-year spread, answer-key balance, and — using the question_relations table — no two members of the same variant family in one paper. Expose it in the admin UI as a blueprint the subject lead saves and reuses, with a 'why did it pick this item' explanation panel and manual override, because auto-assembly that cannot be overridden will simply not be trusted.
- Model translations as versioned children of the English version, not as sibling questions. `question_version_translations(question_version_id, lang_code, stem, options[], solution, translated_by, reviewed_by, status)`. Answer key, marks and correct_index live ONLY on the English parent — this encodes NTA's own rule that the English version is final on translation ambiguity. Constraints: a translation cannot publish unless its parent is published, and editing the parent invalidates all translations rather than silently leaving them stale. Render bilingually stacked on one screen (English block above regional block) to mirror the real bilingual booklet rather than offering a language toggle. Scope Hindi + English for launch; treat the other 11 NEET languages as a per-state expansion decision driven by NTA's geographic centre restrictions, since each additional language is a full editorial and review cost multiplier.
- Put provenance on every item as a mandatory, non-nullable field: ORIGINAL / PYQ_NTA / LICENSED / THIRD_PARTY_UNCLEARED, with source_ref (paper, year, shift, question number). Enforce that THIRD_PARTY_UNCLEARED cannot be published — it must be rewritten and re-approved first. Section 52(1)(i) exempts reproduction 'as part of the questions to be answered in an examination', which is the strongest available position for PYQs, but a commercial subscription app is neither a teacher nor an examination body, and India uses enumerated fair dealing rather than open-ended fair use — so treat PYQ reuse as a managed risk with a takedown process, not as settled. Get a written opinion from an Indian IP counsel before launch; this is a $2–3k spend that protects the entire catalogue.
- Build the duplicate-detection triad at ingest and surface it as a warning, never a hard block: SHA-256 of the LaTeX-normalised stem for exact matches; MinHash-LSH over character 5-grams at Jaccard >= ~0.8 for transcription variants of the same PYQ; pgvector cosine >= ~0.92 for semantic clones with changed numbers. For image-based items use dHash or pHash at hashsize=16 with a Hamming threshold calibrated on ~500 hand-labelled pairs from your own corpus (imagehash publishes no accuracy figures, so vendor defaults are meaningless here). Persist results in `question_relations(a_id, b_id, kind, score)` with kind ∈ {EXACT_DUPLICATE, NEAR_DUPLICATE, VARIANT_OF, SUPERSEDES} — VARIANT_OF is an asset that feeds test assembly, not just a defect to purge.
PITFALLS:
- Letting a delivered test reference a mutable question_id. The moment an admin fixes a typo in a live question, every historical attempt's rendered content silently changes, all accumulated statistics become meaningless, and you lose the ability to reconstruct what a student actually saw. This is unrecoverable retroactively — you cannot reconstruct pre-edit content from an audit log of diffs at scale. Decide it before the first mock test ships.
- Computing a global p-value per question. Difficulty is population-dependent by definition (QTI's own spec calls P-value 'a group-dependent measure'), so a question that is p=0.70 for a NEET cohort may be p=0.30 for a JEE Advanced cohort, and a single blended number is worse than no number because admins will act on it. Every statistic needs a context/cohort scope from the first schema migration.
- Displaying Cronbach's alpha without controlling for test length or cohort homogeneity. Alpha rises mechanically with item count, so a 25-question chapter drill will look 'unreliable' next to a 180-question mock even when its items are better, and a top-rankers-only batch will show depressed alpha purely through range restriction. Admins will draw exactly the wrong conclusion. Either suppress alpha below ~20 items, normalise via Spearman-Brown, or lead with SEM in raw-mark units instead.
- Auto-retiring items on statistical flags. A 2025 study of 14,238 items found static thresholds miss 14.3% of genuinely flawed items while also flagging items that are legitimately hard — automatic retirement would both delete good hard questions and leave broken ones live. Flags must open a review task assigned to a subject lead, with a recorded disposition. Equally, having no RESCORE capability at all is its own failure: when you do drop a bad item mid-series, you must be able to recompute affected scores and ranks, and retrofitting that into a leaderboard built on denormalised Redis totals is very painful.
- Adopting Surya/Marker weights without reading the licence. Code is Apache 2.0 but weights are a modified AI Pubs Open Rail-M free only for research, personal use, and startups under $5M funding/revenue. An ed-tech company that raises a Series A silently becomes non-compliant across its entire ingestion pipeline. Decide early: pay Mathpix ($0.005/page — negligible at any realistic volume), buy the Datalab commercial licence, or restrict yourself to MIT-licensed pix2tex (which reports only 0.60 token accuracy and handles single formulas, not pages).
- Randomising question CONTENT per student on a ranked mock. Different students receiving different items destroys rank comparability unless you implement parallel-form assembly with statistical matching and score equating — a substantial psychometric project. Randomising presentation order is safe; randomising content is not. Teams routinely conflate the two because both are called 'randomisation' in the requirements doc, and the damage only becomes visible when a parent disputes a rank.
- Treating Hindi/regional questions as separate rows in the questions table. The moment the English version is corrected, translations silently diverge, and because the answer key exists in multiple places the translations can end up keyed differently from the parent — which is the single worst failure mode in a bilingual exam product. NTA's own rule (English is final on ambiguity) is the design instruction: keys and marks live only on the parent.
- Shipping a two-role system and retrofitting workflow later. Once 20,000 items exist with created_by pointing at a single shared admin account, you cannot reconstruct who authored or approved anything, which destroys the audit trail exactly when you need it (a disputed key on a high-stakes mock). Separation of duties (approved_by <> created_by) is nearly free to add on day one and effectively impossible to backfill.
- Using Google Document AI or Azure Document Intelligence for question ingestion because they are already in the cloud contract. Neither emits LaTeX; Custom Extraction at $30/1,000 pages is 15x Mathpix's PDF rate for a strictly worse result on mathematics. Reserve them for non-question document flows if at all.
- Building admin analytics as ad-hoc queries against the live transactional tables. At 10k concurrent students the attempt_responses table grows by millions of rows per test, and a subject lead opening a chapter heatmap will lock up the same Postgres instance serving the exam. Precompute into summary tables on a schedule, or push analytics to a read replica / BigQuery on the GCP side.
OPEN QUESTIONS:
- Is NTA a 'Government' body for the purposes of Section 2(k) 'Government work' and Section 17(d) of the Copyright Act 1957? NTA is a registered society under the Societies Registration Act, which likely means its papers are NOT Government work and copyright vests in NTA itself. I could not verify this — the India Code PDF and copyright.gov.in returned 403/404. This materially changes the PYQ risk assessment and needs an Indian IP counsel opinion.
- What did the Delhi High Court Division Bench actually hold in Chancellor Masters of Oxford v Rameshwari Photocopy Services (the DU photocopy case) about whether 'in the course of instruction' extends to a commercial third party acting for an educational institution? I could not retrieve the judgment (Indian Kanoon and SpicyIP fetches failed). This is the single most important precedent for whether a commercial app can lean on Section 52(1)(i) at all.
- NCERT's exact copyright and reproduction policy — both ncert.nic.in/copyright.php and /copyright-policy.php returned 404. NCERT textbooks carry a standard 'no part of this publication may be reproduced' notice, and NCERT content underpins the entire NEET syllabus, so whether NCERT diagrams and passages can be reproduced in solutions is a live question. Unverified.
- Canvas New Quizzes item-bank semantics: specifically whether editing a bank item propagates to already-delivered quizzes or whether the quiz holds a snapshot. My fetch of the Instructure community guide 404'd after redirect and the web-search budget was exhausted. This matters because Canvas is the most likely comparison point for an institutional buyer.
- Mathpix's actual accuracy on Indian coaching-material scans specifically — low-quality photocopies, Devanagari mixed with LaTeX, hand-drawn physics free-body diagrams. Mathpix publishes no accuracy figure at all. This can only be answered by running a 200-page pilot against your own real source material and measuring edits-per-item.
- Minimum sample size for IRT calibration (Rasch/1PL vs 2PL vs 3PL) — conventional guidance is roughly 100–200 for Rasch, 500+ for 2PL and 1000+ for 3PL, but I could not verify this against a citable source (assess.com and Taylor & Francis both returned 403). This determines whether IRT is viable at all at 10k students, or whether classical test theory is the only defensible option for the first year.
- Whether JEE Main's regional-language offering matches NEET's 13. JEE Main has historically offered 13 languages including English, Hindi and regional options, but I verified only the NEET list. Confirm against the current JEE Main information bulletin before scoping translation work.
- How Indian coaching institutes (Allen, Aakash, FIITJEE, PW) actually staff and run their internal item-authoring operations — team sizes, items authored per editor per day, review ratios. No public source found; this is competitive intelligence best obtained by hiring an ex-content-lead from one of them, and it directly determines the realistic throughput assumptions for the editorial workflow.
- Whether QTI 3.0 import/export is a genuine purchase driver for Indian institutional buyers (coaching chains, schools) or a Western-market concern only. This determines whether to align the internal schema to QTI names now (cheap) or ignore the standard entirely.
- Empirically calibrated thresholds for the duplicate-detection triad on Indian PYQ corpora specifically — the Jaccard 0.8 / cosine 0.92 / Hamming values I recommend are reasonable starting points from general literature, not tuned figures. They need calibration on ~500 hand-labelled pairs from the actual bank.

## edge-exam-session (Timed exam session (mock test attempt lifecycle): timer authority, network resilience, resume/session continuity, answer durability, scoring/re-scoring, randomisation determinism, fairness/leaderboard integrity, and in-attempt notes — for a React Native + Expo JEE/NEET app on Supabase + Redis at 10k concurrent attempts)
- [critical/api] EC-TIMER-01: Student backgrounds the app, sets device date/time back 45 min (or forward to skip a cooldown), returns. Android Settings > Date & time > auto-time OFF; iOS Settings > General > Date & Time > Set Automatically OFF. Also covers rooted/jailbroken devices and Android emulators with `adb shell date`. -> FAILS: A client-computed countdown from Date.now() grants unlimited extra time; a client-computed 'deadline passed' check can also be dodged so the attempt never auto-submits. Any client-trusted timestamp on the answer row lets a student backdate submissions to inside the window. -> FIX: Never derive remaining time from wall clock on the client. On attempt creation the server writes an immutable `deadline_at timestamptz` on the attempt row computed as `min(started_at + test.duration, test.window_close_at)` using Postgres `clock_timestamp()` inside the RPC — mirroring Moodle's `get_due_date()` = min(timestart+timelimit, timeclose). Client renders `remaining = deadline_at - server_now_estimate`, where `server_now_estimate = last_server_now + (monotonic_now - monotonic_at_sync)`; monotonic source is elapsedRealtimeNanos on Android / CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW+mach_continuous_time on iOS, never Date.now() (exact API semantics unverified from primary docs — confirm elapsedRealtime resets on reboot but is immune to user clock changes before shipping). Heartbeat edge function every 30s returns `{server_now, deadline_at, seconds_left}`; client hard-snaps to server value whenever |drift| > 2s. Server rejects any answer whose arrival time > deadline_at + grace. Strip all client-supplied timestamps at the API boundary; `answered_at` is set by `default now()` in Postgres. Flag `abs(client_reported_now - server_now) > 120s` into a `clock_anomaly` column for admin review rather than blocking (honest skew exists).
- [high/client] EC-TIMER-02: Honest clock skew: a budget Android device with no NTP sync is 3–8 minutes off UTC. Student's timer and the server's deadline disagree. -> FAILS: Timer shows 04:12 remaining while the server has already closed the attempt, or shows 00:00 while 6 minutes remain — student panics, mass-submits blank, or reports the app 'stole' time. Also breaks JWT validation if the device rejects a valid token as not-yet-valid. -> FIX: Two-clock model: authoritative `deadline_at` on the server, display driven by monotonic elapsed time anchored to the last heartbeat. Compute offset with an NTP-style round-trip: `offset = server_now - (t_send + rtt/2)`, keep an EWMA of the last 5 samples, discard samples with rtt > 2s. Show a subtle 'synced Xs ago' indicator. For JWT, allow the documented clock-skew leeway — RFC 7519 says implementers MAY allow leeway 'usually no more than a few minutes' for exp/nbf — and validate exclusively server-side; the client must never gate on local exp.
- [high/db] EC-TIMER-03: Timezone changes mid-attempt: student travels (Kolkata to Dubai), crosses a network TZ boundary near a border, uses a VPN that changes auto-TZ, or manually switches TZ to game a 'live test opens at 09:00 local' rule. -> FAILS: Any naive `timestamp without time zone` column shifts by hours; UI shows the test as not-yet-open or already-closed; a 'today's daily streak' resets or double-counts; a live test's countdown jumps 3.5 hours. -> FIX: All exam columns are `timestamptz` (stored UTC); DB `timezone = 'UTC'`; the client formats to Asia/Kolkata for display only, and for live/scheduled tests it renders the fixed exam timezone label ('09:00 IST') rather than device-local time. Never send local-time strings to the API. Duration arithmetic uses interval seconds, not calendar dates. Streak/day-boundary logic uses a single declared `exam_timezone = 'Asia/Kolkata'` via `(now() AT TIME ZONE 'Asia/Kolkata')::date`, not device locale.
- [medium/infra] EC-TIMER-04: DST / leap-second edge: India has no DST so IST is stable at UTC+05:30, but NRI/Gulf/US-based aspirants and any server or third-party service running local time do observe DST; separately a leap-second smear or an NTP step is applied to the server fleet mid-window. -> FAILS: A 3-hour attempt spanning a DST 'fall back' computed in local time gains or loses 60 minutes. Under Google's smear, servers on smeared NTP disagree with servers on non-smeared NTP by up to ~0.5s, so two API nodes disagree on whether a submission is late. -> FIX: Never do date arithmetic in a local zone. Additionally, do not compute the deadline as an offset re-derived on each request — persist the absolute `deadline_at` once at attempt start so no later clock policy change can move it. Pin all backend nodes to the same NTP policy (Google's 24-hour linear leap smear from noon-to-noon UTC, ~11.6 ppm slew, diverging up to just under 0.5s from UTC) and never mix smeared and non-smeared sources. Apply a fixed 5s server-side ingress tolerance on the deadline so sub-second fleet disagreement can never decide an attempt.
- [critical/client] EC-TIMER-05: App backgrounded mid-test: student checks a WhatsApp message, pulls the notification shade, opens the calculator, or hits the app switcher. RN AppState goes `inactive` (iOS transition/incoming call/multitasking view) then `background`; Android fires `blur` without changing AppState when the notification drawer is pulled. -> FAILS: JS timers are throttled or frozen while backgrounded; on return the countdown shows the time it had when backgrounded, so the student believes they have more time than they do and the UI jumps by minutes on resume. Unsynced answers sit in memory. -> FIX: Subscribe to `AppState.addEventListener('change')`. On `inactive`/`background`: flush the pending-answer queue immediately, record `background_started_at` (monotonic), and register an iOS `beginBackgroundTask(withName:expirationHandler:)` for the flush — iOS grants roughly 30 seconds and force-terminates the app if `endBackgroundTask` is never called. On return to `active`: force an immediate heartbeat before painting the timer, and animate the correction rather than snapping. Log every background episode to an `attempt_focus_events` table (duration, reason) — this is both the audit trail for disputes and the anti-cheat signal. Never reconstruct elapsed time from JS setInterval ticks across a background episode.
- [critical/client] EC-TIMER-06: OS terminates the app: iOS jetsam kills a memory-heavy RN app rendering 90 LaTeX-laden questions plus full-resolution question images; Android low-memory killer does the same on a 3GB device. No JS lifecycle callback fires reliably. -> FAILS: Everything held only in Redux/Zustand memory is lost — the last 40 seconds of answers, the mark-for-review flags, the note the student typed. On relaunch the app has no idea an attempt was open. -> FIX: Treat memory as a cache and expo-sqlite (WAL journal mode, `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL`) as the source of truth for in-flight attempt state. Every answer/flag mutation is a synchronous local write inside `withExclusiveTransactionAsync` BEFORE the optimistic UI update and before the network call; expo-sqlite persists across app restarts. Keep the attempt id in MMKV so cold start can detect 'attempt in progress' before any network call. Cap memory: virtualise the question list, lazy-load images with an explicit LRU disk cache, unmount off-screen KaTeX renders. Instrument iOS `AppState` `memoryWarning` to pre-emptively flush and drop image caches.
- [critical/client] EC-TIMER-07: Phone reboots, battery hits 0%, or the device thermally shuts down 100 minutes into a 180-minute JEE Advanced mock. Student borrows a charger and relaunches 12 minutes later. -> FAILS: Naive design: attempt state lost entirely → unrecoverable trust event. Second failure: a monotonic clock anchored on boot-time resets to zero on reboot, so an elapsedRealtime-based timer thinks no time has passed and hands the student 12 free minutes. -> FIX: On cold start, read the open attempt from SQLite, then call `resume_attempt(attempt_id)` which returns the server-authoritative `deadline_at` and the full server-side answer set. Merge server answers with local unsynced answers by `(question_id, client_seq)` last-writer-wins on a per-question monotonic counter, never on wall clock. Persist a `boot_id` alongside the monotonic anchor; if `boot_id` changed, discard the monotonic anchor entirely and treat the next heartbeat as the only truth. The 12 lost minutes are real and are NOT refunded automatically — that is the exam contract, stated in the pre-attempt consent screen — but the outage is logged so admin can grant compensatory time (see EC-FAIR-07).
- [medium/client] EC-TIMER-08: Incoming voice call, an alarm, a full-screen intent notification, or an OTP autofill overlay interrupts the attempt. On iOS this drives AppState `inactive`; a call can hold the app inactive for minutes. -> FAILS: Timer visually freezes during `inactive` on iOS, then jumps. Autofill on Android counts as another Activity and moves AppState to `background`, tripping a naive anti-cheat rule that voids the attempt. -> FIX: Treat `inactive` distinctly from `background`: `inactive` alone never triggers anti-cheat and never pauses the server deadline. Anti-cheat counts only sustained `background` episodes > 5s, and the policy is 'log and surface', not 'auto-void'. Keep the deadline running (server-authoritative) but present a non-blocking 'you were away 03:12' banner on return so the time jump is explained rather than mysterious. Use `expo-keep-awake` for the duration of the attempt to prevent screen-lock-induced backgrounding.
- [high/client] EC-TIMER-09: Timer drift accumulated over a 180-minute attempt from `setInterval(fn, 1000)` on a busy JS thread, plus RN bridge/JSI congestion during LaTeX re-renders and image decodes. -> FAILS: setInterval fires late under load; naive `remaining -= 1` per tick accumulates 30–120 seconds of drift over 3 hours. Student sees 00:00 while the server still allows input, or the reverse — and the two students on the same test disagree about their remaining time. -> FIX: Never decrement a counter. Recompute every frame/second as `remaining = deadline_monotonic_anchor - monotonicNow()` so drift cannot accumulate. Recalibrate `deadline_monotonic_anchor` on every 30s heartbeat and on every AppState `active` transition. Run the countdown on the UI thread via Reanimated worklets so a blocked JS thread cannot stall the visible clock. Add a client assertion: if a single tick advances more than 5s of wall time, force an immediate heartbeat.
- [high/api] EC-TIMER-10: Sectioned paper (JEE Main: Physics/Chemistry/Maths; NEET: Physics/Chemistry/Botany/Zoology) where the admin configures per-section time limits and 'no going back to a submitted section', combined with an overall 180-minute cap. -> FAILS: Two independent deadlines drift apart: a student finishes Section A early, banks 12 minutes, and a client-only implementation lets them spend it in Section B when policy forbids it — or the overall deadline fires while a section timer still shows time, producing a mid-section forced submit with no explanation. -> FIX: Model deadlines as a set of rows: `attempt_section(attempt_id, section_id, opened_at, section_deadline_at, locked_at, sequence)`. The effective hard stop is `least(section_deadline_at, attempt.deadline_at)`; the UI must always display which constraint is binding. Section transition is a server RPC that atomically stamps `locked_at` on the outgoing section and `opened_at`/`section_deadline_at` on the incoming one, inside one transaction with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the attempt row. Time banking is an explicit boolean on the test config (`carry_forward_unused_section_time`), default false, enforced server-side.
- [critical/api] EC-NET-01: Student taps 'Start Test' with no connectivity (tier-2/tier-3 India, hostel dead zone, exhausted daily data cap). The app has cached question content from a prior prefetch. -> FAILS: If the app starts a local attempt with a locally-invented start time, the server later has no authoritative `deadline_at` and the student can restart the app repeatedly to get fresh timers, or genuinely completes 3 hours of work the server refuses to accept. -> FIX: Hard rule: an attempt cannot begin without a server-issued attempt row. Gate the Start button on a successful `start_attempt` RPC that returns `{attempt_id, deadline_at, server_now, question_manifest, shuffle_seed}` — one round trip, sub-2KB. Offer explicit 'Download test for offline attempt' as a separate pre-authorised flow: the server pre-issues an attempt row with `deadline_at = null` and `offline_token_expires_at`, and the deadline starts on the first heartbeat after going online, with total offline elapsed time capped at the paper duration. Practice mode (untimed, unranked) is fully offline-capable; ranked mock tests are not.
- [critical/client] EC-NET-02: Connectivity drops at minute 40 and does not return for 25 minutes. Student keeps answering. -> FAILS: Every answer PATCH fails; a naive implementation shows 25 error toasts, or silently discards answers, or blocks the UI. On reconnect an unordered burst overwrites newer answers with older ones. -> FIX: Offline-first write path: each answer mutation writes to a local `pending_ops` table in expo-sqlite with `(attempt_id, question_id, payload, client_seq monotonic int, idempotency_key uuid4, created_at)`. TanStack Query with `onlineManager` + persisted paused mutations drains the queue on reconnect in FIFO order; because a paused mutation's function is not serialised, you MUST register `queryClient.setMutationDefaults(['saveAnswer'], {mutationFn, retry})` before hydration or `resumePausedMutations()` fails with 'No mutationFn found'. UI shows a single persistent 'N answers pending sync' chip, never per-answer errors. Server upsert is `ON CONFLICT (attempt_id, question_id) DO UPDATE ... WHERE excluded.client_seq > answers.client_seq` so out-of-order arrivals are dropped, not applied.
- [high/client] EC-NET-03: Flapping connectivity: a train/metro commute or a congested tower toggles online/offline every 10–30 seconds. NetInfo reports `isConnected: true` while `isInternetReachable` is false or null. -> FAILS: The sync queue restarts mid-drain repeatedly, re-sending the same mutation and multiplying server load; heartbeats fire in bursts; the 'pending' chip flickers; battery burns. At 10k students a flap storm becomes a self-inflicted DDoS. -> FIX: Gate all network work on `isInternetReachable === true`, not `isConnected`. Single-flight the drain with a local mutex so only one drain loop runs. Exponential backoff with full jitter: `delay = random(0, min(30s, base * 2^attempt))`, base 500ms, and honour `Retry-After` on 429/503. Debounce online transitions by 1500ms before resuming. Coalesce answer writes: batch up to 50 pending ops into one `sync_answers` RPC call rather than one request per answer. Heartbeat and sync ride the same request so a flapping client makes at most one request per interval.
- [critical/api] EC-NET-04: Slow 2G/EDGE: 300–1500ms RTT, frequent 30s+ stalls. The final 'Submit Test' request is issued at T-20s and the response has not returned when the timer hits zero. -> FAILS: Student sees a spinner past 00:00, force-closes the app assuming it hung, and the submission is in an unknown state. Or the client times out and retries, creating a second submission. -> FIX: Submission is a two-phase, idempotent operation. Phase 1: the client has already streamed every answer, so 'submit' carries no answer payload — only `{attempt_id, idempotency_key}`. Phase 2: `finalize_attempt` is idempotent on `attempt.status`; a second call with the same key returns the same result (Stripe's model: keys retained 24h, reusing a key with different parameters errors out; generate v4 UUIDs, ≤255 chars). Client request timeout 20s with 3 idempotent retries. Critically, submission is not required for correctness: a server-side sweeper finalises the attempt at `deadline_at` regardless (see EC-NET-08), so a failed submit degrades to auto-submit, never to data loss. Show 'Your answers are saved on our servers. Submitting…' copy so the student knows closing the app is safe.
- [critical/api] EC-NET-05: Duplicate submission: user double-taps Submit; or the client retries a request whose 200 response was lost; or two devices both submit (see EC-SESSION-02). -> FAILS: Two `attempt_result` rows, double coin/streak award, two leaderboard entries for one student, scoring job runs twice and doubles the score. -> FIX: (a) `finalize_attempt` takes an `idempotency_key`; an `idempotency_keys(key pk, attempt_id, request_hash, response jsonb, created_at)` table with a 24h TTL returns the cached response on replay and 409s on the same key with a different request hash. (b) State-machine guard inside a transaction: `UPDATE attempts SET status='submitted' WHERE id=$1 AND status='in_progress' RETURNING *` — zero rows means already submitted, return the existing result rather than erroring. (c) `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_result_per_attempt ON attempt_results(attempt_id)`. (d) Rewards are granted by a separate transactional outbox keyed on `attempt_id`, with `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX ON coin_ledger(attempt_id, reason)` so double-award is structurally impossible.
- [critical/api] EC-NET-06: Out-of-order answer sync: student answers Q17 as option B, changes to option D, goes offline, changes to option A. Reconnect fires three queued writes; HTTP/2 multiplexing plus retry backoff delivers them B, A, D. -> FAILS: The stored answer is D — a value the student explicitly abandoned. Silent, undetectable, and directly costs marks. -> FIX: Per-attempt monotonic `client_seq` (a counter persisted in SQLite, incremented in the same transaction as the local write — never a timestamp, since device clocks are hostile). Server upsert applies only if `excluded.client_seq > answers.client_seq`; stale writes are accepted with HTTP 200 and a `{applied:false, reason:'stale_seq'}` body so the client stops retrying. Drain the queue strictly serially per attempt (concurrency 1) as a second line of defence — TanStack's paused mutations already preserve order. Keep an append-only `answer_events` audit table (attempt_id, question_id, value, client_seq, received_at) so any dispute can be reconstructed.
- [critical/api] EC-NET-07: Buffered answers arrive after the server-side deadline: student was offline from T-6min, answered 4 more questions, reconnects at T+90s and the queue drains. -> FAILS: Naive strict rejection loses 4 legitimately-answered questions and enrages the student. Naive acceptance lets anyone go offline at T-1s, answer everything at leisure, and sync an hour later — a total integrity break. -> FIX: Adopt Moodle's model explicitly, with a configurable `overdue_handling` per test taking Moodle's three values: `autosubmit` (default in Moodle: 'Open attempts are submitted automatically'), `graceperiod` ('There is a grace period when open attempts can be submitted, but no more questions answered'), `autoabandon` ('Attempts must be submitted before time expires, or they are not counted'). Accept a late write ONLY if the client-attested `answered_at_monotonic_offset` places it before `deadline_at` AND the write arrives within an ingress grace of 120s (Moodle's `graceperiodmin` default is 60s). Beyond that, store into `late_answers` with `applied=false` and surface it to the student and to admin — visible, appealable, not scored. Cap total accepted-late answers per attempt (e.g. 10) to bound abuse, and rate-limit accounts with repeated offline-at-deadline patterns.
- [critical/db] EC-NET-08: The timer reaches zero while the device is offline, or the app was killed 40 minutes before the deadline and never relaunched. Nobody ever calls submit. -> FAILS: The attempt sits `in_progress` forever: it never appears in results, blocks the 'one active attempt' rule so the student cannot start another test, never enters the leaderboard, and pollutes analytics. -> FIX: A server-side sweeper is the only reliable finaliser — mirror Moodle's `\mod_quiz\task\update_overdue_attempts` cron, which selects attempts `WHERE state IN ('inprogress','overdue') AND timecheckstate <= :processto` with `processto = timenow - graceperiodmin`. Implement with Supabase Cron (pg_cron; supports schedules down to every second, but keep ≤8 concurrent jobs and each job <10 min per Supabase's guidance) running every 30s: `SELECT id FROM attempts WHERE status='in_progress' AND deadline_at < now() - interval '120 seconds' ORDER BY deadline_at LIMIT 500 FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, then enqueue each into pgmq/Supabase Queues for scoring. `SKIP LOCKED` makes the sweeper safely horizontally scalable. Keep a `timecheck_at` column so the sweep query is index-driven, not a full scan of a table with millions of attempts.
- [critical/infra] EC-NET-09: Thundering herd: a scheduled live mock for 10,000 students starts at 14:00 IST and ends at 17:00 IST. Nearly all clients submit within a 60-second window at the end, and all fetch the paper within a 90-second window at the start. -> FAILS: Connection exhaustion and 5xx storms exactly when submissions are most valuable. Supabase compute tiers cap direct Postgres connections (Large = 160 direct / 800 pooler clients; even 16XL = 500 direct / 12,000 pooler clients), and Realtime concurrent connections cap at 200 on Free, 500 on Pro with spend cap, 10,000 on Pro-no-spend-cap/Team. 10k live clients on Realtime is at the ceiling of the Team plan. -> FIX: (a) Never open a direct Postgres connection per client — all traffic goes through PostgREST/Edge Functions using Supavisor transaction mode (port 6543), which is documented as the right choice for 'serverless or edge functions, which require many transient connections'. (b) Because answers are streamed continuously, the end-of-test spike carries almost no payload; make `finalize_attempt` an O(1) status flip and push scoring to pgmq consumers. (c) Jitter the sweeper and the client's final auto-submit by `random(0, 45s)`. (d) Do NOT use Realtime for the timer — use HTTP heartbeats, which are cacheable and stateless; reserve Realtime for the live leaderboard and cap it via a single broadcast channel with server-side fan-out (broadcast payload cap 256KB Free / 3000KB paid; ≤100 channels per connection; 2,500 msgs/sec on Team). (e) Pre-warm: prefetch the question manifest and all images to device 30 minutes before start via a signed manifest, so 14:00 is not also an image-CDN spike.
- [medium/client] EC-NET-10: Captive portal or ISP interception (hostel wifi, railway wifi, some Indian ISPs' DNS hijack pages) returns HTTP 200 with an HTML login page in response to an API call. -> FAILS: The client parses HTML as JSON, throws, and either shows a nonsense error or — worse — treats the mutation as failed and retries forever while the student believes answers are saving. `NetInfo.isConnected` is true, so offline UI never appears. -> FIX: Validate `Content-Type: application/json` and a signed response envelope on every API response; anything else is classified as `CAPTIVE_PORTAL`, not `SERVER_ERROR`. Use `NetInfo.isInternetReachable` (which does a real reachability probe) rather than `isConnected`. On detection, show a distinct 'Your network requires sign-in — your answers are saved on this device' state and keep queueing locally. Pin the API to HTTPS with certificate validation so an intercepting proxy cannot silently MITM answer traffic.
- [high/api] EC-SESSION-01: Student starts on an Android phone, the phone dies, and they resume on a sibling's iPhone (or a tablet) 15 minutes later. -> FAILS: Wrong behaviours: (a) app creates a brand new attempt with a fresh 3-hour timer; (b) app shows a blank paper because answers were only ever in local SQLite; (c) the question order differs on device 2, so the student's mental map of 'Q34 was the tough integral' breaks and mark-for-review flags point at wrong questions. -> FIX: Attempt state lives server-side and is device-agnostic. On login, `GET /attempts?status=in_progress` returns the open attempt; the app routes straight into it. `resume_attempt` returns the full server answer set plus `shuffle_seed` and `question_order[]` — the order is persisted as an ordered array on the attempt row at creation, so it is identical everywhere (see EC-RAND-01). `deadline_at` is unchanged: the 15 minutes are lost, and that is stated up front. Locally-queued answers from device 1 are gone unless they synced — which is why answers flush every 10s and on every AppState transition, matching Moodle's `quiz/autosaveperiod` default of 60 seconds but tighter for a mobile network.
- [critical/api] EC-SESSION-02: Two devices in the same attempt simultaneously — either innocent (student opens the app on a tablet forgetting the phone is mid-test) or malicious (a friend on a second device answers half the paper in parallel). -> FAILS: Two clients race on the same `(attempt_id, question_id)`; last-writer-wins by seq means device B's counter is independent of device A's and can permanently mask A's newer answers. Two auto-submits fire. Collusion is undetectable. -> FIX: Issue a `session_token uuid` on `start_attempt`/`resume_attempt` and store `attempts.active_session_token` plus `active_device_id`. Every answer/heartbeat request carries the token; the server rejects with 409 `SESSION_SUPERSEDED` if it does not match, and the losing client immediately locks into a read-only 'This test was resumed on another device' screen. Resuming from a new device is an explicit, confirmed takeover that rotates the token — that makes takeover single-writer by construction. Change `client_seq` to a server-assigned `LWW` using a per-attempt Postgres sequence bumped inside the upsert so counters cannot collide across devices. Record every takeover in `attempt_device_events` and surface `device_switch_count` on the admin analytics view as an integrity signal. Optionally use Realtime Presence on a per-attempt channel to detect concurrency in real time (note the documented limit of 5 presence calls per client per 30s, and that presence sync can emit spurious join/leave during reconciliation).
- [critical/api] EC-SESSION-03: JWT expires mid-attempt. Supabase's default access token lifetime is 1 hour, so a 180-minute JEE Advanced mock spans at least two refreshes. A refresh fires while the device is offline or on 2G. -> FAILS: Every answer write starts returning 401. A naive client redirects to the login screen and destroys the in-memory attempt; or the queued mutations all fail permanently and drain into the void; or the student is asked to type a password at minute 95 of an exam. -> FIX: (a) Enable `autoRefreshToken` and pre-emptively refresh at 50% of TTL (30 min), not at expiry, and always immediately before the final submit. (b) Pause auto-refresh while AppState is `background` and force a refresh on return to `active` — Supabase refresh tokens are single-use with a documented 10-second reuse interval, so overlapping refreshes from a resumed queue can revoke the session. (c) Never navigate away from an active attempt on 401: hold the UI, retry refresh with backoff, keep writing to local SQLite. (d) Make the answer-sync endpoint tolerate a short 401 window by queueing rather than discarding. (e) As a break-glass path, `start_attempt` also returns a narrow-scope `attempt_write_token` (JWT, aud=`attempt:write`, exp = `deadline_at + 15min`, claims limited to that one attempt_id) that the answer-sync Edge Function accepts independently of the user session — so even a fully broken auth session cannot cost a student their paper. Validate this token in the Edge Function and let it bypass RLS via a `security definer` function scoped to the single attempt.
- [high/api] EC-SESSION-04: Student tries to resume after the test window has closed — e.g. a weekend-long window test where the paper's `window_close_at` passes while their attempt is still `in_progress`, or they open the app three days later. -> FAILS: Either the app lets them continue (integrity break: unlimited time) or it shows a raw error and the attempt stays `in_progress` forever, blocking new attempts. -> FIX: The effective deadline is already `min(started_at + duration, test.window_close_at)` at attempt creation, so window close is enforced by the same mechanism. The sweeper (EC-NET-08) will have finalised the attempt long before the student returns; on resume the app finds `status='submitted'` and routes to the result screen with an explanatory banner ('Auto-submitted at 21:00 on 14 Aug — window closed'). Distinguish `submitted` vs `abandoned` (Moodle's `stateabandoned` = 'Never submitted') so an attempt with zero answers is reported as not-counted rather than as a 0% score that tanks the student's visible average.
- [high/api] EC-SESSION-05: Refresh-token rotation race: two threads (the sync drain and the heartbeat) both hit an expired access token and both POST the same refresh token; or the app is restored from an iOS backup carrying a stale refresh token; or the project has 'single session per user' enabled and the student logs in on a second device. -> FAILS: Supabase treats reuse outside the 10s interval as compromise — 'the whole session is regarded as terminated and all refresh tokens belonging to it are marked as revoked'. The student is hard-logged-out mid-exam. -> FIX: Single-flight the refresh: one shared in-flight promise guarded by a mutex; all callers await it (supabase-js does this internally but a custom fetch wrapper can defeat it — do not roll your own). Do not enable 'single session per user' for student accounts during exam windows, or exempt attempt-scoped tokens from it. Persist the session in `expo-secure-store`, and clear it on restore-from-backup by keying the store entry to an install-scoped UUID. Combine with the attempt-scoped write token (EC-SESSION-03) so a revoked session degrades to 'you must log in again after submitting' rather than losing the paper.
- [medium/client] EC-SESSION-06: An OTA update (expo-updates / EAS Update) downloads and the app reloads mid-attempt; or a store update installs; or the student force-quits during a JS bundle reload. -> FAILS: The JS context is torn down and rebuilt; in-memory attempt state and any un-flushed answers are lost; the new bundle may have a different local DB schema and fail to read the pending queue. -> FIX: Set `checkAutomatically: 'ON_ERROR_RECOVERY'` or gate `Updates.reloadAsync()` behind an explicit 'no attempt in progress' guard — never reload while `attempt.status === 'in_progress'`. Version the local SQLite schema and run migrations forward-only on cold start before reading `pending_ops`; never drop the pending table on migration. Keep `pending_ops.payload` as opaque JSON so schema evolution cannot orphan queued writes. Ship a server-side `min_supported_client_version` on the attempt manifest; if the client is below it, block starting a NEW attempt but always allow resuming/submitting an existing one.
- [critical/api] EC-DATA-01: Partial answer save: a batched `sync_answers` call carrying 40 answers is cut off mid-write, or an Edge Function hits the documented 2-second CPU limit (256MB memory, 150s free / 400s paid wall clock, 150s idle timeout → 504) while writing. -> FAILS: 22 of 40 answers land, 18 do not; the client marks the whole batch synced on a partial 2xx and permanently loses 18 answers, or marks the whole batch unsynced and re-sends 22 already-applied writes. -> FIX: Make the batch write atomic in a single Postgres transaction inside a `security definer` RPC (`sync_answers(attempt_id, ops jsonb[])` doing one `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE ... FROM jsonb_to_recordset`) so it is all-or-nothing. Return a per-op result array `[{client_seq, applied, reason}]`; the client only clears local ops whose `client_seq` is acknowledged. Cap batch size at 50 ops and keep the RPC well inside the 2s CPU ceiling by avoiding per-row round trips. On 504/timeout, retry with the same idempotency key — the upsert is naturally idempotent under the `client_seq` guard.
- [critical/infra] EC-DATA-02: Answers are saved but the scoring job crashes: an OOM in a scoring worker, a deploy mid-batch, a null-pointer on a question whose answer key is missing, or a poison message that fails every retry. -> FAILS: The attempt is `submitted` with no `attempt_result` row. The student sees an infinite 'Calculating your score…' spinner. If scoring is fire-and-forget from the submit handler, the work is simply gone. -> FIX: Decouple submission from scoring. `finalize_attempt` only flips status and enqueues a pgmq message (Supabase Queues: 'delivered exactly once to a consumer within a customizable visibility window'; messages are durable Postgres rows). Set visibility timeout to ~3× p99 scoring time. Workers `archive()` on success rather than delete, giving an audit trail. Track `read_ct` and route messages exceeding 5 reads to a `scoring_dlq` with an admin alert. Scoring must be a pure, idempotent function of `(attempt_id, answer_key_version)` writing through `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (attempt_id, answer_key_version) DO UPDATE`, so replay is always safe. A pg_cron reconciler every 5 minutes finds `attempts WHERE status='submitted' AND NOT EXISTS (result) AND submitted_at < now()-interval '3 minutes'` and re-enqueues. UI shows 'Score being computed' with an ETA, never a bare spinner.
- [critical/product] EC-DATA-03: Answer key correction after results are published — the single most consequential fairness event. Real precedent: NTA revised the NEET (UG) 2024 answer key and issued re-revised scorecards/ranks on 26 July 2024 following the Supreme Court order dated 23 July 2024 in W.P.(Civil) No. 335/2024, for a cohort of 23,33,297 candidates who appeared. -> FAILS: Naive fix: UPDATE the key row in place. Every existing score is now silently wrong, historical analytics are corrupted, leaderboards are inconsistent with stored scores, coins/rewards already paid out on the old ranking are unrecoverable, and there is no way to explain to a student why their score changed. -> FIX: Answer keys are immutable, versioned rows: `answer_keys(question_id, version int, correct_option[], marking_scheme jsonb, effective_from, authored_by, reason text)`, unique on `(question_id, version)`. `attempt_results` stores `answer_key_version_map jsonb` (or a single `key_snapshot_id`) so every score is reproducible. A correction creates version N+1 and enqueues a `rescore_test(test_id, from_version, to_version)` job that recomputes every affected attempt into a NEW `attempt_results` row (never overwriting), then atomically swaps the published result pointer and recomputes ranks in one transaction. Emit a `score_revision` record per affected student and push a notification with a before/after breakdown. Rewards use a compensating-entry ledger: never claw back coins, only top up — clawback is a trust event worse than the original error.
- [critical/db] EC-DATA-04: Admin edits or deletes a question after attempts exist — fixes a typo in the stem, replaces a blurry image, changes an option, or deletes the question entirely mid-window while 400 students are attempting it. -> FAILS: Students who attempted before and after the edit answered materially different questions but are ranked together. If the question row is hard-deleted, every referencing `answers` row FK-cascades away and attempts silently lose questions. If an option's text changes but the option id is reused, stored answers now point at different content. -> FIX: Follow Moodle 4.x's question-versioning model: separate the stable identity from the content. `questions(id)` is the bank entry; `question_versions(id, question_id, version, status ready|draft|hidden, content, options jsonb)` holds immutable content; a test references either a pinned `question_version_id` or 'always latest'. Ranked mock tests MUST pin versions at test-publish time; only practice pools may float. `answers` FK to `question_version_id` and store the chosen `option_id` (a stable uuid per option, never an index). Never hard-delete: `status='hidden'` plus `ON DELETE RESTRICT` where an attempt exists. Editing a question that has attempts creates a new version and requires an explicit admin decision: 'apply to future attempts only' (default) or 'void for everyone and re-score' (EC-FAIR-02). Show the admin an attempt-count warning before any edit.
- [critical/infra] EC-DATA-05: A question image fails to load: the Supabase Storage signed URL expired mid-attempt (a 1-hour `createSignedUrl` TTL inside a 3-hour exam), a CDN miss on 2G times out, or the device is offline and the image was never prefetched. Many JEE/NEET questions are image-only (circuit diagrams, ray optics, structures) — an unloaded image makes the question literally unanswerable. -> FAILS: Blank box. The student cannot answer, loses marks, and correctly claims the platform's fault. At 10k concurrent this can hit a whole cohort if a bucket or transform pipeline degrades. -> FIX: (a) Serve question images from a PUBLIC bucket (documented 'high CDN cache HIT ratio') with unguessable path UUIDs, so there is no expiry to get wrong; reserve signed URLs for solutions/answer keys. If signed URLs are required, mint them with `expires_in = deadline_at - now() + 30min` at `start_attempt`, and refresh the manifest on heartbeat. Note Storage signed URLs are signed with a dedicated internal key separate from the Auth JWT signing key, so they survive auth key rotation. (b) Mandatory prefetch: `start_attempt` returns a manifest of every asset with sha256 + byte size; the client downloads all of them to disk before the timer starts (show a determinate progress bar), and the timer does not start until prefetch completes or the student explicitly opts into 'start anyway'. (c) Per-image retry with backoff plus a 'Report image not loading' button that writes an `attempt_incident` row. (d) Server-side rule: any question with ≥N image-failure incidents in a live test is auto-flagged for admin void review. (e) Store an `alt_text`/LaTeX fallback rendering for every image at authoring time.
- [high/client] EC-DATA-06: A question renders broken LaTeX: the admin pasted `\cline`/`\multicolumn` (unsupported in KaTeX), a Word-smart-quote mangled a macro, an unescaped `$` split the expression, or a `\newcommand` defined in one question leaks into another. -> FAILS: KaTeX in default `strict:'warn'` mode renders unsupported command names in red (configurable via `errorColor`) — the student sees red garbage instead of the integrand and cannot answer. With `throwOnError:true` the whole question screen white-screens and the attempt appears crashed. -> FIX: Validate at authoring time, not render time: the admin question editor runs `katex.renderToString(src, {throwOnError:true, strict:true})` server-side in an Edge Function on save and BLOCKS publishing on failure, storing a `latex_valid boolean` + `latex_errors jsonb` on the question version. A pre-publish CI job re-validates every question in a test paper and refuses to publish a test containing an invalid render. At runtime use `throwOnError:false` with a React error boundary per question so one bad question can never kill the attempt; on fallback, show the raw source in a monospace block plus a prominent 'Report this question' action, and auto-create an `attempt_incident`. Disable `\def/\gdef` macro persistence between questions (fresh macro scope per render) to prevent cross-question leakage. Track `render_failure` telemetry per question_version_id so a systemic failure is caught in minutes.
- [high/api] EC-DATA-07: Numerical-value questions (JEE Main Section B integer answers, JEE Advanced numeric with decimals): a student enters '2.50' vs '2.5', '−3' with a Unicode minus, '1/2', '2.0e0', trailing whitespace, or a value inside a stated tolerance band. -> FAILS: Exact string comparison marks a correct answer wrong. Locale-specific numeric keyboards on Hindi/regional device locales emit Devanagari digits or a comma decimal separator, which parse to NaN and score 0. -> FIX: Store the student's raw input verbatim AND a normalised canonical form. Normalisation: NFKC Unicode normalise, map U+2212/U+2013 to ASCII '-', strip whitespace and thousands separators, parse as decimal (not float) with a fixed-point library. Marking scheme is data, not code: `{type:'numeric', value:2.5, tolerance_abs:0.01}` or `{type:'numeric', value:2.5, decimals:2, rounding:'truncate'}` — JEE Advanced historically specifies truncation to 2 decimals, so encode it explicitly per question. Force the numeric keypad to ASCII digits regardless of device locale. Unit-test the comparator against a fixture set of pathological inputs; run it as a CI gate.
- [high/api] EC-DATA-08: Marking schemes differ per exam and per section: JEE Main MCQ +4/−1, Section B numeric +4/0 (no negative), JEE Advanced partial marking (+4 full, +3/+2/+1 partial, −2 wrong), NEET +4/−1. An admin cross-tags a question into both a JEE Main and a NEET paper. -> FAILS: A single global scoring constant produces wrong scores for one exam family. Partial-marking multi-correct questions scored as all-or-nothing systematically under-scores every JEE Advanced attempt. Because scores drive ranks, coins and streaks, the error propagates through the whole rewards layer. -> FIX: Marking scheme belongs to the `(test_section, question)` join, not to the question and not to global config: `test_questions(test_id, section_id, question_version_id, marks_correct, marks_incorrect, marks_unattempted, partial_scheme jsonb, display_order)`. The scoring function is a pure Postgres function taking the row, so it is testable and reproducible. Snapshot the entire `test_questions` config into the attempt result as `scoring_config_hash` so a later config change cannot silently alter historical scores. Add a publish-time validator: every question in a section must have a scheme, and multi-correct questions must declare an explicit partial policy or publishing is blocked.
- [critical/api] EC-DATA-09: Answers mapped by position rather than identity: the client sends `{index: 17, option: 2}` because the UI works off the shuffled array, and the server resolves index 17 against the unshuffled canonical order (or against a re-shuffled order after a resume). -> FAILS: Every answer is recorded against the wrong question. Catastrophic, silent, and it looks like the student simply performed badly — it can go undetected for months. -> FIX: Never transmit positional indices. The wire format is `{question_version_id: uuid, option_id: uuid}` — both stable uuids minted at authoring time. The shuffled order exists only as a rendering concern derived from the persisted `question_order uuid[]` on the attempt row. Add a server-side assertion that every submitted `question_version_id` is a member of that attempt's `question_order`, rejecting with 422 otherwise — this alone catches the entire class of bug. Add a contract test that scores a shuffled attempt and an unshuffled attempt with identical answers and asserts equal scores.
- [critical/db] EC-RAND-01: Per-student randomised question order (and option order) must be identical on every resume, on every device, and after a reinstall — for the whole 3 hours and for the results review afterwards. -> FAILS: If the shuffle is computed client-side from Math.random() or from `Date.now()`, a reinstall or a second device produces a different paper. Mark-for-review flags and the student's mental map break; worse, a student could reshuffle repeatedly to surface an easier subset if the pool is larger than the paper. -> FIX: Materialise the order once, server-side, at `start_attempt`, and persist it: `attempts.question_order uuid[]` and `attempts.option_order jsonb` (map question_version_id → ordered option_id[]). Generate it with a seeded Fisher–Yates using `seed = hmac_sha256(server_secret, attempt_id)` — attempt-scoped, not user-scoped, so it is deterministic, reproducible for audit, and unpredictable to the client (the raw seed is never sent; only the resulting order is). Store the seed for reproducibility. Redis may cache the order for read throughput but is never the source of truth — Redis eviction or a flush must not be able to change a student's paper; always read-through to Postgres on miss.
- [high/product] EC-RAND-02: Option shuffling applied blindly to questions whose options are order-dependent: 'All of the above', 'Both (A) and (C)', 'None of these', assertion-reason pairs, matching/match-the-column questions with List-I/List-II, and sequencing questions ('arrange in increasing order'). -> FAILS: 'All of the above' moves to position B and now refers to nothing coherent; 'Both (A) and (C)' names options that no longer exist at those labels. The question becomes unanswerable or has a different correct answer than the key. This is the classic silent shuffle bug and it directly costs marks. -> FIX: Make shufflability an explicit, authored property, defaulting to OFF: `question_versions.shuffle_options boolean default false` plus `option_group` for options that must move together and `pinned_position smallint` for options that must stay last (None/All of the above). Add an automated authoring-time linter that scans option text for `/all of the (above|these)|none of (the above|these)|both \(?[a-d]\)?|only \(?[a-d]\)?|and \(?[a-d]\)?/i` and forces `shuffle_options=false` with an admin warning. Question types `matching`, `assertion_reason`, `sequencing`, `comprehension` are hard-coded non-shufflable. Because the key is stored as `option_id` (not a letter), a correct shuffle can never break scoring — the risk is purely semantic, which is why the linter is the control.
- [high/product] EC-RAND-03: Fair ranking under randomisation: students receive the same 90 questions in different orders (fine), OR different questions drawn from a larger pool (not fine for a shared leaderboard). Order effects are real — students fatigue and run out of time, so whoever gets the hard mechanics question at position 3 vs position 88 is not equally advantaged. -> FAILS: A single leaderboard silently compares non-equivalent papers.
